# Research: agent fleets, graphics agents, evaluator loops, realism metrics, Claude Code features

Raw output of the `fleet-research` workflow (2026-10-18, cloud machine, read and web search only). Five research agents, each followed by a fact-checking agent that opened the sources. Full JSON: `report.json`; every agent's answer: `agents.jsonl`.

## multi-agent-evidence

### Parallel sub-agents in coding agents (arXiv 2610.10263, Oct 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10263 (Han Li, HanHaoNing Li, Ziqian Jiang, Yiling Lou, Oct 2026, arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** First systematic study of native dynamic concurrency (main agent decides at run time whether to spawn parallel sub-agents) in Codex, Claude Code and Kimi Code, with concurrency switched on vs off. Concurrency helps most on long-horizon tasks; on bounded tasks it often gives little or negative improvement. Authors give a failure taxonomy of concurrent runs.
- **Numbers:** 354 tasks from 4 long-horizon benchmarks + 1 bounded benchmark; 2,124 executions; cost over $20K; token use 1.41-3.31x that of sequential runs; failure taxonomy = 4 main categories, 13 subcategories, 28 patterns. UNVERIFIED third-party auto-generated GitHub critique (jjakimoto/research-issues #2072): Kimi Code 59.0 vs 77.0 task pass on a 100-task SWE-bench Verified sample (Holm-McNemar p=0.0132); Codex 71.0 vs 71.0 at about 2.5x tokens; each cell is a single run and the treatment flag also changes the agent's instructions.
- **For the fleet:** Default to sequential execution for short, bounded probe-edit tasks; only allow fan-out when a task is long-horizon or genuinely independent. Budget 1.4-3.3x tokens for any concurrency and measure whether pass rate actually rises.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** arXiv itself unreachable (DNS). Existence and numbers are corroborated only by two auto-generated third-party GitHub notes (jjakimoto/research-issues #2055 and #2072, both say 'verify before citing'). Real title: 'When Sub-Agents Work in Parallel: The Promises and Pitfalls of Dynamic Concurrency in Long-Horizon Coding Tasks'; the report's title is a made-up label. Authors (Han Li et al.) not confirmed by any source I could open. #2055 matches 354 tasks (SWE-bench Verified 100 + RepoZero 150 + NL2Repo 26 + ProgramBench 50 + LoopsBench 28), 2,124 executions, $20,780.02, tokens 1.41-3.31x, taxonomy 4/13/28, effect -11.9 to +2.3 pp, one run per cell. #2072 matches Kimi Code 59.0 vs 77.0, Codex 71.0 vs 71.0 at 3.37M vs 1.34M tokens (2.5x), Holm-McNemar p=0.0132. Omitted by the report: Claude Code has the strongest penalty (p=5.08e-7; #2055 says 83.0 -> 59.0 on SWE-bench Verified) but rises 7.1 -> 21.4 on LoopsBench. Unresolved: both Kimi (#2072) and Claude Code (#2055) are given as 59.0 with concurrency on SWE-bench Verified; one note may be wrong. Agents were Codex (GPT-5.4), Claude Code (Opus 5), Kimi Code (Kimi K3).

### Anthropic Scion agent-scale source control (Oct 2026)

- **Source:** https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/redesigning-source-control (Anthropic Engineering, 2026-10-13, Anthropic engineering blog)
- **Claim:** Anthropic says agents create far more concurrent changes than people, so human-scale tooling (global merge-queue lock, offline-first git mirrors) became the bottleneck. They avoid shared locks, accept that concurrent changes can conflict, and say Claude usually fixes conflicts faster than humans can detect them; they invest in post-merge and pre-deploy testing instead of a global merge lock.
- **Numbers:** about 2,500 changes merged per day in May 2026 and over 30,000 per day by September; main history almost 100 GB; 25 million CI checkouts per day; 200x smaller CPU/memory footprint than the old mirror setup; team of seven, five-month migration.
- **For the fleet:** At our scale (a handful of agents on one small repo) conflicts are cheap if every merge is followed by a re-render and regression check; the lesson is to rely on post-merge verification, not elaborate locking, while still serializing merges to main.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** Opened https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/redesigning-source-control. Title 'Redesigning source control at Anthropic', Oct 13 2026 (updated Oct 15), by Nick G. and the source control team. All numbers verified: ~2,500 merges/day in May 2026, over 30,000/day by September, main history almost 100 GB, ~25M CI checkouts/day, 200x smaller footprint, team of seven, five months. 'Claude can typically fix these conflicts faster than humans can even identify them' is there. Mischaracterised: the article says the bottleneck was source control, builds and CI (git's offline-first full-clone model and mirrors), and that 'We haven't been able to effectively replicate the industry standard of a global repository lock on the merge queue'. It does not say a global lock was the bottleneck, and it never says to serialize merges to main. The report's 'while still serializing merges to main' is its own addition and goes against the article's lock-free design.

### Teams of coding agents inference-time compute (arXiv 2610.17471, Oct 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17471 (Keenan Samway, Nouha Dziri and four co-authors (Cohere-affiliated; Cohere blog 'Scaling agent teams: reach the frontier at 30% of the cost'), 2026-10-12, arXiv preprint (cs.MA))
- **Claim:** Treats team design as compute allocation. On ProgramBench, teams beat solo agents but cost several times more. Planning is the most valuable use of extra agents; cheap models work well as planners but not as implementers (as summarized from the abstract); planner value keeps rising with team size while implementer and verifier gains level off beyond four workers.
- **Numbers:** teams cost roughly 3-7x a solo agent; heterogeneous 17-agent team within 1.4 points of a strong homogeneous 5-agent team at about 70% lower cost; Cohere blog reports up to about 10 percentage points gain on ProgramBench. Only abstract and first page seen.
- **For the fleet:** Cap implementer/verifier workers at about four; spend marginal effort on planning/decomposition quality rather than more implementers; heterogeneous (mixed-tier) teams can cut cost a lot. Note this partly conflicts with the Cursor/Decoder framing that frontier models should plan and cheap models execute: unresolved.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED (not proven fake). arxiv.org and cohere.com did not resolve; a GitHub search for the ID found nothing. The 3-7x cost, 17-agent team within 1.4 points at 70% lower cost, and 'gains level off beyond four workers' cannot be checked.

### Humanize builder/reviewer (arXiv 2610.08900, Oct 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08900 (Humanize authors (names not captured), 2026-10-06 (v2 2026-10-08), arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** A builder agent implements a human-approved plan in rounds; a reviewer from a DIFFERENT vendor, in a fresh context, is the only party allowed to declare the work complete. Builder and reviewer are treated as alternating samplers so a defect survives only if both miss it. Evidence is observational (118 public postmortems of real loops): independent review catches unsupported builder claims, but stopping remains a weakness.
- **Numbers:** 72 mechanical gates enforced by deterministic hooks; 118 postmortems; in reports that separate rounds by phase, two thirds of rounds occurred after implementation was already accepted. No controlled comparison.
- **For the fleet:** Separate who edits from who judges, use a different model family for the judge where possible, put the plan/acceptance contract in writing before execution, and make termination a deterministic rule because open-ended review loops waste rounds.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable; no secondary source found. The 72 gates, 118 postmortems and 'two thirds of rounds after acceptance' are unchecked.

### Attention Tax, Handoff Tax (arXiv 2610.06069, Oct 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06069 (Akshit Anchan, Nayonika Sen, 2026-10-05, arXiv preprint (cs.MA), 23 pages)
- **Claim:** Stylised reliability model reconciling conflicting results: decomposition relieves long-context degradation but pays a handoff tax when information is compressed between agents; redundancy (multiple samples) helps only to the extent failures are not shared. On a ledger-reconciliation task, the model predicted the depth at which decomposition beats a single agent.
- **Numbers:** crossover predicted at depth 10; decomposition predicted to win at depths 20, 50, 100; observed decomposed success lands within 9 percentage points of prediction at every depth. Abstract only.
- **For the fleet:** Split work only when the single agent's context would genuinely degrade (long render logs, many files); for short tasks the handoff tax outweighs the benefit. Keep handoffs lossless (files) to shrink the tax.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable; the crossover-at-depth-10 and within-9-points numbers are unchecked.

### EditJudgeBias MLLM judges (arXiv 2610.01670, Oct 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/html/2610.01670 (EditJudgeBias authors (names not captured), Oct 2026, arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** Audit of five multimodal-LLM judges for image-editing evaluation: pairwise judgments are highly sensitive to presentation order, and agreement with humans is only a partial check of reliability. Cues that significantly change agreement almost always reduce it.
- **Numbers:** swapping the display order reverses up to 60.9% of decisions, versus 1.0-10.5% reversal under repeated queries of the same order.
- **For the fleet:** Never trust a single-order A/B image verdict: run both orders (and repeat), accept only consistent verdicts, and treat order-flipping as 'no verified difference'.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable. The '60.9% reversed by order swap' vs '1.0-10.5% on repeat' numbers cannot be quoted. Related but different 2026 work exists on GitHub: naver-ai/MM-JudgeBias (ACL 2026 Main, compositional biases in MLLM judges).

### Facts Without Rules handoffs (arXiv 2608.29028, Aug 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29028 (Yian Wang, Agam Goyal, Eshwar Chandrasekharan, Hari Sundaram (UIUC), 2026-08-29, arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** Summarized handoffs keep operational facts but drop the 'boundary metadata' saying how facts may be used ('summary collapse'). Vague boundary wording leaks downstream; explicit constraints largely fix it. A single-agent control also leaked, so it is not only a multi-agent artifact.
- **Numbers:** boundary survival about 0.80 uncompressed vs about 0.57 under a 25-word summary budget, while fact survival stays near ceiling; vague boundary language leaked in 73% of GPT cases and 50% of DeepSeek-R1-32B cases; explicit constraints cut leakage to under 15% across all three tested models.
- **For the fleet:** When the orchestrator hands a worker a summary, constraints such as 'do not change sun calibration' or 'only touch rtx_look.py' must be explicit, typed fields, not prose that a summary can drop.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable. The 0.80 vs 0.57 boundary survival, 73% / 50% leakage and under-15% figures are unchecked.

### Claim Plane pre-write admission (arXiv 2608.00947, Aug 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00947 (Maxim Nikolaev (single author); companion architecture paper arXiv 2607.21909, Aug 2026, arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** Confirmatory study of deterministic pre-write admission for parallel coding agents (each worker declares a versioned change intent with base commit, resources and operations before writing). The reliability gain comes mostly from collapsing toward serial execution; dynamic admission was more selective but failed closed too often.
- **Numbers:** 30 frozen CooperBench feature pairs (15 conflict, 15 clean) x 3 seeds, 4 arms, 360 executions; pair pass 23.3% -> 50.0% (95% bootstrap CI 9.6-60.0 points on the paired difference); integration success 65.6% -> 96.7%; conflict-labeled pairs 6.7% -> 60.0%; static admission serialized 96.7% of executions; dynamic admission serialized 66.7% of conflict and 13.3% of clean cases but 46 of 90 executions failed closed on undeclared scope, pair pass only 22.2%.
- **For the fleet:** Require every worker to declare the files/settings it will touch before it starts; overlapping claims run serially. Accept that most of the reliability comes from serialization and that useful parallel speedup is still unproven.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable. CooperBench is real (see that entry), but the Claim Plane results (23.3% -> 50.0%, 96.7% serialized, 46/90 fail-closed) are unchecked.

### 2026 orchestration benchmarks and surveys

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25656 (OrchBench (Zhenzhen Ren +7); OrchestraBench; ClawArena-Team; MASEval; MDPI Future Internet survey; arXiv 2601.13671 survey, Jan-Aug 2026, arXiv preprints; MDPI Future Internet 18(6):326 (2026))
- **Claim:** OrchBench (2607.25656) scores orchestration plans (DAG of dependent subtasks, declared information transfers with retention ratios) by deterministic simulation; its conclusion is that preserving task-critical information matters more than adding agents and that parallelism benefits shrink as coordination failures accumulate (a reviewer notes this is partly built into the simulator). MASEval (2603.08835) argues topology, orchestration logic and error handling change results, so benchmark systems, not just models. The Future Internet survey proposes centralized / decentralized / hierarchical topologies plus an adaptive axis. OrchestraBench (2608.05263), ClawArena-Team (2606.31174) and arXiv 2601.13671 (Orchestration of Multi-Agent Systems) were seen by title only. A financial-document study (2603.22651) lists a reflexive pattern at F1 0.943 for 2.3x cost vs hierarchical 0.921 for 1.4x (from a reference list, unverified).
- **Numbers:** OrchBench: Pearson r=0.816 against Claude Code executions using 1.3% of the tokens and 10.3% of the wall-clock (leave-one-out r drops to 0.421 per a reviewer; six models).
- **For the fleet:** There is no settled 2026 benchmark that tells us the right topology for image-judged code editing; we must measure our own fleet against a single-agent baseline. A cheap plan-level simulation is a possible pre-check on decompositions.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** Only MASEval was confirmed: github.com/maseval/MASEval, 'MASEval: Extending Multi-Agent Evaluation from Models to Systems', Emde, Rubinstein, Goel, Heakl, Yun, Oh, Gubri; ACL 2026 System Demonstrations pp. 345-356. It says implementation choices such as topology, orchestration logic and error handling substantially affect performance. The README gives no arXiv ID, so 2603.08835 is unconfirmed. OrchBench (2607.25656, r=0.816, 1.3% tokens, 10.3% time), OrchestraBench, ClawArena-Team, 2601.13671, the MDPI survey and 2603.22651 are unverifiable. A GitHub search for 'OrchBench' returned only dklyle/orchbench, an unrelated 2019 repo.

### Merge conflicts in agent PRs (2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03551 (AgenticFlict (arXiv 2604.03551); arXiv 2607.04697; SBES 2026 paper on AI conflict resolution, Apr-Jul 2026, arXiv preprints; SBES 2026)
- **Claim:** Large-scale evidence that independently working coding agents collide often: conflicts are common in agent-authored pull requests, even between identical agent instances, and vendors differ enormously in how well agents resolve conflicts themselves.
- **Numbers:** AgenticFlict: over 142K agentic PRs, 29K+ with merge conflicts = 27.67% conflict rate; arXiv 2607.04697 merge-replay: 19.8% textual conflict rate for identical agent instances; SBES 2026: self-resolution rates differ by nearly 60x across vendors.
- **For the fleet:** Assume a double-digit percentage of parallel candidate edits will conflict textually if they touch the same files; give each candidate a disjoint knob/file and have the orchestrator do the merges.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** AgenticFlict is real (github.com/unlv-evol/AgenticFlict): 'AgenticFlict: A Large-Scale Dataset of Merge Conflicts in AI Coding Agent Pull Requests on GitHub', Ogenrwot and Businge (UNLV), arXiv 2604.03551 confirmed. It reports 142,652 PRs identified, 107,026 successfully simulated, 29,609 conflicting = 27.67%, 5 agents. Correction: 27.67% is 29,609 / 107,026 simulated PRs, not of 142K (29,609 / 142,652 = 20.8%). arXiv 2607.04697 (19.8%) and the SBES 2026 '60x' figure are unverifiable.

### Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage (arXiv 2606.13003, Jun 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13003 (Prathyusha Jwalapuram, Hehai Lin et al., Jun 2026 (v2), arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** Automatically generated multi-agent systems trail chain-of-thought with self-consistency (CoT-SC) on standard reasoning sets and on interactive BrowseComp-Plus, while costing up to about 10x more. On a synthetic diagnostic set with real decomposition/context-separation/parallelization potential, expert-designed MAS beat auto-generated ones. Authors blame 'architectural bloat'. Models: GPT-4o, GPT-5, GPT-OSS-120B, Gemini-2.5-Pro. A reviewer notes no error bars or statistical tests in the abstract.
- **Numbers:** up to about 10x cost for equal or worse accuracy than CoT-SC.
- **For the fleet:** Do not let agents design their own elaborate topologies; hand-design a minimal fleet with justified roles and always compare against a strong single-agent/sampling baseline.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** A matching repo exists, github.com/linhh29/the-illusion-of-multi-agent-advantages (README title 'The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantages', plural). It lists automatic MAS (ADAS, AFlow, DyLAN, MaAS, MAS-Orchestra, MAS-Zero), an expert-designed MAS, CoT and CoT-SC baselines, benchmarks GPQA, HLE-Math, BrowseComp+, Smfrs, SWE-Bench, and models including gpt-4o, gpt-5, gpt-oss-120b, gemini-pro. The README has no results, authors, arXiv ID, or '10x cost' figure, so the headline claims are unchecked and 2606.13003 is unconfirmed.

### Do More Agents Help? (arXiv 2606.05670, Jun 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05670 (Yuhang Fu and six co-authors, 2026-06-04, arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** Controlled, protocol-aligned comparison of six multi-agent systems against a single-agent anchor on ten reasoning/coding/tool-use benchmarks with GPT-4.1: most multi-agent systems are worse in accuracy and cost. A Claude-Code-style runtime workflow did very well on GAIA. Versions disagree on details.
- **Numbers:** v1: five of six MAS fall 2.56-11.29 points below the single-agent anchor on benchmark-balanced average accuracy; v2: EvoAgent 79.70% vs Core 76.65%, the other five trail by 1.71-8.76 points; LLM-Debate gains 9.67 points on HumanEval but loses 1.96 pooled at 1.39x tokens; GAIA: Claude-Code-style runtime workflow 66.72% overall, 69.23% on Level 3, more than 20 points above the strongest non-Claude baseline (Jarvis, a fixed MAS).
- **For the fleet:** A fixed hand-wired multi-agent pipeline is not automatically better than one strong agent; a flexible orchestrator-with-subagents runtime (what Claude Code provides) looked better than rigid MAS on GAIA, supporting a lead-plus-subagents design over a fixed pipeline of role agents.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable; a GitHub search for the ID returned nothing. The v1/v2 numbers (2.56-11.29 points, 79.70% vs 76.65%, GAIA 66.72%) are unchecked.

### Co-Coder cohesion-aware partitioning (arXiv 2606.00953, May 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00953 (Xu Yang, Lunyiu Nie, Ethan Chandra, Stanislav Gannutin, Fangru Lin, Swarat Chaudhuri, 2026-05-31, arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** Treats multi-agent coding orchestration as graph partitioning: build a dependency graph by static analysis, isolate hub files, form cohesive partitions by community detection, schedule with a dependency-aware scheduler. Parallelism pays off only when partitions are cohesive; the paper reports Claude Code with Agent Teams cut latency but had the lowest pass rate.
- **Numbers:** 28 real tasks (DevEval, CodeProjectEval); pass rate up to +14.0 points (34.1% vs 20.1% sequential on CodeProjectEval); up to 2.10x wall-clock speedup; up to 35% lower API cost; Claude Code with Agent Teams pass rate 54.1% (lowest). Largest gains on the most dependency-dense projects.
- **For the fleet:** Partition by dependency cohesion, not by role: keep tightly coupled files (rtx_look.py, drone_look.py, scene.py, quadcopter_pbr.py) with one writer and parallelize only across weakly coupled modules (e.g. world/ builder vs lucid.py/sensor_sim.py).
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable; a GitHub search found nothing. The +14.0 points, 2.10x speedup and 'Agent Teams 54.1% (lowest)' are unchecked.

### STORM state management (arXiv 2605.20563, May 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20563 (Mengyang Liu and four co-authors (project page lists NeurIPS 2026 poster), 2026-05-19, arXiv preprint; NeurIPS 2026 poster per project page)
- **Claim:** Per-agent git worktrees defer conflict resolution to a post-hoc merge where recovery is expensive. STORM instead rejects a write if the agent's view of the target file and its context dependencies is stale and returns fresh content for retry. Late-caught conflicts correlate with lower pass rates; STORM's edge grows with cross-file coupling.
- **Numbers:** Commit0-Lite with Sonnet 4.6: STORM 82.5% macro / 46.2% weighted vs single-agent 66.4% / 20.7% vs GitWorktree 63.8% / 24.6%; PaperBench Code-Dev 74.1 vs 72.7 vs 68.7 (baseline labels unclear). Only abstract/intro/conclusion seen.
- **For the fleet:** Worktrees alone are not a free lunch (macro pass 63.8 was below single-agent 66.4); stale-view checks (rebase/re-read before merge, re-render after merge) matter. Make each candidate rebase onto current main and re-verify before the orchestrator merges.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable; a GitHub search found nothing. The Commit0-Lite numbers (82.5 / 66.4 / 63.8) and the NeurIPS 2026 poster claim are unchecked.

### Cognition: Multi-Agents: What's Actually Working (Apr 2026)

- **Source:** https://cognition.com/blog/multi-agents-working (Walden Yan, Cognition (Devin), about 2026-04-21/22 (date inferred from secondary sources), Cognition blog)
- **Claim:** Ten months after 'Don't Build Multi-Agents', Cognition says a narrower class of patterns works: many agents contribute intelligence while WRITES STAY SINGLE-THREADED. Shape called 'map-reduce-and-manage' (manager splits work, children execute, manager synthesizes). Working examples reported: clean-context reviewer, 'Smart Friend' (a stronger model consulted by a weaker primary agent), a manager coordinating scope across children; unstructured agent networks are called mostly a distraction. Reported problems: managers trained on small-scope delegation become overly prescriptive when they lack codebase context; agents wrongly assume they share state with their children; a weaker model has trouble knowing when it is at its limits. Secondary reports: Devin Security Swarm (Jul 2026) tests findings in an isolated sandbox instead of trusting the reducer's written conclusion.
- **Numbers:** none (qualitative). Primary page could not be fetched from this machine (DNS failure for cognition.ai/cognition.com); content is from search-result summaries and secondary write-ups.
- **For the fleet:** Matches the intended fleet: intelligence is parallel (explorers, critics), the write path is single-threaded through the orchestrator; use a clean-context reviewer; tell workers explicitly what state they do NOT share.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** cognition.com did not resolve (DNS) and no secondary copy was opened. Everything, including 'writes stay single-threaded', 'Smart Friend' and map-reduce-and-manage, is unchecked.

### Single vs multi-agent at equal thinking tokens (arXiv 2604.02460, Apr 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02460 (Dat Tran, Douwe Kiela (Stanford), Apr 2026, arXiv preprint (under review))
- **Claim:** When intermediate thinking tokens are held equal, single agents match or beat multi-agent systems on multi-hop reasoning; multi-agent only becomes competitive when a single agent's effective context use is degraded or when more compute is spent. Argument rests on the Data Processing Inequality (handoffs can only lose information). Scope limited to multi-hop reasoning; may not transfer to settings with different tools or trust boundaries.
- **Numbers:** six thinking-token budgets from 100 to 10,000; Qwen3-30B, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-70B, Gemini 2.5; five MAS architectures (sequential, subtask-parallel, parallel-roles, debate, ensemble); FRAMES and MuSiQue.
- **For the fleet:** Part of any multi-agent 'win' is just extra tokens. Compare the fleet against a single agent given the same total token budget before crediting the topology.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable; no secondary source found. Authors (Tran, Kiela) and the budgets and models are unchecked.

### Scaling test-time compute for agentic coding (arXiv 2604.16529, Apr 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16529 (Salakhutdinov group (Russ Salakhutdinov announced; author list not captured), 2026-04-16, arXiv preprint (cs.SE))
- **Claim:** Converts each agent rollout into a compact structured summary (hypotheses, progress, failure modes) and drops low-signal trace detail. Parallel scaling uses Recursive Tournament Voting (RTV: recursive small-group comparisons over rollout summaries); sequential scaling uses Parallel-Distill-Refine conditioned on summaries of prior attempts.
- **Numbers:** Claude-4.5-Opus 70.9% -> 77.6% on SWE-Bench Verified (mini-SWE-agent) and 46.9% (47.0% elsewhere on the page) -> 59.1% on Terminal-Bench v2.0; Gemini-3.1-Pro 72.3% -> 76.6% on SWE-Bench Verified.
- **For the fleet:** Between rounds, pass structured summaries of earlier attempts (hypothesis, what was tried, render result, why it failed), not raw transcripts; pick among parallel candidates with small-group comparisons rather than one big ranking.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable. The 70.9 -> 77.6 and 46.9/47.0 -> 59.1 numbers are unchecked, and the report itself notes an internal 46.9 vs 47.0 inconsistency.

### Anthropic multi-agent coordination patterns (Apr 2026)

- **Source:** https://claude.com/blog/multi-agent-coordination-patterns (Anthropic (claude.com blog), 2026-04-10, Anthropic blog)
- **Claim:** Five patterns: generator-verifier, orchestrator-subagent, agent teams, message bus, shared state. 'For most use cases, we recommend starting with orchestrator-subagent.' Named failure modes: generator-verifier - 'A verifier told only to check whether output is good, with no further criteria, will rubber-stamp the generator's output', and loops stall without a max-iteration limit and fallback; orchestrator-subagent - 'The orchestrator becomes an information bottleneck'; agent teams - 'Independence is the critical requirement' (conflicting edits on shared resources); message bus - silent failure if the router drops an event; shared state - reactive loops need 'first-class termination conditions' (time budget, convergence threshold).
- **Numbers:** none
- **For the fleet:** Use orchestrator-subagent as the base topology; give every verifier concrete criteria; put a max-iteration cap and a fallback on every generate-verify loop; avoid shared-state/message-bus designs.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened https://claude.com/blog/multi-agent-coordination-patterns. Title 'Multi-agent coordination patterns: Five approaches and when to use them', April 10 2026, Cara Phillips with contributors. All five patterns and all five quoted phrases are verbatim: 'starting with orchestrator-subagent', verifier 'will rubber-stamp', 'information bottleneck', 'Independence is the critical requirement', 'first-class termination conditions'.

### Anthropic advisor strategy (Apr 2026)

- **Source:** https://claude.com/blog/the-advisor-strategy (Anthropic (claude.com blog), Apr 2026 (beta header advisor-tool-2026-03-01), Anthropic blog / Messages API beta)
- **Claim:** Cheap executor (Sonnet or Haiku) runs the task end to end and consults an Opus advisor only when stuck; the advisor never calls tools or writes user-facing output. Opposite of the usual big-model-decomposes-for-small-workers design: the small model drives and escalates. Anthropic recommends evaluating executor-solo, executor+advisor and Opus-solo on your own eval suite.
- **Numbers:** Sonnet+Opus advisor: +2.7 percentage points on SWE-bench Multilingual with cost per agentic task down 11.9%; Haiku+Opus advisor 41.2% vs 19.7% solo on BrowseComp ('trails Sonnet solo by 29% in score but costs 85% less per task'; relative vs absolute not specified); advisor writes typically 400-700 text tokens per consultation; max_uses caps advisor calls (example 3); advisor tokens billed at Opus rates and reported separately.
- **For the fleet:** Cheap workers should escalate to the strongest model through a capped advisor call instead of the strongest model running everything; track advisor spend separately.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened https://claude.com/blog/the-advisor-strategy (dated April 9, 2026; full title 'The advisor strategy: Give Sonnet an intelligence boost with Opus'). Verified: +2.7 pp on SWE-bench Multilingual with cost per agentic task down 11.9%; Haiku+Opus advisor 41.2% vs 19.7% solo on BrowseComp; 'trails Sonnet solo by 29% in score but costs 85% less' (relative vs absolute unspecified); 400-700 tokens; max_uses (example 3); header advisor-tool-2026-03-01; advisor never calls tools or writes user-facing output; advisor tokens billed at advisor rates and reported separately. Minor: the article says the executor consults when it hits a decision it cannot reasonably solve, not 'when stuck'.

### Anthropic harness design for long-running apps (Mar 2026)

- **Source:** https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps (Anthropic Engineering, 2026-03-24, Anthropic engineering blog)
- **Claim:** GAN-style generator/evaluator separation (planner + generator + evaluator using Playwright to click through the live app). Agents 'reliably skew positive when grading their own work'; a standalone evaluator is far more tractable to tune toward skepticism, but it is still an LLM prone to leniency and had to be calibrated against the author's own judgments with few-shot examples and detailed score breakdowns. Grading criteria had hard thresholds; sprint contracts (generator proposes what/how verified, evaluator agrees) had up to 27 criteria. Components were removed one at a time to find which are load-bearing; with Opus 4.6 the sprint decomposition was dropped, the planner kept (without it the generator under-scoped), evaluator moved to one final pass. Evaluator is worth its cost only when the task 'sits beyond what the current model does reliably solo'. Agents communicate via files. Context resets vs compaction: resets were needed for Sonnet 4.5 'context anxiety' but not Opus 4.5.
- **Numbers:** retro game maker: solo 20 min / $9 vs full harness 6 hr / $200 (over 20x cost); DAW build (Opus 4.6): total 3 hr 50 min / $124.70 (planner $0.46; build rounds $71.08, $36.89, $5.88; QA rounds $3.24, $3.09, $4.06); frontend runs 5-15 iterations, up to 4 hours, scores improved then plateaued with headroom remaining.
- **For the fleet:** Evaluator rounds are cheap relative to builder rounds (about $3-4 vs $6-71), so spend on independent, calibrated, threshold-based critique; calibrate the image critic with the owner's own good/bad examples; prune harness components that are not load-bearing as models improve.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps; March 24 2026, Prithvi Rajasekaran. Verified: 20 min/$9 vs 6 hr/$200 (over 20x); DAW table 3 hr 50 min/$124.70 (body text says about 4 hours and $124) with the exact per-agent costs; 'agents reliably skew positive when grading their own work'; 5-15 iterations, runs up to four hours; planner kept, evaluator moved to a single final pass; 'worth the cost when the task sits beyond what the current model does reliably solo'; communication via files; components removed one at a time. Minor fixes: '27 criteria' is Sprint 3's count, not a maximum; hard per-criterion thresholds describe the earlier sprint-based evaluator; Playwright is the evaluator's tool. I did not check the 'scores plateaued' wording.

### CAID asynchronous SWE agents (arXiv 2603.21489, Mar 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21489 (Jiayi Geng, Graham Neubig (CMU); code github.com/JiayiGeng/CAID on OpenHands, 2026-03-23, arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** CAID (Centralized Asynchronous Isolated Delegation): a manager builds a dependency graph and delegates tasks to engineer agents that each work in their own git worktree, run tests, and are integrated by git merge. Improves over single-agent baselines across three models (Claude 4.5 Sonnet, GLM 4.7, MiniMax 2.5). Repo exposes max_subagents and rounds_of_chat parameters; numbers are in the paper, not the README.
- **Numbers:** abstract: +26.7 points absolute on PaperBench and +14.3 on Commit0; secondary review (unconfirmed against tables): PaperBench 63.3% (CAID, Claude 4.5) vs 57.2% single agent; Commit0-Lite MiniMax 2.5 42.3% -> 57.0%.
- **For the fleet:** The strongest published positive result for parallel coding agents is exactly central manager + dependency graph + isolated worktrees + tests + merge by the manager: adopt that shape, not peer-to-peer.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** github.com/JiayiGeng/CAID confirms arXiv 2603.21489, authors Jiayi Geng and Graham Neubig, OpenHands, isolated git worktrees, max_subagents and rounds_of_chat (max rounds of task assignment per engineer), and Commit0 and PaperBench support. Paper title is 'Effective Strategies for Asynchronous Software Engineering Agents' (the report's name is a label). The README has none of the results (+26.7, +14.3, 63.3 vs 57.2, 42.3 -> 57.0), so those are unverified, as are the three models and the 2026-03-23 date.

### Selection bottleneck (arXiv 2603.20324, Mar 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.20324 (Artem Maryanskyy (v2 adds Dmitry Budnikov, Alibek T. Kaliyev), Mar 2026 (published in Applied Sciences 2026), arXiv; MDPI Applied Sciences 16(10):4914)
- **Claim:** Resolves conflicting 'diverse teams help / hurt' results: the deciding factor in generate-then-select pipelines is the quality of the selection step; judge-based selection beat MoA-style synthesis by a wide margin. Pith review: crossover threshold is fitted not derived, selector quality never measured directly, synthesis result rests on one implementation.
- **Numbers:** 42 tasks, 7 categories, N=210: diverse team with judge-based selection 0.810 win rate vs 0.512 for a homogeneous team (near chance); win rates shrink 53-67% under independent evaluation (Spearman rho=0.90 for direction); synthesis never preferred by the original judge panel but gets 0.312 under the independent panel; weaker-model result was exploratory (p<1e-4, not pre-registered).
- **For the fleet:** With N candidate edits per round, the quality of the choose-the-best step (calibrated, order-robust, independent judge) matters more than N or candidate diversity. Select, do not blend.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv and mdpi.com did not resolve. The win rates 0.810 / 0.512, N=210, rho=0.90 and 0.312 are unchecked.

### Anthropic C compiler with parallel Claudes (Feb 2026)

- **Source:** https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (Anthropic Engineering, 2026-02-05, Anthropic engineering blog)
- **Claim:** 16 parallel Claude agents (Opus 4.6), each in its own Docker container with its own clone of a bare upstream repo, built a Rust C compiler. NO orchestration agent and no inter-agent messaging: agents claim tasks via lock files in current_tasks/, pull-merge-push, and pick the 'next most obvious' problem. Worked because many tests were failing independently; the Linux-kernel build was one giant task where all 16 agents hit the same bug and overwrote each other's fixes, fixed by using GCC as a known-good oracle so agents could work on different files. Test harness lessons: tests must be nearly perfect ('Claude will solve whatever problem it's given'), keep console output small with details in log files, precompute aggregate stats, add a deterministic --fast 1%/10% sample for 'time blindness'. Near the end new commits often broke existing features, so a CI gate was added. Specialized roles (dedupe, performance, design critique, docs). Author: result 'has nearly reached the limits of Opus's abilities'.
- **Numbers:** 16 agents; nearly 2,000 Claude Code sessions over about two weeks; 2 billion input and 140 million output tokens; just under $20,000; about 100,000 lines; 99% pass on most compiler test suites; output code less efficient than GCC with optimizations off.
- **For the fleet:** Leaderless lock-file swarms work only with a near-perfect automated test oracle and many independent failures - we have neither for 'looks realistic', so we need a central orchestrator and an explicit oracle (baseline frames, numeric radiance checks, log checks). Add a regression gate so merged edits cannot silently undo earlier verified improvements; keep tool output short.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler; Feb 5 2026, Nicholas Carlini. Verified: 16 agents, Opus 4.6, nearly 2,000 sessions over two weeks, 2B input and 140M output tokens, just under $20,000, 100,000 lines, 99% on most suites, lock files in current_tasks/, 'I don't use an orchestration agent', GCC oracle, --fast 1%/10% sample, time blindness, CI added after regressions, specialized roles, 'nearly reached the limits of Opus's abilities', output less efficient than GCC with optimizations off. Quote fixes: the article says 'Claude will work autonomously to solve whatever problem I give it', not 'Claude will solve whatever problem it's given'; for the kernel build it says every agent would hit the same bug and overwrite each other's changes, not literally all 16.

### Anthropic infrastructure noise in agentic evals (Feb 2026)

- **Source:** https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/infrastructure-noise (Anthropic Engineering, 2026-02-05, Anthropic engineering blog)
- **Claim:** Resource settings alone move agentic-coding benchmark scores by several points; resources should be treated as a controlled variable like prompt format or temperature, with guaranteed allocation and a separate kill threshold.
- **Numbers:** Terminal-Bench 2.0: setups at the extremes differ by 6 percentage points (p<0.01); infra error rate 5.8% at 1x vs 0.5% uncapped; 1x->3x lowers infra errors to 2.1% but score change within noise (p=0.40); 'leaderboard differences below 3 percentage points deserve skepticism'; SWE-bench at 5x RAM only +1.54 points; naive binomial CIs already span 1-2 points.
- **For the fleet:** For the render loop, fix and log everything that affects pixels (seed, resolution scale, warm-up frames, time of day, GPU state) and measure baseline-vs-baseline noise before accepting a candidate; ignore small differences.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/infrastructure-noise; title 'Quantifying infrastructure noise in agentic coding evals', Feb 5 2026, Gian Segato. All five claims are verbatim: 6 pp gap (p<0.01), 5.8% -> 0.5% infra errors, 1x-3x within noise (p=0.40), 'below 3 percentage points deserve skepticism', SWE-bench 5x RAM only +1.54 points. The 3-point rule is about leaderboard comparisons on those benchmarks, not a universal threshold for render scores.

### Cursor scaling agents (Jan 2026)

- **Source:** https://cursor.com/blog/scaling-agents (Cursor (Wilson Lin); FastRender browser experiment, Jan 2026 (post ~2026-01-14/20; follow-up 'Towards self-driving codebases'), Cursor blog; secondary summaries (ZenML, DEV, GIGAZINE, Simon Willison))
- **Claim:** Flat peer-to-peer coordination among agents broke down: shared-file locks became bottlenecks and agents became risk-averse; optimistic concurrency control made agents avoid hard tasks and make only small safe changes. Cursor moved to a hierarchy: planners (which can spawn sub-planners) explore and create tasks, workers execute one task without coordinating with other workers, and a judge decides after each cycle whether to continue; each cycle starts fresh. An 'integrator' role for conflicts created more bottlenecks than it solved. A later post reports infrastructure shifting to disk I/O with hundreds of agents. Browser working state is contested.
- **Numbers:** scale figures conflict between sources: up to 2,000 concurrent agents and nearly 30,000 commits over three weeks (one source); over 1 million lines of Rust (most sources; one says 3 million); about 1,000 commits per hour over a week (another). Primary page not reachable from this machine (cursor.com DNS failure).
- **For the fleet:** Do not build flat or lock-based coordination; use planner -> narrow workers -> judge with fresh context each cycle, no integrator agent (the orchestrator merges).
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** cursor.com and simonwillison.net did not resolve (DNS), so nothing was opened. The locks/optimistic-concurrency/integrator claims and the scale numbers are unverified; the report already notes the numbers conflict across secondary sources.

### CooperBench (arXiv 2601.13295, Jan 2026)

- **Source:** https://github.com/hhh2210/CooperBench (Stanford and SAP Labs researchers (benchmark paper 'Why Coding Agents Cannot be Your Teammates Yet'); numbers here are from a GitHub README of a fork, Jan 2026, arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** 'Curse of coordination': two peer agents implementing different features of the same repo (communicating through messages) succeed far less often than one agent doing both. Communication reduces merge conflicts but does not raise success; failures are coordination (expectations, commitments, communication), not coding skill. Settings: solo, coop (N peers via Redis messaging, optional shared git remote), team (lead + members with shared task list).
- **Numbers:** 652 tasks, 12 repositories, 4 languages (Python, TypeScript, Go, Rust); GPT-5 and Claude Sonnet 4.5 reach about 25% success in two-agent coop, 'roughly 50% lower' than a single agent doing both; agents spend up to 20% of budget on communication; failure categories: expectation 42%, commitment 32%, communication 26%; success declines monotonically from 2 to 4 agents (secondary summary).
- **For the fleet:** No peer-to-peer negotiation between workers; workers get disjoint, fully specified slices and never need to coordinate; keep concurrent peers to a minimum.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** Both github.com/hhh2210/CooperBench (a fork, 0 stars) and the canonical github.com/cooperbench/CooperBench READMEs confirm: title 'CooperBench: Why Coding Agents Cannot be Your Teammates Yet', arXiv 2601.13295, authors Khatua, Zhu, Tran, Prabhudesai, Sadrieh, Lieberwirth, Yu, Fu, Ryan, Pei, Yang (Stanford and SAP Labs), 652 tasks, 12 repos, 4 languages, GPT-5 and Sonnet 4.5 about 25% in 2-agent coop vs 'roughly 50% lower than a single agent', up to 20% of budget on communication, failures 42/32/26%, modes solo/coop (Redis)/team. NOT in the README: 'success declines monotonically from 2 to 4 agents' (the page has no 4-agent data). Cite the canonical repo, not the fork.

### Anthropic when to use multi-agent systems (Jan 2026)

- **Source:** https://claude.com/blog/building-multi-agent-systems-when-and-how-to-use-them (Anthropic (claude.com blog), 2026-01-23, Anthropic blog)
- **Claim:** Start with a single agent; teams have spent months on multi-agent designs that matched 'improved prompting on a single agent'. Multi-agent is justified in three situations: context protection (subtask emits more than about 1,000 tokens that mostly do not matter to the main task), parallelization (gain is thoroughness more than speed, often longer total time), specialization (single agent has too many tools, often 20+, or conflicting modes; routing errors are the risk). Decompose by CONTEXT, not by problem type: planner/implementer/tester/reviewer splits create a 'telephone game'. Tightly coupled work, sequential phases and shared-state work stay in one agent. A dedicated verifier needs only criteria + artifact, but 'the most significant failure mode for verification subagents is marking outputs as passing without thorough testing' (mitigate with concrete criteria, comprehensive checks, negative tests, run the full suite). Thresholds 'will shift as models improve'.
- **Numbers:** multi-agent implementations typically use 3-10x more tokens than single-agent approaches for equivalent tasks.
- **For the fleet:** Justify every agent by one of the three situations; make critics/verifiers the main non-exploration subagent; do not create role-per-phase agents for work that shares context.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened https://claude.com/blog/building-multi-agent-systems-when-and-how-to-use-them; Jan 23 2026, Cara Phillips et al. Verified: three situations, >1000 tokens of context volume, 20+ tools (also '15-20+'), 3-10x tokens, telephone game, 'most significant failure mode for verification subagents is marking outputs as passing without thorough testing'. Wording fix: the article says improved prompting on a single agent 'achieved equivalent results' after months of multi-agent work.

### Single-agent baselines (arXiv 2601.12307, 2601.04748, Jan 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12307 (Jiawei Xu +10 (Rethinking the Value of Multi-Agent Workflow, cited as ICLR 2026); Xiaoxiao Li (When Single-Agent with Skills Replace Multi-Agent Systems and When They Fail), Jan 2026, arXiv preprints)
- **Claim:** A single LLM simulating a homogeneous multi-agent workflow over multi-turn conversation matches its performance (7 benchmarks) with a KV-cache-reuse efficiency edge; it cannot capture heterogeneous multi-model workflows because models do not share a KV cache. A single agent selecting from a skill library matches multi-agent accuracy with fewer tokens (GSM8K, HumanEval, HotpotQA, preliminary), but selection accuracy collapses beyond a critical library size and competing skills hurt.
- **Numbers:** 2601.04748: adding competing skills degrades accuracy by 7-30% with one competitor per skill and 17-63% with two; selection accuracy stable up to a critical library size then drops sharply. 2601.12307: no single headline number captured.
- **For the fleet:** Prefer skills (loadable instructions) over extra role-agents where roles share context; keep the skill library small and non-overlapping; use separate agents only for context isolation, parallel exploration, independence of judging, or heterogeneous models.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED for either ID. arXiv unreachable. The '7-30% / 17-63% degradation from competing skills' numbers are unchecked.

### Anthropic demystifying evals (Jan 2026)

- **Source:** https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/demystifying-evals-for-ai-agents (Anthropic Engineering, 2026-01-09, Anthropic engineering blog)
- **Claim:** Combine code-based, model-based and human graders; prefer deterministic graders where possible, LLM graders where necessary, humans judiciously. Vague rubrics produce inconsistent judgments; use structured per-dimension rubrics, give the judge an 'Unknown' way out, calibrate LLM judges against human experts, grade outcomes not paths, make graders hard to game, isolate each trial from clean state, and read many transcripts. A 0% pass rate across many trials usually signals a broken task. Capability evals start low; regression evals should be near 100%; saturated evals hide progress.
- **Numbers:** example: 75% per-trial success gives pass^3 = 0.75^3 about 42% (pass@k = at least one success in k tries; pass^k = all k succeed). Gives no specific multi-agent eval guidance.
- **For the fleet:** Build the realism judge as per-dimension rubric + deterministic measurements + calibration set, with an 'unsure' option; keep a regression set of previously fixed defects; re-render for consistency (pass^k mindset) before accepting an improvement.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/demystifying-evals-for-ai-agents; Jan 9 2026, Grace, Hadfield, Olivares, De Jonghe. Verified: pass^3 = 0.75^3 about 42%, the 'Unknown' way out for LLM judges, code/model/human graders, regression evals near 100% while capability evals start low. '0% pass rate' is 'most often a signal of a broken task' (a heuristic, not a rule).

### Google/MIT Science of Scaling Agent Systems (Dec 2025)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08296 (Yubin Kim et al. (Google Research, Google DeepMind, MIT); Google Research blog and MIT Media Lab project page, Dec 2025 (v1); later revision, arXiv 2512.08296)
- **Claim:** Quantitative study of single-agent vs four multi-agent architectures (independent, centralized, decentralized, hybrid) across three LLM families. Multi-agent coordination dramatically helps parallelizable tasks and hurts sequential ones; coordination shows diminishing or negative returns once the single-agent baseline is already strong (capability saturation); tool-heavy tasks are disproportionately hurt by coordination overhead under fixed compute; independent agents amplify errors far more than centrally verified ones. Predictive model picks the best architecture for 87% of held-out configurations. Versions DISAGREE: v1 = 180 configurations, 4 benchmarks (Finance-Agent, BrowseComp-Plus, PlanCraft, Workbench), R2 about 0.51-0.52; later revision = 260 configurations, 6 benchmarks, cross-validated R2 0.373 (0.413 with a task-grounded metric) and the 17.2x/4.4x wording dropped. MIT page warns the error-amplification metric measures extra computational work from coordination failures, not that final answers are 17.2x/4.4x more likely wrong.
- **Numbers:** error amplification 17.2x (independent) vs 4.4x (centralized); saturation threshold about 45% single-agent accuracy (beta=-0.408, p<0.001); centralized +80.9% (one source +80.8%) on parallelizable Finance-Agent; decentralized +9.2% on dynamic web navigation; all multi-agent variants degrade sequential PlanCraft by 39-70% (later revision: range +80.8% to -70.0%). Exact figures taken from search-result summaries and a GitHub note of the abstract; arXiv and research.google were unreachable from this machine.
- **For the fleet:** Use a centralized topology with a verification step, parallelize only decomposable work, keep sequential edit chains single-agent, and expect little gain from extra agents on sub-problems the single agent already solves well (above about 45% reliability).
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** github.com/ybkim95/agent-scaling README confirms arXiv 2512.08296 (earlier title 'Towards a science of scaling agent systems', authors include Yubin Kim, Ken Gu, Chanwoo Park). It was published in Nature Machine Intelligence 2026 as 'Capable language models can outgrow the benefits of collaboration' (DOI 10.1038/s42256-026-01268-y), which the report omits. The article uses 260 configurations and six benchmarks (BrowseComp-Plus, PlanCraft, SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench, Workbench, Finance Agent), matching the report's 'later revision'. The README does not contain 17.2x/4.4x, 87%, 45% or 80.9%, so those, the 180-configuration v1 and the R2 values are unverified. research.google and arxiv.org did not resolve.

### Anthropic effective harnesses for long-running agents (Nov 2025)

- **Source:** https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents (Anthropic Engineering, 2025-11-26, Anthropic engineering blog)
- **Claim:** Initializer agent (first session) writes init.sh, a progress file and a JSON feature list (over 200 features, all initially failing); later coding-agent sessions do ONE feature at a time, commit with descriptive messages and update claude-progress.txt. JSON chosen because the model is less likely to overwrite it than Markdown; instruction: 'It is unacceptable to remove or edit tests'. Failure modes: one-shotting, premature victory declaration, marking features complete without testing; fixed by browser-automation testing 'as a human user'. Open question whether a single general agent or specialised agents (testing, QA, cleanup) do better.
- **Numbers:** over 200 features in the claude.ai-clone example.
- **For the fleet:** Persist the loop state in a JSON experiment ledger (id, hypothesis, files claimed, render ids, metrics, verdict) that agents may update only in permitted fields, plus a progress file and git commits for handoff; one hypothesis per commit.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents; Nov 26 2025, Justin Young. Verified: init.sh, claude-progress.txt, over 200 JSON features all initially failing, JSON less likely to be overwritten than Markdown, 'unacceptable to remove or edit tests', one-shotting and premature victory, browser automation, open question on a single general agent vs specialised agents.

### Debate or Vote (NeurIPS 2025)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.17536 (Hyeong Kyu Choi, Xiaojin Zhu, Sharon Li (UW-Madison), 2025 (arXiv Aug 2025), NeurIPS 2025)
- **Claim:** Across seven NLP benchmarks, simple majority voting without inter-agent debate accounts for most of the gain usually credited to multi-agent debate; debate is modeled as a martingale over belief trajectories so it does not raise expected correctness on average. Biasing belief updates toward correction can help, but ensembling is often more reliable.
- **Numbers:** 7 benchmarks; no further numbers captured.
- **For the fleet:** For picking among candidates or judgments, use independent judges plus a vote; do not have critics debate each other.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** github.com/deeplearning-wisc/debate-or-vote confirms 'Debate or Vote: Which Yields Better Decisions in Multi-Agent Large Language Models?', Hyeong Kyu (Froilan) Choi, Xiaojin (Jerry) Zhu, Yixuan (Sharon) Li, NeurIPS 2025 Spotlight, arXiv 2508.17536. The README does not state the majority-vote-explains-the-gain claim, the martingale model or the seven benchmarks, so those are unverified here.

### Anthropic effective context engineering (Sep 2025)

- **Source:** https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents (Anthropic Engineering, 2025-09-29, Anthropic engineering blog)
- **Claim:** Sub-agents with clean context windows do focused work and return only a condensed summary to the lead; multi-agent architectures pay off for 'complex research and analysis where parallel exploration pays dividends', while compaction suits long back-and-forth and note-taking suits iterative work with clear milestones. Just-in-time retrieval (keep file paths/identifiers, load at runtime) is preferred to pre-loading; lightest-touch compaction is tool-result clearing.

### MAST Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail (2025)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.13657 (Mert Cemri, Melissa Z. Pan, Shuyi Yang et al. (UC Berkeley), 2025 (arXiv Mar 2025), NeurIPS 2025 Datasets and Benchmarks)
- **Claim:** MAST taxonomy: 14 failure modes in 3 categories (system design/specification issues, inter-agent misalignment, task verification and termination), built from annotated execution traces of popular multi-agent frameworks; ChatDev used as a case study (e.g. failure mode 1.2 'disobey role specification'). Paper states it does not claim to cover every failure pattern.
- **Numbers:** 1,642 annotated traces from 7 frameworks (MAST-Data; repo README says 'over 1K'); inter-annotator Cohen's kappa 0.88 (secondary); paper Figure 1 category shares 44.2% system design, 32.3% inter-agent misalignment, 23.5% task verification (a secondary blog gives 41.77/36.94/21.30 - conflict, trust the paper); 41%-86.7% failure rates across 7 open-source MAS (secondary). A NeurIPS slide shows +9.4% for a better-designed MAS with the same model. Primary PDF not reachable from this machine.
- **For the fleet:** Largest failure classes are spec/role problems and verification/termination problems, both fixable by design: write explicit role contracts and stop/accept criteria, and log every agent's claim against evidence.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** github.com/multi-agent-systems-failure-taxonomy/MAST confirms arXiv 2503.13657 and authors Mert Cemri, Melissa Z. Pan, Shuyi Yang et al. The README says 'over 1K annotated MAS traces' (1,642 not stated). The 14 modes / 3 categories appear only as an image. NeurIPS 2025 D&B, kappa 0.88, 7 frameworks and the Figure 1 shares 44.2/32.3/23.5% are not in the README and are unverified.

### Stop Overvaluing Multi-Agent Debate (2025)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08788 (Hangfan Zhang et al., 2025 (Feb 2025), arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** Systematic evaluation of 5 multi-agent debate methods on 9 benchmarks with 4 models: debate often fails to beat chain-of-thought and self-consistency even at much higher inference cost; model heterogeneity gives consistent improvement.
- **Numbers:** v1: no debate method exceeded a 20% win rate against CoT across 36 scenarios (4 models x 9 benchmarks); gap larger against Self-Consistency at comparable call/token budgets; abstract wording softened from 'fail to' (v2) to 'often fail to' (v3).
- **For the fleet:** Do not build debate loops between critics; sample independent critiques and aggregate.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Existence NOT CONFIRMED. arXiv unreachable; a GitHub repo search returned nothing. The '5 methods, 9 benchmarks, 4 models, under 20% win rate' details are unchecked.

### Self-MoA (2025)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.00674 (Wenzhe Li et al. ('Rethinking Mixture-of-Agents: Is Mixing Different Large Language Models Beneficial?'), 2025 (Feb 2025); TMLR 2026, arXiv; TMLR 2026)
- **Claim:** Mixing different LLMs in Mixture-of-Agents often lowers average proposer quality; aggregating many samples from only the single best model (Self-MoA) does better, with a quality-diversity trade-off found over 70 proposer mixtures; a sequential Self-MoA works as well. A 2026 follow-up (2603.20324) argues the contradiction with earlier MoA results depends on the aggregation mechanism.
- **Numbers:** Self-MoA +6.6% over standard MoA on AlpacaEval 2.0; average +3.8% across MMLU, CRUX and MATH.
- **For the fleet:** Candidate quality beats diversity: sample several candidates from the best-suited model for a task rather than a mixed zoo, and put a careful selector on top.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** github.com/wenzhe-li/Self-MoA confirms the title 'Rethinking Mixture-of-Agents: Is Mixing Different Large Language Models Beneficial?', authors Wenzhe Li, Yong Lin, Mengzhou Xia, Chi Jin, arXiv 2502.00674, the diversity-quality trade-off, sequential Self-MoA, +6.6% over MoA on AlpacaEval 2.0 and +3.8% average on MMLU, CRUX and MATH. TMLR 2026 and '70 proposer mixtures' are not in the README and are unverified.

### Anthropic multi-agent research system (Jun 2025)

- **Source:** https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system (Anthropic Engineering, 2025-06-13, Anthropic engineering blog)
- **Claim:** Orchestrator-worker: Claude Opus 4 lead plans, saves its plan to memory (context beyond 200,000 tokens truncates) and spawns Sonnet 4 subagents that search in parallel; a CitationAgent attributes claims. Token usage explains most of the variance in performance. Prompts embed effort-scaling rules. Subagents write outputs to external storage and return lightweight references to avoid a 'game of telephone'. Early failure modes: 50 subagents for simple queries, endless searching, duplicated work, continuing after results were sufficient, over-long queries. Not well suited: tasks needing shared context, many inter-agent dependencies, most coding. Lead runs subagents synchronously (a slow one blocks the system). Resume from point of failure with deterministic retries/checkpoints. LLM-as-judge (single call, 0.0-1.0, rubric of accuracy, citations, completeness, source quality, tool efficiency) started with about 20 queries; human testers caught what automation missed. A tool-testing agent rewrote tool descriptions.
- **Numbers:** multi-agent (Opus 4 lead + Sonnet 4 subagents) outperformed single-agent Opus 4 by 90.2% on Anthropic's internal research eval; agents use about 4x and multi-agent systems about 15x the tokens of chat; 3 factors explain 95% of BrowseComp variance, token usage alone 80%; effort rules: 1 agent / 3-10 tool calls (simple), 2-4 subagents / 10-15 calls each (comparison), 10+ subagents (complex); parallel spawning of 3-5 subagents and 3+ parallel tool calls cut research time by up to 90%; tool-description rewrite cut task completion time by 40%.
- **For the fleet:** Lead = strongest model, workers = cheaper model; put explicit effort-scaling rules in every prompt; subagents return file references + short summaries; expect the benefit to come mostly from breadth-first read-only exploration and from spending more tokens.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system, 'How we built our multi-agent research system', Jun 13 2025, Hadfield et al. Verified: 90.2% over single-agent Opus 4 on the internal research eval, 15x tokens for multi-agent (4x for agents), 95%/80% BrowseComp variance, effort-scaling rules, up to 90% time cut, 40% task-time cut from tool-description rewrite, 200,000-token truncation, 50 subagents, 0.0-1.0 judge, about 20 queries, synchronous subagents. Wording fix: the article says domains needing shared context or with many inter-agent dependencies are 'not a good fit for multi-agent systems today' (not 'not well suited'), and that most coding tasks have fewer parallelizable parts than research.

### Cognition: Don't Build Multi-Agents (Jun 2025)

- **Source:** https://cognition.com/blog/dont-build-multi-agents (Walden Yan, Cognition (Devin), 2025-06-12 (per secondary sources), Cognition blog)
- **Claim:** Two principles per secondary summaries: (1) share context, and share full agent traces, not just individual messages; (2) actions carry implicit decisions, and conflicting decisions carry bad results. Illustrated by a Flappy Bird task split into background and bird subtasks whose outputs do not fit together (Mario-like background, bird that does not behave like the game). Recommended: a single-threaded linear agent; for long jobs, a dedicated compression model that distills history into key details instead of splitting among peers. Exact wording not verified: primary page unreachable from this machine (DNS failure) - read from search summaries only.
- **Numbers:** none
- **For the fleet:** Parallel writers must not be given vague fragments: either give them the full relevant trace/decisions or keep the write path single-threaded. Prefer one writer with a compressor over peer writers.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable

### Best-of-N verifiers on SWE-bench (2025-2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01496 (R2E-Gym (arXiv 2504.07164, 2025); DeepSWE (Together AI, 2025); Kimi-Dev (arXiv 2509.23045, 2025); SWE-HERO (arXiv 2604.01496, Apr 2026), 2025-2026, arXiv preprints / blog)
- **Claim:** Sampling many patches and selecting with an execution-based plus execution-free hybrid verifier gives large gains over pass@1, but selector precision is the bottleneck: the gap between verifier-picked Best@K and oracle Pass@K widens with K. Kimi-Dev reports parallel scaling emerging by having the model summarize differences across candidate patches and compose a new patch.
- **Numbers:** R2E-Gym (2025): Best@26 with hybrid verifier 51.0% vs 34.4% pass@1 on SWE-bench Verified with a 32B model; DeepSWE (2025): pass@1 42.2% -> 59% with hybrid test-time scaling; SWE-HERO (2026): K=32 candidates, gains up to 7.9% for smaller models and 4.5% for the 32B variant, Best@K vs Pass@K gap reaches 15% at K=16 and keeps widening.
- **For the fleet:** Best-of-N pays only if the selector is strong (tests plus an independent judge); with expensive minutes-long renders keep N small (2-3) and invest in the selector rather than more samples.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** Primary URL (SWE-HERO, arXiv 2604.01496) NOT CONFIRMED; a GitHub search for SWE-HERO returned nothing relevant. R2E-Gym (2504.07164) and DeepSWE (together.ai did not resolve) are unchecked. Only Kimi-Dev was opened (github.com/MoonshotAI/Kimi-Dev): arXiv 2509.23045 and title 'Kimi-Dev: Agentless Training as Skill Prior for SWE-Agents' are right, but the README does not mention the 'summarize differences across candidate patches and compose a new patch' parallel-scaling claim (it reports Kimi-Dev-72B 60.4% on SWE-bench Verified).

### Magentic-One (Nov 2024)

- **Source:** https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/articles/magentic-one-a-generalist-multi-agent-system-for-solving-complex-tasks/ (Microsoft Research (AI Frontiers), MSR-TR-2024-47, Nov 2024, Microsoft Research technical report / blog)
- **Claim:** Lead Orchestrator with two loops: outer loop maintains a Task Ledger (facts, educated guesses, plan); inner loop maintains a Progress Ledger (is the task complete, is progress being made, which agent acts next). When progress stalls (diagram shows 'stall count > 2') it updates the Task Ledger and re-plans. Specialist agents: WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, ComputerTerminal; agents can be added or removed without retuning. o1-preview tried for the orchestrator outer loop and Coder, GPT-4o for the others. MSR page shows only bar-chart readings, no exact scores; ablation numbers not found.
- **Numbers:** headline scores UNCONFIRMED here (search snippet only): about 38% GAIA, 32.8% WebArena, 27.7% AssistantBench, described as statistically competitive with state of the art in Oct 2024; stall threshold 2 confirmed from the MSR page.
- **For the fleet:** Give the orchestrator an explicit two-level ledger (plan/facts vs per-turn progress) and a stall counter that forces re-plan or escalation after 2 non-improving rounds.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened the MSR page; published November 4, 2024. Verified: Task Ledger (facts, guesses, plan) outer loop, Progress Ledger inner loop, 'Stall count > 2' (appears only in the diagram), WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, ComputerTerminal. Models: GPT-4o is the default; o1-preview for the Orchestrator's outer loop and Coder was an experimental configuration. The page gives no numeric scores (GAIA, WebArena, AssistantBench stay unconfirmed, as the report says) and does not give 'MSR-TR-2024-47'.

- **Claim:** Layered architecture: each layer has several LLM agents, each sees the previous layer's outputs as extra context; all-open-source MoA beats GPT-4 Omni on AlpacaEval 2.0 and reports state of the art on MT-Bench and FLASK (ICLR version adds Arena-Hard).

- **Claim:** Workflows (predefined code paths) vs agents (LLM directs its own process). - **For the fleet:** Keep the render->critique->edit loop as a fixed code-defined workflow with agents only inside steps; route cheap steps to small models.
