### Claude Code docs: subagents, teams, worktrees, workflows (2026)

- **Source:** https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-teams (Anthropic (Claude Code documentation; also /sub-agents, /worktrees, /workflows, /costs, /agents pages), 2026 (current docs, Claude Code v2.1.2xx), Product documentation)
- **Claim:** Agent teams are experimental, disabled by default; for sequential tasks, same-file edits or many dependencies 'a single session or subagents are more effective'; two teammates editing one file overwrite each other, so give each a disjoint file set; start with 3-5 teammates and 5-6 tasks per teammate ('three focused teammates often outperform five scattered ones'); start with research and review; teammates do not inherit the lead's conversation history (spawn prompt must be self-contained); no nested teams; hooks TeammateIdle/TaskCreated/TaskCompleted can enforce quality gates (exit code 2 rejects). Subagents: built-in read-only Explore and Plan; frontmatter model, tools, isolation: worktree, maxTurns, effort; subagents can nest up to 3 layers; 20 concurrent subagents by default; subagent worktrees branch from the repo default branch unless worktree.baseRef is set to 'head' (important for building on unpushed work). Dynamic workflows: a script (not the model) holds the plan, intermediate results stay in script variables; default 16 concurrent agents (1-256), 1,000 agents per run, 'Large workflow' warning above 25 agents or 1.5M projected tokens; model per stage can be set; example pattern: loop fixing until a check passes or two rounds in a row make no progress. Costs: agent teams use approximately 7x more tokens than standard sessions when teammates run in plan mode; docs advise Sonnet for teammates and haiku for simple subagents; /batch splits a change into 5 to 30 worktree-isolated subagents.
- **Numbers:** 7x tokens (teams in plan mode); 3-5 teammates; 5-6 tasks per teammate; 20 concurrent subagents; 3 nesting layers; 16 concurrent workflow agents; 1,000 agents/run; warning at more than 25 agents or 1.5M tokens.
- **For the fleet:** The fleet can be built from native pieces: orchestrator session, read-only Explore-style subagents on a cheap model, worktree-isolated candidate subagents, a workflow script for the fixed loop, and hooks as hard gates. Set worktree.baseRef='head' (or branch from the integration branch) so candidates start from the current verified state.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Opened the agent-teams, sub-agents, worktrees, workflows, costs and commands pages on code.claude.com. Verified: teams experimental and off by default; 'a single session or subagents are more effective' for sequential or same-file work; same-file edits overwrite; start with 3-5 teammates, 5-6 tasks each; 'three focused teammates often outperform five scattered ones'; lead's history does not carry over; no nested teams; TeammateIdle/TaskCreated/TaskCompleted hooks, exit code 2; built-in read-only Explore and Plan; frontmatter model/tools/isolation/maxTurns/effort; three nesting layers; 20 concurrent subagents; worktree.baseRef 'fresh' (default) or 'head'; workflows run a script with results in script variables, 16 concurrent agents (1-256), 1,000 per run, 'Large workflow' warning above 25 agents or 1.5M projected tokens, per-stage model, 'two rounds in a row make no progress' example; about 7x tokens for teams in plan mode, Sonnet for teammates, haiku for simple subagents; /batch splits into 5 to 30 independent units, each in its own worktree. Extra hazard: when a subagent definition is used as a teammate, its 'skills' frontmatter is NOT applied (teammates load skills from project and user settings).

### Design rules proposed by the researcher

- TOPOLOGY - One central orchestrator (strongest model) with short-lived subagents; no peer-to-peer messaging, no flat swarms, no leaderless lock-file coordination, no integrator agent. All coordination goes through files the orchestrator owns (JSON experiment ledger + progress file + git). Rests on: Anthropic multi-agent coordination patterns (Apr 2026); Google/MIT Science of Scaling Agent Systems (Dec 2025) (centralized limits error amplification, 4.4x vs 17.2x for independent, with the metric caveat); CooperBench (arXiv 2601.13295, Jan 2026) (peer coop about 25%, roughly half of solo); Cursor scaling agents (Jan 2026); Cognition: Multi-Agents: What's Actually Working (Apr 2026); Anthropic C compiler with parallel Claudes (Feb 2026) (leaderless swarm worked only with a near-perfect test oracle, which we do not have for realism).
- PLAN LOOP - Keep the fixed render -> check -> critique -> edit -> re-render loop as a code-defined workflow (a workflow script or a deterministic driver) and let agents act only inside steps; give the orchestrator a two-level ledger (plan/facts vs per-round progress) and a stall counter that forces re-plan or escalation after 2 non-improving rounds. Rests on: Anthropic building effective agents (Dec 2024); Claude Code docs: subagents, teams, worktrees, workflows (2026); Magentic-One (Nov 2024); Anthropic multi-agent coordination patterns (Apr 2026).
- PARALLELIZE (read-only) - Fan out cheap, read-only explorers (code reading, render-log/warning triage, reference-photo and material research, static realism audit of a file) because breadth-first read-only work is where multi-agent clearly wins and nothing can conflict; each returns a 1-2k-token structured summary with file:line pointers, writing bulk output to files. Rests on: Anthropic multi-agent research system (Jun 2025) (90.2% gain, 4x/15x tokens, subagents return references); Anthropic effective context engineering (Sep 2025); Anthropic when to use multi-agent systems (Jan 2026) (context protection when a subtask emits more than about 1,000 irrelevant tokens).
- PARALLELIZE (independent candidates) - Generate 2-3 candidate edits per round, each testing a different hypothesis on a disjoint knob (sun/sky calibration, one material family, camera/ISP) in its own git worktree off the current verified integration commit; do not run the same vague task N times. Rests on: CAID asynchronous SWE agents (arXiv 2603.21489, Mar 2026) (manager + dependency graph + worktrees + tests + merge: +26.7 PaperBench, +14.3 Commit0); Co-Coder cohesion-aware partitioning (arXiv 2606.00953, May 2026); Best-of-N verifiers on SWE-bench (2025-2026); Scaling test-time compute for agentic coding (arXiv 2604.16529, Apr 2026); Claude Code docs: subagents, teams, worktrees, workflows (2026) (set worktree.baseRef to 'head').
- KEEP SINGLE-THREADED - (a) the GPU render queue: exactly one renderer role or a lock, FIFO, fixed render settings; (b) merges to main, one candidate at a time, each followed by a re-render and regression check; (c) any chain where step N needs the result of step N-1; (d) edits to hub or tightly coupled files (rtx_look.py, drone_look.py, scene.py, quadcopter_pbr.py) which have a single writer per round; (e) default to sequential for short bounded tasks. Rests on: Google/MIT Science of Scaling Agent Systems (Dec 2025) (sequential tasks degrade 39-70%); Claim Plane pre-write admission (arXiv 2608.00947, Aug 2026) (reliability gain came from serializing 96.7% of runs); Parallel sub-agents in coding agents (arXiv 2610.10263, Oct 2026) (1.41-3.31x tokens, little or negative gain on bounded tasks); Co-Coder cohesion-aware partitioning (arXiv 2606.00953, May 2026); Anthropic infrastructure noise in agentic evals (Feb 2026).
- NUMBER OF AGENTS - Start at 1 orchestrator + at most 3 concurrent workers (hard ceiling 4); more render-bound candidates than the render queue can serve just waits. Scale up only after a pilot shows the extra worker raises verified-improvement yield per dollar. Simple lookups use 1 agent; comparisons 2-4; broad audits more only if read-only. Rests on: Claude Code docs: subagents, teams, worktrees, workflows (2026) (start with 3-5; 5-6 tasks each); Teams of coding agents inference-time compute (arXiv 2610.17471, Oct 2026) (implementer/verifier gains level off beyond four workers); CooperBench (arXiv 2601.13295, Jan 2026) (success declines from 2 to 4 peers); Anthropic multi-agent research system (Jun 2025) (effort-scaling rules; 50 subagents for a simple query was a failure mode).
- WRITERS - Single writer to main: only the orchestrator merges. Many writers are allowed only in isolated worktrees and only after declaring the files/settings they will touch (claim); overlapping claims run serially; each candidate rebases on current main and is re-verified before merge; stale-view check before merge. Expect double-digit conflict rates if agents touch the same files. Rests on: Cognition: Don't Build Multi-Agents (Jun 2025); Cognition: Multi-Agents: What's Actually Working (Apr 2026); Claim Plane pre-write admission (arXiv 2608.00947, Aug 2026); STORM state management (arXiv 2605.20563, May 2026) (worktree baseline 63.8 macro was below single-agent 66.4); Merge conflicts in agent PRs (2026) (27.67%, 19.8% rates); Anthropic Scion agent-scale source control (Oct 2026).
- HANDOFF FORMAT - Spawn prompts must be self-contained (workers and teammates do not inherit the lead's history): goal, acceptance criteria, exact files and line pointers, explicit constraints as typed fields (files allowed, settings frozen, e.g. sun calibration), prior failed attempts as a structured summary (hypothesis, change, render result, reason failed), and the output contract (patch path, render id, JSON verdict). Pass file references, not pasted blobs; keep status in a JSON ledger with permitted-field updates (JSON resists accidental overwrite better than Markdown) plus a progress file and one-hypothesis-per-commit git history. Rests on: Cognition: Don't Build Multi-Agents (Jun 2025); Facts Without Rules handoffs (arXiv 2608.29028, Aug 2026) (explicit constraints cut leakage to under 15%); Attention Tax, Handoff Tax (arXiv 2610.06069, Oct 2026); Anthropic effective harnesses for long-running agents (Nov 2025); Anthropic multi-agent research system (Jun 2025); Scaling test-time compute for agentic coding (arXiv 2604.16529, Apr 2026); Claude Code docs: subagents, teams, worktrees, workflows (2026).
- VERIFY CODE FIRST - Deterministic gates run before any GPU time and are enforced by hooks, not by agent judgment: python3.11 -m py_compile, the plain-unittest suites, grep of the render log for the known risk lines (HdrColor missing, scene-radiance vs 18% grey card, rtx_look unregistered-setting warnings, tracebacks). A failed gate means no render and no merge. Verification output stays short (details in log files). Rests on: Anthropic C compiler with parallel Claudes (Feb 2026); Anthropic demystifying evals (Jan 2026) (prefer deterministic graders); Anthropic harness design for long-running apps (Mar 2026) (hard thresholds per criterion); Claude Code docs: subagents, teams, worktrees, workflows (2026) (hooks can reject completion).
- VERIFY IMAGES - Judge with a fresh-context critic that is never the agent that made the edit, ideally a different model family or tier; give it a per-dimension rubric with an 'unsure' option, calibrated with few-shot examples taken from the owner's own judgments, and require it to name concrete defects and run negative tests (find the strongest artifacts) so it cannot rubber-stamp. Compare baseline vs candidate pairwise in BOTH orders, repeat, and accept only order-consistent verdicts; aggregate several independent critics by vote, never by debate; supplement with measured image statistics (exposure/histogram, mean radiance) where they exist. Rests on: Anthropic harness design for long-running apps (Mar 2026); Anthropic when to use multi-agent systems (Jan 2026); Anthropic multi-agent coordination patterns (Apr 2026); Humanize builder/reviewer (arXiv 2610.08900, Oct 2026); EditJudgeBias MLLM judges (arXiv 2610.01670, Oct 2026) (order swap reverses up to 60.9%); MLLM-as-a-Judge (ICML 2024); Debate or Vote (NeurIPS 2025); Stop Overvaluing Multi-Agent Debate (2025); Selection bottleneck (arXiv 2603.20324, Mar 2026).
- ACCEPTANCE AND NOISE - Keep an edit only if it beats the baseline by more than measured noise: first re-render the baseline twice with fixed seed, frames, resolution scale, warm-up and time of day, and record the spread; treat differences under about 3 points on any score as unverified; re-render the winner once more for consistency before merging (pass^k mindset); maintain a regression set of defects already fixed and re-check it after every merge. Rests on: Anthropic infrastructure noise in agentic evals (Feb 2026); Anthropic demystifying evals (Jan 2026); Anthropic C compiler with parallel Claudes (Feb 2026) (new commits broke old features).
- TERMINATION - Every loop has hard caps on rounds, renders, dollars and wall-clock (set before the run) and stops on any of: two consecutive rounds with no verified improvement, rubric thresholds met, budget exhausted; the independent reviewer (not the builder) declares done, and the orchestrator enforces the caps and logs the stop reason. Agents may not edit the rubric, tests, baselines or ledger schema. Rests on: Anthropic multi-agent coordination patterns (Apr 2026) (max-iteration limit and fallback, first-class termination conditions); MAST Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail (2025) (verification/termination is a top failure category, about 23.5% in the paper's Figure 1); Humanize builder/reviewer (arXiv 2610.08900, Oct 2026) (stopping remains a weakness); Anthropic effective harnesses for long-running agents (Nov 2025) (premature victory; 'unacceptable to remove or edit tests'); Anthropic harness design for long-running apps (Mar 2026) (scores plateau after 5-15 iterations).
- MODEL TIERING - Strongest model only for: orchestrator planning/synthesis, calibrating the image-critic rubric, adjudicating split or ambiguous visual verdicts, and genuinely hard design decisions. Mid tier (Sonnet) for scoped code-edit workers and routine critics. Cheapest tier (Haiku) for read-only exploration, log/warning triage and lint-like checks. Let cheap workers escalate through a capped advisor-style consult to the strongest model (max_uses) instead of running the strongest model everywhere, and log escalation spend separately. Caveats: I found no 2025-2026 evidence on which tier is needed for judging rendered realism, and sources disagree on whether cheap models are better planners or implementers, so validate on our own probe set. Rests on: Anthropic advisor strategy (Apr 2026) (+2.7 points, -11.9% cost; Haiku 19.7% -> 41.2%); Anthropic multi-agent research system (Jun 2025) (Opus lead, Sonnet workers); Teams of coding agents inference-time compute (arXiv 2610.17471, Oct 2026) (mixed team within 1.4 points at about 70% lower cost); Cognition: Multi-Agents: What's Actually Working (Apr 2026) (Smart Friend); Claude Code docs: subagents, teams, worktrees, workflows (2026).
- COST AND JUSTIFICATION - Budget multi-agent at roughly 3-10x single-agent tokens (about 15x for broad research, about 7x for teams in plan mode, 1.4-3.3x for dynamic concurrency); remember token volume itself explains much of the gain, so compare against a single agent given the same total token budget on the same probe tasks before keeping any role. Justify each agent by one of: context protection, parallel breadth, independent judging, or tool/domain specialization; split by context, not by problem phase; prefer a loadable skill to an extra role-agent when the context is shared, and keep the skill library small and non-overlapping. Remove components that are not load-bearing. Rests on: Anthropic when to use multi-agent systems (Jan 2026); Anthropic multi-agent research system (Jun 2025); Single vs multi-agent at equal thinking tokens (arXiv 2604.02460, Apr 2026); Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage (arXiv 2606.13003, Jun 2026); Do More Agents Help? (arXiv 2606.05670, Jun 2026); Single-agent baselines (arXiv 2601.12307, 2601.04748, Jan 2026); Anthropic harness design for long-running apps (Mar 2026) (20x cost for solo vs full harness; components pruned one at a time).
- DESIGN FOR KNOWN FAILURE MODES - Write explicit role and spec files (largest MAST category), forbid agents from marking work done without evidence attached (render id + metrics + critic verdict), keep status fields in JSON, do one feature/hypothesis per commit, instruct subagents on what state they do NOT share with the orchestrator, and keep the lead from doing workers' jobs. Use /batch-style or workflow fan-out only for read-only audits. Rests on: MAST Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail (2025); Anthropic effective harnesses for long-running agents (Nov 2025); Cognition: Multi-Agents: What's Actually Working (Apr 2026); Claude Code docs: subagents, teams, worktrees, workflows (2026).

### Rules the fact-checker says to drop or fix

- TOPOLOGY: Drop the '4.4x vs 17.2x error amplification' support (not in the Google/MIT README; the report says the later revision dropped it; cite the Nature Machine Intelligence 2026 version if cited at all). Drop or label as anecdotal the Cursor and Cognition citations and the 'no integrator agent' clause, which rests only on the unreachable Cursor post. The rule still stands on the verified Anthropic coordination-patterns post, CooperBench (25% vs about half of solo) and the C compiler post.
- PARALLELIZE (independent candidates): Remove Co-Coder (2606.00953), 2604.16529 and SWE-HERO as supporting evidence (all unverifiable). Mark CAID's '+26.7 PaperBench / +14.3 Commit0' as unverified (the paper's real title is 'Effective Strategies for Asynchronous Software Engineering Agents'). Keep the mechanics, which rest on the verified Claude Code docs (isolation: worktree, worktree.baseRef 'head').
- KEEP SINGLE-THREADED: Drop Claim Plane (2608.00947, '96.7% serialized'), Co-Coder, and the Google/MIT '39-70%' sequential degradation figure (v1 only, unverified). The infrastructure-noise post does not support single-threading; it supports fixed resource settings. Re-base the rule on the Claude Code docs ('same-file edits... a single session or subagents are more effective'), the Anthropic when-to-use post, and the owner's one-GPU constraint. If cited, state that Parallel sub-agents (2610.10263) is known only through auto-generated third-party notes.
- NUMBER OF AGENTS: Drop 'gains level off beyond four workers' (arXiv 2610.17471, unconfirmed) and CooperBench's 'success declines from 2 to 4 agents' (not in the README). The '1 orchestrator + at most 3 workers' default can rest on the Claude Code docs' 3-5 teammates guidance and the research-system post's effort-scaling rules.
- WRITERS: Remove STORM (2605.20563) numbers such as '63.8 below 66.4', Claim Plane, and the Cognition posts as evidence. Correct the AgenticFlict figure: 27.67% is of the 107,026 simulated PRs, not of 142K. Drop the 19.8% (2607.04697) and the SBES '60x' figures. Do not cite Scion for serialized merges: the article says it could not replicate a global merge lock and relies on post-merge testing and conflict-tolerant, lock-free commits.
- HANDOFF FORMAT: Drop 'explicit constraints cut leakage to under 15%' (Facts Without Rules, unverifiable), Attention Tax / Handoff Tax, Cognition Don't Build Multi-Agents, and 2604.16529. Keep the rule on the verified support: Claude Code docs (teammates do not inherit the lead's history), effective harnesses (JSON vs Markdown), the research-system post (references instead of blobs).
- VERIFY IMAGES: Do not quote '60.9% of decisions reversed' (EditJudgeBias 2610.01670, unconfirmed). The 'different model family for the judge' advice rests only on Humanize (unconfirmed), so mark it a preference, not evidence. 'Vote, never debate' rests on Debate or Vote (the README confirms the paper but not the claim) and on two unverifiable papers (Stop Overvaluing MAD, Should we be going MAD). It also sits awkwardly with the Claude Code docs, which recommend adversarial 'competing hypotheses' teammates for debugging. MLLM-as-a-Judge findings were not checked (only venue and existence). The both-orders and rubric-with-'unsure' requirements are still sound: they rest on the verified Anthropic harness-design, evals and when-to-use posts.
- ACCEPTANCE AND NOISE: Fix the hard-coded 'about 3 points' threshold. In the infrastructure-noise post it applies to Terminal-Bench/SWE-bench leaderboard comparisons, not to render scores. Replace it with 'the spread measured from baseline-vs-baseline renders'.
- TERMINATION: Drop 'about 23.5% in Figure 1' (MAST shares unverified) and Humanize 'stopping remains a weakness' (unconfirmed). Do not state that scores 'plateau after 5-15 iterations' without rechecking: the harness post confirms '5 to 15 iterations' and runs up to four hours, but I did not confirm the plateau wording. The max-iteration/fallback and termination-condition requirements stay (verified in the coordination-patterns post).
- MODEL TIERING: Remove 2610.17471 ('mixed team within 1.4 points at about 70% lower cost') and Cognition 'Smart Friend' as evidence (unconfirmed). Keep the advisor-strategy numbers (+2.7 pp, -11.9% cost, Haiku 19.7% -> 41.2%), the research-system Opus-lead/Sonnet-workers pattern and the Claude Code docs guidance (Sonnet for teammates, haiku for simple subagents).
- COST AND JUSTIFICATION: Keep 3-10x (when-to-use), about 15x (research system), about 7x teams in plan mode (costs page) and over 20x (harness post). Treat '1.4-3.3x' as coming from an unopened paper and unconfirmed authorship. Drop Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage, Do More Agents Help, Single vs multi-agent at equal thinking tokens, and Single-agent baselines as evidence until opened (existence of 2606.13003, 2606.05670, 2604.02460, 2601.12307 and 2601.04748 not confirmed). Token volume explaining much of the gain is supported by the verified research-system post (80% of BrowseComp variance).
- DESIGN FOR KNOWN FAILURE MODES: Soften 'largest MAST category' (the README does not give category shares; 44.2/32.3/23.5% is unverified). Drop the Cognition 'What's Actually Working' support. Keep the effective-harnesses and Claude Code docs support.
- PLAN LOOP: Fine overall. Note the 'stall count > 2' threshold comes only from a diagram in the Magentic-One post, so present it as an illustrative default, not a measured one.
- Cross-cutting: the 'Teams of coding agents', Humanize, Attention Tax, EditJudgeBias, Facts Without Rules, Claim Plane, Do More Agents Help, Co-Coder, STORM, 2604.02460, 2604.16529, Selection bottleneck, Stop Overvaluing MAD and Should we be going MAD entries are all unverifiable from this machine. Every rule should stay supportable by its verified sources (Anthropic posts, Claude Code docs, CooperBench, AgenticFlict, Self-MoA, MoA, MASEval) after those citations are removed. Rules that lose all their support: 'hard ceiling of four workers from literature' and 'use a different model family for the judge'.

### Open questions

- Primary sources I could not open from this machine (DNS failures for arxiv.org, research.google, cognition.ai/cognition.com, cursor.com, openreview.net, huggingface.co, x.com and others): the Google/MIT scaling paper, MAST, Cognition's two posts, Cursor's post, Magentic-One's scores, and all 2026 arXiv papers. Their numbers come from search-result summaries and GitHub READMEs and are flagged confirmed_from_primary_source=false; re-check against the papers before quoting.
- The Google/MIT scaling paper has conflicting versions (180 configs / 4 benchmarks / R2 about 0.51-0.52 / 17.2x vs 4.4x in v1; 260 configs / 6 benchmarks / R2 0.373 later) and the 17.2x/4.4x figure measures extra work from coordination failures, not answer error: which version and metric should we cite?
- MAST category shares differ between the paper's Figure 1 (44.2 / 32.3 / 23.5%) and a secondary blog (41.77 / 36.94 / 21.30%); Magentic-One headline scores and ablations were not confirmed.
- No 2025-2026 study found that tests MLLM/VLM judges on realism of rendered or simulated imagery (only edit-judging bias, 2024 MLLM-as-a-Judge, and generic judge-validity work). Which model tier and which prompting protocol best judges our probe frames is untested and needs a calibration set built from the owner's own good/bad examples.
- Evidence on tiering is mixed: Anthropic and Cursor/Decoder-style reports favour a strong planner with cheaper executors and an advisor escalation path, while arXiv 2610.17471 (abstract only) says cheap models work as planners but not implementers. Needs a pilot on our own tasks.
- Optimal number of concurrent candidates when renders are serialized (minutes each, one GPU) is unknown; the literature assumes parallel compute. Is it better to spend render minutes on N candidates once each or on repeated renders of fewer candidates for noise control?
- Are Claude Code agent teams (experimental, disabled by default, no resumption, no nested teams) worth using versus subagents + worktrees + a workflow script? One 2026 paper (Co-Coder) reports Agent Teams had the lowest pass rate (54.1%), but only as seen in a search summary.
- CAID's ablation versus multi-agent WITHOUT isolation, the exact Commit0/PaperBench tables, and the 2610.10263 per-agent results (the Kimi Code penalty appears only in an auto-generated third-party critique) are unverified.
- Cursor scale figures conflict across secondary sources (agents, commits, lines of code) and the browser's working state is contested; treat as anecdotal.
- Several 2026 arXiv IDs (2607.*, 2608.*, 2610.*) surfaced only via search snippets; I could not independently verify they exist as described. Du et al. (2023) multi-agent debate and the original Mixture-of-Agents ablations were not retrieved.

### Fact-checker summary

I checked all 45 findings. 15 are fully supported, 10 are partly supported, and 20 are unverifiable. No files were changed and nothing was pushed.

What I could and could not open:
- arxiv.org, huggingface.co, openreview.net, semanticscholar, cognition.com, cursor.com, cohere.com, mdpi.com, proceedings.mlr.press, research.google, together.ai, neurips.cc and simonwillison.net all failed DNS (ENOTFOUND). I did not use mirrors or archives to get around that.
- The WebSearch budget for this turn was already used up, so I could not search by title.
- anthropic.com, claude.com, code.claude.com, microsoft.com and github.com were readable. GitHub gave READMEs and an auto-generated third-party notes repo. A few GitHub fetches returned 404 or 429.
- In the entries, source_exists=false means 'not confirmed because I could not open it', not 'proven fake'.

Fully supported against the primary source: the Anthropic and Claude Code pages (coordination patterns, advisor, harness design, C compiler, infra noise, when-to-use, evals, effective harnesses, context engineering, research system, building effective agents, Scion), Magentic-One and the Claude Code docs. Self-MoA and Mixture-of-Agents are also supported, apart from unverified venues. Corrections found:
- Scion: the bottleneck was git, builds and CI, not a global merge lock, and the article does not say to serialize merges.
- C compiler: one quote is a paraphrase of what the article says.
- Harness post: '27 criteria' is a single sprint's count, not a maximum.
- 2610.10263: the real title is 'When Sub-Agents Work in Parallel...', not the report's label.
- 2610.10263: the 354 tasks, 2,124 executions, $20.8K, 1.41-3.31x tokens and Kimi 59.0 vs 77.0 match auto-generated GitHub notes, not the paper.
- 2610.10263: Claude Code's penalty is the strongest, and its 59.0 figure conflicts with Kimi's 59.0 across the two notes.
- AgenticFlict: 27.67% is of the 107,026 simulated PRs, not of 142K.
- Google/MIT: it was published in Nature Machine Intelligence 2026 under a different title, and 17.2x/4.4x, 87% and 45% are not in its README.
- CooperBench: the '2 to 4 agents decline' claim is not in the README.
- MAST: the README says 'over 1K' traces, not 1,642.
- CAID: its results numbers are not in the README.

Every 2026 arXiv paper from Aug-Oct 2026 and most of May-Jun 2026 is unverifiable here. The rules that cite them need the fixes listed in rules_to_drop_or_fix. Most rules survive on their Anthropic and Claude Code docs support. The 'hard ceiling of four workers' and 'different model family for the judge' rules lose all their support.

The docs also say teammates do not apply a subagent definition's 'skills' frontmatter, which matters for the planned fleet.

## graphics-agents

### BlenderGym (CVPR 2025 Highlight): benchmark and generator/verifier compute study

- **Source:** https://openaccess.thecvf.com/content/CVPR2025/html/Gu_BlenderGym_Benchmarking_Foundational_Model_Systems_for_Graphics_Editing_CVPR_2025_paper.html (Yunqi Gu, Ian Huang, Jihyeon Je, Guandao Yang, Leonidas Guibas (Stanford), 2025-04 (arXiv 2504.01786); code released 2025-04-11, CVPR 2025 (Highlight))
- **Claim:** Benchmark of 245 handcrafted start/goal Blender scene pairs in 5 tasks (object placement, lighting, procedural material, blend shape, procedural geometry), scored with image/3D metrics (photometric loss, negative CLIP, Chamfer) instead of a judge. Uses a BlenderAlchemy-style generator -> verifier pipeline. Findings: (1) all VLM systems are far behind human Blender users, and even strong closed VLMs fail to set lighting colours accurately; (2) the VERIFIER also benefits from inference scaling (a 'reselection' parameter k reruns verification with shuffled candidates and keeps the winner), with diminishing returns; (3) 'VeriRatio' = share of total compute spent on verification; the best split depends on the budget (more total compute -> a higher verification share pays off more) and on the task (the gain from verification is smaller for lighting than for blend shape because verifying subtle, continuous changes is harder); (4) scaled InternVL2-8B beat unscaled or lightly scaled GPT-4o and Claude 3.5 Sonnet on all three metrics. Generation and verification queries were treated as equal cost. [Repo README read directly: Highlight status, release date, 19 supported models. Everything else is from WebSearch summaries of the paper and leaderboard; I could not open the paper itself.]
- **Numbers:** 245 pairs; lighting = 40 instances; human baseline 10 Blender users on ~20% of instances, 8 min cap each (secondary). Leaderboard, lower is better, photometric loss / N-CLIP: LIGHTING GPT-4o 2.410/2.398, Claude 3.5 Sonnet 2.897/4.049, Human 1.239/1.632; MATERIAL GPT-4o 3.653/8.942, Claude 3.5 Sonnet 5.769/11.44, Human 0.629/3.043. VeriRatio values reported as 0.33, 0.62, 0.73 (secondary, unverified). Models used were 2024-era.
- **For the fleet:** Our critic is the scarce resource, not the proposer. Because a critic call costs seconds and a render costs minutes, spend extra budget on repeated, order-swapped critic calls per render (verifier scaling) rather than on more blind candidates. Lighting/exposure is the hardest thing for a VLM to verify, so pin it with numeric measurements (mean scene radiance vs 18% grey card, patch colours) instead of VLM opinion. Cheap models plus many verification calls can beat one call from a stronger model (2024 evidence, re-test on our own critic).
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### BlenderAlchemy (ECCV 2024): edit generator + pairwise tournament state evaluator

- **Source:** https://www.ecva.net/papers/eccv_2024/papers_ECCV/papers/12578.pdf (Ian Huang, Guandao Yang, Leonidas Guibas (Stanford), 2024-04 (arXiv 2404.17672), ECCV 2024)
- **Claim:** A VLM (GPT-4V) 'edit generator' proposes several candidate edits to the current Blender program per iteration (Python edits to materials, geometry, lighting); a VLM 'state evaluator' picks the best render by PAIRWISE comparison applied recursively as a tournament, so choosing among b candidates takes O(log b) queries. Refinement is parameterised by depth d and breadth b. A 'visual imagination' step uses a text-to-image model to create a reference image (DALL-E 3 per one secondary source; unconfirmed) to ground abstract text. An ablation reports that removing vision hurts. [Official repo README read directly: per-iteration winner kept, best_of.png output, thought_process JSON per iteration, GPT-4V recommended, Claude/Gemini/Ollama supported; the README does NOT define breadth/depth/tournament or say if the incumbent competes in the tournament. Paper numbers are from search summaries.]
- **Numbers:** Text-based material editing: preferred over Paint3D 73% and over TEXTure 56% of the time; humans agreed with the evaluator's pick in 71% of comparisons (all secondary, unverified). Depth/breadth defaults: unconfirmed (output folder names like tune_d4_b8 hint at d=4, b=8 but the README does not define them).
- **For the fleet:** Template for our inner loop: several cheap candidate edits -> pairwise tournament -> keep winner. Pairwise A/B against the current best is cheaper and more reliable than absolute scoring. Whether the incumbent must be in the bracket is unconfirmed, so make it mandatory in our design (that is the regression guard).
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### SceneCraft (ICML 2024 oral): scene graph -> Blender code -> VLM review, with skill-library learning

- **Source:** https://proceedings.mlr.press/v235/hu24g.html (Ziniu Hu, Ahmet Iscen, Aashi Jain, Thomas Kipf, Yisong Yue, David Ross, Cordelia Schmid, Alireza Fathi (Google DeepMind / Caltech), 2024-03 (arXiv 2403.01248), ICML 2024 (oral), PMLR 235)
- **Claim:** An LLM writes a scene graph, converts spatial relations to numeric constraints in a Python/Blender script, renders, and a multimodal LLM reviewer inspects renders to find unsatisfied constraints and fix the script (textual feedback, not pairwise, not a scalar). A dual loop adds library learning: an inner loop refines each scene; an outer loop reviews the function updates, picks the one that reflects consensus, and merges it into a global skill library, improving results without fine-tuning the LLM. Example from the paper: the 'parallel' constraint first checked only location; VLM feedback showed orientation also had to be checked, and the improved function entered the library. Handles scenes with up to about 100 assets. [From WebSearch summaries of the paper and PMLR page; not opened directly.]
- **Numbers:** Constraint passing score 88.9 vs 5.6 for BlenderGPT; CLIP-score gains over BlenderGPT of >45.1% and 40.9%; 40 synthetic queries with ground-truth constraints (all secondary). Number of feedback rounds and library-learning ablation numbers: unconfirmed.
- **For the fleet:** Keep a persistent library of accepted, tested helper functions and material recipes (consensus-merged, not auto-appended), so later rounds start from verified pieces. Encode 'checkable' properties as numeric constraints (like SceneCraft's constraint scorers) so the VLM only handles what numbers cannot.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### LL3M (2025): multi-agent Blender code generation with render-based critic and verification

- **Source:** https://github.com/threedle/ll3m (threedle group, University of Chicago (Sun, Chen et al.), 2025-08-11 (arXiv 2508.08228), arXiv 2025)
- **Claim:** Specialised agents plan, retrieve, write, debug and refine Blender Python. Per secondary summaries there are six agents (planner, retrieval, coding, critic, verification, user feedback); the critic renders from several viewpoints and flags visual problems, the verification agent checks the coding agent actually applied the fixes, and an auto-refinement phase repeats until quality is met, followed by a user-guided phase. BlenderRAG = retrieval index over Blender 4.4 API docs (about 1,729 files per one secondary source). [README read directly: agent roles in one line, 'changes made during refinement can't be reverted', results vary run to run from LLM randomness, config controls headless/GPU rendering, images rendered per instruction and resolution scale, 5 requests/day, hosted server shut down because the paper's model Claude Sonnet 3.7 was retired.]
- **Numbers:** About 59% of the paper's user-edit examples needed a single edit instruction, the rest 3 to 4 follow-ups (search summary of the paper). BlenderRAG '26% fewer errors, 5x more complex operations' is a third-party claim, unverified. No user-study scores confirmed.
- **For the fleet:** Separate roles: critic (finds problems) vs verifier (confirms the edit was applied and nothing else moved) is a cheap two-step check we should copy. The 'cannot revert' limitation is the exact failure we avoid by using git worktrees and incumbent-vs-candidate acceptance. RAG over renderer/USD docs helps code correctness for the proposer.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### Make-it-Real (NeurIPS 2024): GPT-4V material library matching, one-shot

- **Source:** https://github.com/Aleafy/Make_it_Real (Ye Fang, Zeyi Sun, Tong Wu, Jiaqi Wang, Ziwei Liu, Gordon Wetzstein, Dahua Lin, 2024-04 (arXiv 2404.16829), NeurIPS 2024)
- **Claim:** GPT-4V annotates a material library once, then for each segmented part of an albedo-only object it matches a library material using visual prompts and hierarchical (category tree) text prompts, and generates SVBRDF maps from the matched reference and the original albedo. It is a one-shot assignment pipeline; I found no render-critique-revise loop. [README read directly: pipeline = texture segmentation, material identification, matching; library is 'thousands of materials' with 'over 80 subcategories'; results rendered in Blender to output/refine_output vs output/ori_output; README reports no evaluation numbers.]
- **Numbers:** Library about 1,400 MatSynth PBR materials and user preference 77% to 84% for refined assets come from a secondary summary only and are unconfirmed. README: none.
- **For the fleet:** For land-cover to material mapping (grass, asphalt, roofing, soil), a described and tree-indexed material library lets the VLM pick by hierarchy instead of free-form invention, keeping the search space small and the result physically plausible. It still needs our own render-and-compare step on top.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### VLMaterial (ICLR 2025 spotlight): fine-tuned VLM writes Blender shader programs, verify, then MCMC

- **Source:** https://github.com/mit-gfx/VLMaterial (Beichen Li et al. (MIT), 2025-01 (arXiv 2501.18623), ICLR 2025 (spotlight))
- **Claim:** Blender procedural materials are converted to Python programs; a LLaVA-NeXT (LLaMA-3 8B) VLM is fine-tuned to write the program from an image. Dataset: about 1.6K artist materials augmented to >550K examples with GPT-4o-mini (genetic crossover of graphs plus parameter perturbation). Inference = sample candidate programs, render and verify them, then optional gradient-free MCMC on node parameters. [README read directly: two-step inference (generate candidates, render and verify), param_search.py MCMC, EEVEE needs an X11/OpenGL display, 48 GB VRAM recommended for training, MIT code, CC BY-NC dataset, archival repo.]
- **Numbers:** MCMC: 200 iterations, accept a worse sample with probability 0.05, perturb 10% of node parameters per step (quoted from the paper's appendix via search snippet). Style loss 0.019 (Blender), 0.026 (Substance), 0.025 (real images) under an N=50, K=20 sampling protocol (third-party review, unverified).
- **For the fleet:** Two-stage pattern that fits a render-limited loop: broad cheap sampling of material programs with render-and-verify, then a cheap local parameter search. Note MCMC accepts worse samples with probability 0.05, so it is not monotone; keep the best-so-far explicitly.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### MultiMat (ICLR 2026): multimodal program synthesis for procedural materials with tree search

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.22151 (Belouadi et al., 2025-09 (arXiv 2509.22151), ICLR 2026)
- **Claim:** Renders intermediate node states and feeds them back into the multimodal model's context, so each generated node can be checked as it is produced; a tree search backtracks on invalid states. Trained on 6,878 production Substance Designer materials. A third-party review notes OCR-like errors reading node names from visualised graphs and that code was listed as unavailable. [Search summaries only.]
- **Numbers:** Disabling tree search worsens the VLMaterial (SBS) comparison NER from 14.846 to 33.953; parameter optimisation gives average perceptual-similarity gains of 6% and 8% for two MultiMat variants (all via search summary, unverified).
- **For the fleet:** Validate intermediate states cheaply (compile, schema, value-range, tiny preview) and backtrack before spending a full render. Supports a cheap-first funnel in front of the expensive render.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### Reflecting Process Expertise in Procedural Material Generation (July 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13318 (Kunal Gupta, Gaurav Joshi, Yen-Ru Chen, Seemandhar Jain, Ishit Mehta, Manmohan Chandraker (UC San Diego), 2026-07-14, arXiv 2026 (project name MaterialApprentice per the promised project page))
- **Claim:** Treats procedural material generation as retrieval-time reasoning over expert process traces (textual records of construction steps, parameters and design intent extracted from tutorial videos). An LLM ProcessSynthesizer writes a trace for the user's intent and an LLM Compiler grounds it into an executable Blender material graph. Appears not to use render feedback in the loop. Studies: 5 Blender artists (fewer edits needed, closer to professional strategies) and a 150-participant user study (better generation and editing than prior procedural systems). [Search summary only; code and data promised, release status unconfirmed.]
- **Numbers:** 5 artists; 150 participants; no metric values confirmed.
- **For the fleet:** A retrieval library of 'how an artist builds roof shingle / mowed grass / worn asphalt' as short text recipes is a cheap way to give proposer agents domain knowledge without more renders.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### VIGA (arXiv Jan 2026, ECCV 2026): analysis-by-synthesis agent with generator/verifier roles and evolving memory

- **Source:** https://github.com/Fugtemypt123/VIGA (Shaofeng Yin, Jiaxin Ge, Xiuyu Li et al. (UC Berkeley and collaborators), 2026-01 (arXiv 2601.11109), ECCV 2026)
- **Claim:** Training-free agent that rebuilds an image as an editable Blender scene in a write-run-render-compare-revise loop. One self-reflective agent alternates a Generator role (plan, write and run scene programs, retrieve assets, query the scene) and a Verifier role (reviews renders from multiple viewpoints, finds mismatches, feeds corrections forward). Keeps an evolving contextual memory of plans, code diffs and render history, plus a semantic skill library. The authors name VLM spatial perception and context-window length in very long runs as the current ceiling. [README read directly for the loop, roles, memory, benchmark list and the GPT-5 usage example; the numbers below are from the abstract via search summary.]
- **Numbers:** Gain over one-shot baselines: BlenderGym +35.32%, SlideBench +117.17%, BlenderBench (30 tasks, authors' own, multi-step) +124.70% (relative improvements, abstract via search summary). A truncated table row (VIGA 113.96% vs BlenderAlchemy 67.56% best-of-1) is unreliable. Cost not stated.
- **For the fleet:** Closest published analogue to our fleet loop. Copy: a role switch between propose and verify, multi-viewpoint verification renders, and a persistent diff-and-render history, but summarise it, since long histories hit the context ceiling the authors report.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### Thinking in Blender / SEIG (June 2026): staged executable inverse graphics

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02580 (Guangzhao He, Rundong Luo, Wei-Chiu Ma, Hadar Averbuch-Elor (Cornell), 2026-06-01, arXiv 2026)
- **Claim:** Reconstructs a scene from one image as an editable Blender program by refining factors in dependency order (geometry, materials, composition, lighting), where each stage depends only on earlier ones and a generator-verifier loop inside each stage decides whether to revise or advance. Reported to improve reconstruction fidelity substantially. Stated limitations: greedy stage ordering, initialisation errors, inference cost, single-view ambiguity. [Search summaries only; benchmark numbers not found.]
- **Numbers:** none confirmed
- **For the fleet:** Gate edits by concern and advance only when a stage passes its verifier. For our scene I would order concerns as global exposure/sky/sun, then ground/vegetation/road materials, then buildings, then geometry/density, then sensor/post (my inference; SEIG itself orders geometry, materials, composition, lighting). Expect greedy-order errors and allow a revisit when a late stage exposes an early mistake.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### Code-as-Room (May 2026) and WorldClaw (Aug 2026): agentic Blender/engine world builders

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18451 (Code-as-Room: Yixuan Yang, Zhen Luo et al.; WorldClaw: Tencent Hunyuan, 2026-05-18 (Code-as-Room, arXiv 2605.18451); 2026-08-05 (WorldClaw, arXiv 2608.05248), arXiv 2026)
- **Claim:** Code-as-Room: an MLLM agent turns a top-down room image into Blender code for geometry, materials and lighting; its reported problems with single-agent loops are instability and infinite looping, which it addresses with a multi-stage pipeline plus a cross-stage memory module; it compares against VIGA. Whether it has a render-critique-revise step is unconfirmed. WorldClaw: coarse-to-fine open-world generation (plan regions, terrain, assets, materials, then render-guided refinement agents for terrain appearance, object quality and object-terrain contact); the agent is reported to run on a Claude Opus-class model with task-specific skills (model name from a search summary, unverified). [Search summaries only.]
- **Numbers:** Code-as-Room: GPT-5.5 with the harness 67.5% vs 42.2% without on one visual-understanding metric (table columns were misaligned in the extract, so treat as unreliable). WorldClaw: none confirmed.
- **For the fleet:** Newest evidence that scaffolding (staged pipeline, cross-stage memory, bounded loops) matters more than raw model strength, and that unbounded self-refinement loops can spin. Put hard iteration caps and a persistent memory file into the fleet.

### NVIDIA usd-content-agents v0.6 (Material, Texture, Physics, Validation agents; Oct 2026)

- **Source:** https://github.com/NVIDIA-Omniverse/usd-content-agents (NVIDIA-Omniverse (Apache-2.0, reference implementation), 2026 (repo updated 2026-10-16), GitHub reference implementation)
- **Claim:** Closest vendor system to our task. Agents: Geometry (research preview), Material (beta), Texture (research preview), Physics (beta), Joint (research preview), Validation (research preview). Agentic mode = a coding agent (Codex or Claude Code harness; the repo ships .claude and .codex skill mirrors) plans, calls typed operations, reviews evidence and makes bounded refinements; runs live in a run directory with frozen inputs, decision records, digest-bound checkpoints and resume; failed runs are kept as diagnostics. Material Agent pipeline: optimize_usd, render_preview, generate_reference_image, per-prim multi-view renders, VLM picks a PBR material from a supplied library with reasoning, fuzzy-match validation of names against the library, harmonise instanced parts, apply, render; optional clustering of repeated prims. Material REFINEMENT loop: a BoTorch sweep over base-colour RGB, roughness, metallic (plus strength for textured) is scored DETERMINISTICALLY (linear-light albedo, ORM maps; no VLM), then ONLY the best valid trial is rendered on a canonical swatch and the VLM approves it or returns feedback that is turned into a new target with widened bounds; each iteration gets a fresh verdict. Benchmark design: two independent VLM judges score 0 to 100 (identity/part mapping 45%, colour 25%, finish 20%, consistency 10%), averaged; thresholds 70 acceptable and 85 high fidelity, explicitly 'operational baselines, not validated gates'; judges must be calibrated against human review; dev/validation/sealed-test split by asset family; content digests of datasets, workflows, skills, model settings and evaluators recorded per run. The README states there is no published controlled benchmark of agentic vs fixed pipelines on quality, cost or latency. Hardware guidance: 16 GB RAM / 250 GB minimum; recommended RTX 6000 Ada 48 GB class; local OVRTX about 2.5 GB; first smoke test 10 to 30 minutes. [All read directly from README and docs; trial counts, round limits and regression handling are not specified in the docs.]
- **Numbers:** No benchmark results published. Judge weights 45/25/20/10; pass 70, high fidelity 85; smoke test 10 to 30 min; OVRTX about 2.5 GB.
- **For the fleet:** Adopt three of its patterns: (1) cheap deterministic scoring of many candidates, render and VLM-judge only the winner; (2) a run directory with digests, decision records and resume; (3) two independent judges and a held-out test partition so the critic is calibrated before it gates anything. Its own docs admit no measured agentic-vs-pipeline benefit, so we must measure ours.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

It is ground-level facade work; the README gives no loop bound and no aerial-view results. UrbanVerse (search summary): converts crowd-sourced city-tour videos into physics-aware Isaac Sim scenes using a 100K asset library; releases 160 scenes from 24 countries; human-rated realism comparable to hand-crafted scenes; limits: street-level only, struggles with low light, fast motion, heavy occlusion.

- **For the fleet:** Both validate OSM-style geodata plus a VLM reflect step, but neither measures realism from a 40 m aerial viewpoint, so our critic has no published calibration for this view. Keep our deterministic geodata builder (cached by BUILD_VERSION) fixed during critique runs, and let agents change material mapping and parameters rather than regenerate geometry with non-deterministic generators.

### NVIDIA Cosmos Transfer 1 / 2.5 (2025) and Cosmos 3 (June 2026): sim-to-real appearance transfer, hardware and status

- **Source:** https://github.com/nvidia-cosmos/cosmos-transfer2.5 (NVIDIA (Cosmos), Transfer1 arXiv 2503.14492 (2025-03); Transfer2.5 Edge distilled 2026-02-23; Cosmos 3 launched 2026-06-01, Edge 4B released 2026-07, GitHub / NVIDIA)
- **Claim:** Both the Cosmos-Transfer1 and Cosmos-Transfer2.5 READMEs now say the repo is no longer under active development and point to Cosmos 3 ('significantly stronger transfer capabilities'); repos moved to github.com/nvidia/cosmos. Transfer2.5-2B generates video from multi-ControlNet inputs (depth, edge, segmentation, blur), described as turning simulated footage into realistic video (and real-to-real weather augmentation). Docs: example control weights depth 0.5, edge 1.0, seg 1.0, blur 0.5; the Cosmos Cookbook uses edge weight 1.0 for full structure preservation when changing lighting and lowers it (0.2) to allow shape changes. Single-GPU inference needs 65.4 GB VRAM; timings use 720p at 16 FPS with guardrails disabled; the distilled edge model handles only 93 frames in 4 steps. Requirements: Ampere or newer, driver >= 570.124.06, Linux glibc 2.35+. Cosmos 3 README: Super 64B (H200/B200/GB200), Nano 16B (RTX Pro 6000, H100, B200), Edge 4B (Jetson AGX Orin/Thor, RTX Pro 6000); license OpenMDW-1.1; shows a control-layout to photoreal-video transfer demo but does not state sim2real control inputs. Isaac Sim 5.1 docs include a Replicator CosmosWriter that emits RGB, depth, segmentation, shaded segmentation and edges as Cosmos Transfer inputs (search summary). [Transfer2.5, Transfer1 and Cosmos 3 READMEs and the inference doc read directly.]
- **Numbers:** 65.4 GB VRAM (2B model, 1 GPU). 93-frame segmentation-controlled generation: B200 92.25 s, H100 PCIe 264.13 s, H100 NVL 445.52 s, H20 683.65 s. End-to-end 121-frame input: B200 186.92 s, H100 PCIe 533.58 s. Distilled vs base average diffusion time: 24.2 s vs 179.7 s on B200, 49.8 s vs 374.4 s on H200 NVL (7.4x to 7.8x). No timings for consumer GPUs.
- **For the fleet:** Cosmos Transfer is a heavy, off-loop batch job. At 65.4 GB it will not fit a typical consumer card, it cannot share the single GPU with an Isaac Sim render, and the 1/2.5 repos are frozen. Keep it out of the keep/revert loop and decide later, once the owner's VRAM is known.

### Does a generative post-process fit a physically based camera-sensor dataset? Evidence on diffusion sim-to-real translation vs calibrated sensor models

- **Source:** https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/file/743771397cef2aa0ef497c428c3a46b7-Paper-Conference.pdf (ODGEN (NeurIPS 2024); segmentation-guided ControlNet for AVs (Springer 2025/26); SimuScope (WACV 2025); Carlson et al. (2018); SNIC (arXiv 2512.15905, Dec 2025), 2018 to 2025, NeurIPS 2024; WACV 2025; Springer chapter; arXiv)
- **Claim:** Evidence for: ControlNet/diffusion translation conditioned on segmentation or edges can improve downstream detection (segmentation-guided ControlNet on KITTI: YOLOv8 trained on refined data gained over 20 mAP points, FID 41.95; BCon construction-site pipeline reports near parity with real-image training). Evidence against / caveats: ODGEN reports generated images can become inconsistent with the label conditions (concept bleeding, merged overlapping objects); SimuScope fine-tunes with LoRA specifically to stay aligned with simulator labels; NVIDIA itself scores Transfer outputs with alignment metrics (blur SSIM, edge F1, depth si-RMSE, mask mIoU). Physically based sensor modelling has its own evidence: Carlson et al. report that physically based sensor effects (chromatic aberration, blur, exposure, noise, colour temperature) reduce the sim-to-real detection gap; SNIC shows calibrated noise beats additive white Gaussian noise. No head-to-head study of radiometric fidelity for generative enhancement vs physics-based sensor simulation was found, and no Cosmos Transfer evaluation on drone/aerial footage was found. [All via search summaries.]
- **Numbers:** Segmentation-guided ControlNet: >20 mAP points, FID 41.95. Others: none confirmed.
- **For the fleet:** My conclusion (inference, not a published result): a generative post-process rewrites pixels, so it destroys the radiometric and sensor-noise provenance that makes the LUCID pipeline dataset physically based. If used at all, make it a separate, clearly labelled augmentation track applied AFTER the sensor model on 8-bit frames, with edge/depth/seg controls from the same render, gated by alignment metrics against the untouched frames, and never as the acceptance criterion of the physical-realism loop.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### VLM judges: pairwise beats pointwise; known biases (GPT-4V Elo 2024, GenArena 2026, rank-vs-score 2026)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06013 (GPTEval3D (Wu, Yang et al., CVPR 2024); GenArena (arXiv 2602.06013); 'VLM Judges Can Rank but Cannot Score' (arXiv 2604.25235); 'Judging the Judges' (IJCNLP 2025); 'Pair Difficulty Matters' (arXiv 2609.37577); 'MLLM-as-a-Judge Exhibits Model Preference Bias' (arXiv 2604.11589); 'Fooling the LVLM Judges' (arXiv 2505.15249), 2024 to 2026, CVPR 2024; arXiv 2026; IJCNLP 2025)
- **Claim:** GPTEval3D (2024) has GPT-4V compare two 3D assets by user-set criteria and turns the pairwise verdicts into Elo ratings that track human preference; the authors note hallucination and systematic errors that ensembling mitigates but does not remove. GenArena (Feb 2026) reports pairwise judging is more accurate and stable than absolute scoring for visual generation. 'VLM Judges Can Rank but Cannot Score' (Apr 2026) finds judges order outputs correctly but their absolute scores are unreliable, with uncertainty covering about 40% of the score range for aesthetics/natural images. Position bias is strongest on close pairs (Judging the Judges; Pair Difficulty Matters); one study finds visual markers such as bounding boxes pull LVLM judges toward the marked image; another reports judges favouring their own model family's outputs (shown for captions, not images). [All via search summaries; I could not open the papers.]
- **Numbers:** GenArena: Qwen3-VL 8B pairwise beat the EditScore-72B reward model on editing; GLM-4V-Flash pairwise 87.2% vs GPT-5 75.5% on EditScore-Bench. Rank-vs-score: score uncertainty about 40% of range (aesthetics/natural images) vs about 70% (charts/math).
- **For the fleet:** Use pairwise A/B (candidate vs incumbent) with both orders and a mandatory tie outcome; do not accept on an absolute 1-to-10 score. Escalate close pairs (where bias is worst) to the strongest model. Use a critic from a different model family than the proposer, strip annotations from images, and calibrate the critic on artificially degraded copies of our own frames before trusting it.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### Multi-agent scaling evidence: Google/MIT 'Science of Scaling Agent Systems' (Dec 2025) and Anthropic's multi-agent research system (Jun 2025)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08296 (Google Research and MIT; Anthropic engineering, 2025-12 (arXiv 2512.08296); 2025-06 (Anthropic post), arXiv; Anthropic Engineering blog)
- **Claim:** Google/MIT, 180 configurations: centralised coordination improved a parallelisable financial-reasoning task by about 81% over one agent, while every multi-agent variant lost 39% to 70% on the strictly sequential PlanCraft planning task; independent agents amplified errors 17.2x vs 4.4x for centralised coordination; returns diminish or turn negative once the single-agent baseline exceeds about 45%; a predictive model picks the best coordination strategy for 87% of held-out configs. Anthropic: a lead Opus 4 with Sonnet 4 sub-agents beat single-agent Opus 4 by 90.2% on its internal research eval at roughly 15x the tokens of chat; weaker for tightly interdependent work such as coding (figures from secondary write-ups; the original post was not opened).
- **Numbers:** +80.8 to 80.9% parallelisable; -39 to -70% sequential; 17.2x vs 4.4x error amplification; about 45% saturation; 90.2% and about 15x tokens (secondary).
- **For the fleet:** Our loop is sequential at its core (one GPU, serialised renders, each accepted edit changes the baseline). Parallelise only the decomposable parts (many proposals, many critic votes, static checks, several worktrees), keep one orchestrator that owns acceptance, and avoid agents that mutually edit the same files. Use a stronger lead and cheaper workers.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: not checked

### Design rules proposed by the researcher

- RULE 1, one GPU owner. Run exactly one render-job server process that owns the GPU and a FIFO queue; agents never launch Isaac Sim themselves. A job is (git commit SHA, probe-set id, settings digest) and returns images plus numeric stats. While a render is in progress the other agents do CPU-only work (draft patches, read logs, run static checks, critique the previous render). Rests on: Multi-agent scaling evidence (sequential steps degrade, only decomposable work gains), Isaac Sim MCP (community) and BlenderMCP (we need our own 'look' tool), BlenderGym.
- RULE 2, cheap-first funnel before any minutes-long render. Order: compile and unit tests (python3.11 py_compile, the repo's unittest files) -> value-range lint on material and light parameters (albedo, roughness, sun intensity) -> optional low-resolution or cropped preview (the repo's --resolution_scale 0.5 mode) -> full probe render only for the 1 or 2 best survivors. Rests on: NVIDIA usd-content-agents (deterministic scoring of all trials, render and VLM-judge only the winner), MultiMat (validate intermediate states and backtrack before expensive steps), VLMaterial.
- RULE 3, fixed paired probes. Define a small fixed probe set (my suggestion: 4 to 8 views at fixed poses, time of day, seed and resolution covering known weak spots such as ground, roofs, tree canopy, road, horizon/sky) and render baseline and candidate under identical conditions; cache baseline renders by commit SHA plus settings digest so each experiment costs one new render set, not two. Record digests of code, settings and model IDs for every run. Rests on: BlenderGym (goal-image metrics under fixed instances), NVIDIA usd-content-agents (hold case sets constant, digest-bound runs, sealed test partition).
- RULE 4, scale the critic, not just the proposer. After each render set, run k independent pairwise critiques (my suggestion: k = 3 to 5, cheap VLM, order-swapped, different prompt seeds) because critic calls take seconds and renders take minutes. Shift budget toward verification as total budget grows, and expect verification to be weakest for subtle continuous changes such as exposure and colour. The optimal split under asymmetric costs is unmeasured, so treat k as a tunable default. Rests on: BlenderGym, BlenderAlchemy, VLM judges (pairwise beats pointwise).
- RULE 5, accept only by pairwise win against the incumbent, never on an absolute score. Candidate must win a majority of probes in both presentation orders AND show no probe where it loses clearly (regression veto) AND pass the numeric guards (rule 6). Keep absolute 0-to-100 rubric scores (two independent judges averaged, as NVIDIA does) only as logged trend data, and do not treat the 70/85 thresholds as validated gates. Rests on: BlenderAlchemy, VLM judges, NVIDIA usd-content-agents, GPTEval3D.
- RULE 6, hybrid critic: numbers first, VLM second. Put objective measurements beside every VLM verdict: mean scene radiance vs the 18% grey card line from the log, histogram and clipping fractions, sky/grass/roof/asphalt patch colours and luminance vs target ranges, HdrColor availability, sensor-model output statistics. The VLM judges gestalt, texture repetition, scale and artefacts. Rests on: BlenderGym (VLMs fail at accurate lighting colour; large human gap on lighting and material), SceneCraft (numeric constraint scorers plus VLM), SceneWeaver (separate physical/visual/semantic axes).
- RULE 7, give the critic references, and calibrate it before it gates anything. Build a reference pack of real photos of west Ames (or comparable Iowa) taken from low-altitude oblique or drone viewpoints, same season and sun angle if possible, and ask the critic 'which is closer to the reference' in addition to 'which looks more real'. Because no surveyed system critiques reference-free aerial realism, first measure the critic: build pairs where one frame is deliberately degraded (wrong albedo, flat lighting, missing shadows) and require the critic to pick the better one with a high hit rate before enabling accept/reject. Also have a human audit a random sample of accepted edits each cycle. Rests on: BlenderAlchemy (visual imagination reference), VIGA, NVIDIA usd-content-agents (reference-grounded scoring, calibrate against humans), VLM judges, Geodata to photoreal twin.
- RULE 8, regression control by construction. One concern per worktree branch (for example 'roof albedo' or 'sun disk size'), atomic diffs, no cross-branch edits. Merge into main only after the incumbent-vs-candidate test passes, then re-run the full probe set including sentinel probes from earlier accepted edits to catch interaction regressions. Never rely on in-place edits that cannot be reverted. Use git revert, not agent memory, to undo. Keep a ledger of rejected edits with reasons so they are not retried. Rests on: LL3M (no revert), CityCode/Awomo (versioned restore, bounded repair), BlenderAlchemy.
- RULE 9, small, bounded search per concern. Use breadth 2 to 4 candidate diffs per round (cheap model drafts, whole-file rewrites not allowed), depth at most 3 rounds per concern, and stop after 2 consecutive non-improvements or a fixed render cap per concern (my suggested defaults, unvalidated). A pairwise tournament over b candidates costs O(log b) critic queries. Only render the top 1 or 2 after the cheap funnel. Rests on: BlenderAlchemy, NVIDIA usd-content-agents (bounded refinement), Code-as-Room and WorldClaw (loops spin without caps), BlenderGym (diminishing returns).
- RULE 10, stage the concerns and gate advancement. Fix global exposure, sky and sun first (it confounds every later judgment), then ground/vegetation/road materials, then buildings, then geometry and density, then sensor/post. Each stage is its own proposer+verifier loop with an explicit 'advance' gate; allow one backtrack if a later stage reveals an early error. This ordering is my inference from SEIG's staged design, not a published result for this scene. Rests on: Thinking in Blender / SEIG, BlenderGym.
- RULE 11, persistent memory. Keep a run ledger on disk (JSON/markdown): decision records, commit SHAs, settings digests, model IDs, critic verdicts, rejected edits, resumable state. Keep a curated library of accepted helpers and material recipes merged by review, not auto-appended, and a retrieval library of short artist-style recipes for materials. Summarise old history instead of replaying it, since long histories hit context limits. Rests on: SceneCraft, VIGA, NVIDIA usd-content-agents, Make-it-Real, Reflecting Process Expertise in Procedural Material Generation.
- RULE 12, model tiering. Cheap models: patch drafting, doc lookup, log and traceback reading, static checks, repeated critic votes and screening. Strongest model only for (a) diagnosing why a render looks wrong from several probes plus stats, (b) final adjudication when cheap votes split or the pair is close, (c) adversarial review of a merge. Use a critic of a different model family or version from the proposer to reduce self-preference. Evidence is mixed and dated (2024 models: scaled small VLM beat unscaled strong ones; coordination returns saturate once a single agent is already good), so re-measure on our own probes. Rests on: BlenderGym, Multi-agent scaling evidence, VLM judges.
- RULE 13, safe execution. Agents edit files in git worktrees only; only the render server executes scene code, after py_compile and a path/diff allowlist check; no unsandboxed 'execute arbitrary script' tool is exposed to agents, and blocked scripts return a reason so the agent can retry (BlenderMCP safe-mode pattern). The render server should also reject runs on any machine other than the owner's PC (owner rule). Rests on: Isaac Sim MCP (community) and BlenderMCP.
- RULE 14, keep Isaac Sim warm and measure first. Start one persistent Isaac Sim process (or a restartable one with shader/asset caches) so startup and shader compile do not repeat per probe; the first run has not happened yet, so the very first fleet task is a measured timing run (cold start, warm render, per-view cost, HdrColor works or not, mean radiance line). ovrtx documents a 1 to 2 minute first-step shader compile and usd-content-agents a 10 to 30 minute smoke test, so expect cold costs. Treat ovrtx as an optional later experiment because nothing confirms it matches Isaac Sim 5.1 output. Rests on: NVIDIA ovrtx and the Omniverse agent-skills catalog, NVIDIA usd-content-agents.
- RULE 15, keep generative sim-to-real out of the loop. Do not use Cosmos Transfer (or any diffusion restyle) to improve or judge the physical render. If wanted later, run it as a separate batch track on 8-bit frames AFTER the sensor model, with edge weight 1.0 and depth/seg controls from the same render, 93-frame chunks, and accept it only if alignment metrics (edge F1, depth si-RMSE, mask mIoU, blur SSIM) against the untouched frames pass; label those frames as generative, and keep the physically based frames as the primary ground truth. Check VRAM first: Transfer2.5-2B needs 65.4 GB, the Transfer 1/2.5 repos are frozen in favour of Cosmos 3, and it cannot share the GPU with an Isaac Sim render. Rests on: NVIDIA Cosmos Transfer 1 / 2.5 and Cosmos 3, Does a generative post-process fit a physically based camera-sensor dataset.
- RULE 16, prove value with an objective, independent metric. A VLM-judged improvement can be critic-hacking. Alongside the loop, track a metric the critic cannot see: distribution distance to matched real aerial crops (FID/KID or similar), a downstream detector or segmenter trained on frames, or the radiometric checks in rule 6; require the trend to agree with the critic before declaring a campaign successful. Rests on: Aerial-twin generation 2025 to 2026 (Awomo downstream gain, UAV-SynthScene), 3D-Generalist (CLIP-style proxy risk), VLM judges.

### Rules the fact-checker says to drop or fix

- RULE 4 (scale the critic): the sentences 'shift budget toward verification as total budget grows' and 'expect verification to be weakest for subtle continuous changes such as exposure and colour' are not confirmed. Only 'verifiers benefit from inference scaling' and 'a Goldilocks VeriRatio exists' (0.33/0.62/0.73) are verified, from the BlenderGym project page. Keep k as a tunable default and drop the budget-growth and lighting-vs-blend-shape claims until the paper is opened. Its support from 'pairwise beats pointwise' (GenArena etc.) is also unverified.
- RULE 5 (accept by pairwise win): the evidence that pairwise comparison is more reliable than absolute scoring (BlenderAlchemy tournament, GenArena, 'rank but cannot score') is unverified; BlenderAlchemy's README does not describe the tournament. Keep as a design choice, not as evidence-backed. Fix the NVIDIA reference: its doc says the 70/85 thresholds are 'operational baselines, not ground truth' and must be calibrated against a stratified human-reviewed set; it does not say 'validated gates'. Two independent judges averaged is confirmed.
- RULE 6 (hybrid critic): attribution 'BlenderGym: VLMs fail at accurate lighting colour; large human gap on lighting and material' is unconfirmed (no leaderboard or finding seen). SceneCraft constraint scorers and SceneWeaver's physical/visual/semantic axes are unverified and the SceneWeaver tool names in the report are wrong (README: Initializer/Implementer/Modifier). The rule itself is a sensible design choice but should not cite these as support.
- RULE 9 (bounded search): support is weak. 'Loops spin without caps' (Code-as-Room, WorldClaw) is not in either README; BlenderAlchemy's O(log b) tournament and default depth/breadth are unconfirmed; usd-content-agents docs state NO round limit or trial count ('bounded' refers to parameter bounds); BlenderGym 'diminishing returns' is unconfirmed. Keep the suggested numbers (breadth 2-4, depth <=3) labelled as untested defaults and remove the citations or mark them unverified.
- RULE 10 (stage the concerns): rests on SEIG (arXiv 2606.02580), which could not be opened at all, and on BlenderGym, which has no staging claim in anything verified. The report already calls the ordering its own inference; drop SEIG and BlenderGym from 'Rests on' until SEIG is checked.
- RULE 11 (persistent memory): drop or soften three attributions. 'Long histories hit context limits' as a VIGA finding is not in the VIGA README; VIGA's 'semantic skill library' is not in the README; SceneCraft's consensus-merged library and 'Reflecting Process Expertise' recipes are unverified. Still supported: VIGA's evolving memory of plans, code diffs and render history, usd-content-agents' run directory with frozen inputs, decisions and resume, Make-it-Real's tree-indexed material library.
- RULE 12 (model tiering): 'scaled small VLM beat unscaled strong ones on all three metrics' is only partly supported (project page says scaled open-source verifiers can exceed closed-source ones; the 'all three metrics' and 'unscaled or lightly scaled' details are unconfirmed). 'Coordination returns saturate once a single agent is already good (~45%)' (Google/MIT) is unverified. Supported: Anthropic's lead-Opus-plus-Sonnet-subagents result (+90.2%, about 15x tokens, weaker for coding). The self-preference bias claim is unverified and, per the report itself, was shown for captions not images.
- RULE 8 (regression control): remove CityCode and Awomo as support (both unverifiable); LL3M's 'cannot revert' limitation is confirmed, so cite that instead.
- RULE 14 (keep Isaac Sim warm): the 1-2 minute shader compile (ovrtx) and the 10-30 minute smoke test (usd-content-agents) are confirmed but describe those other products, not Isaac Sim 5.1. Say so explicitly; do not present them as expected Isaac Sim cold-start costs.
- RULE 15 (keep generative out of the loop): the numbers (65.4 GB, 93-frame chunks, edge weight 1.0, frozen Transfer 1/2.5 repos, driver and Ampere requirements) are confirmed. Fix: the Cosmos 3 date (README says May 2026, not 2026-06-01), the '7.4x to 7.8x' speed-up (quoted rows give 7.43x and 7.52x), and do not cite the cookbook 0.2 edge weight or Isaac Sim CosmosWriter until checked. The cited evidence finding 'Does a generative post-process fit...' is unverifiable; present the rule as the author's inference only.
- RULE 16 (objective independent metric): all three supports (Awomo downstream gain, UAV-SynthScene, 3D-Generalist CLIP proxy risk) are unverifiable, and the GPTEval3D/other judge-bias papers are mostly unverified. The principle (do not let the critic be the only measure) is sound but currently has no verified citation.
- RULE 7 (references and calibration): the NVIDIA calibration requirement and BlenderAlchemy's imagined reference images are confirmed; VIGA's multi-view verifier is confirmed. The claim that no surveyed system critiques reference-free aerial realism is an absence claim, unverified; UrbanVerse is video-based, not OSM-based, so 'both validate OSM-style geodata' in the geodata finding should be corrected.
- RULE 1, 2, 3, 13: sound as written. RULE 13 is fully supported (Isaac Sim MCP and BlenderMCP READMEs). RULE 2 is supported by usd-content-agents and VLMaterial's two-step pattern, but remove MultiMat as a citation (unverifiable). RULE 1 should cite only the Anthropic post for 'sequential work does not parallelise', since the Google/MIT figures are unverified.

### Open questions

- Source access limit: WebFetch could open only github.com pages. Every other host I tried (arxiv.org, openaccess.thecvf.com, proceedings.mlr.press, huggingface.co, openreview.net, semanticscholar.org, mlanthology.org, alphaxiv.org, github.io project pages, blogs.nvidia.com, developer.nvidia.com, docs.isaacsim.omniverse.nvidia.com, globenewswire.com) failed with DNS error ENOTFOUND, and I did not try to bypass this. So paper-level numbers (BlenderGym, BlenderAlchemy, SceneCraft, LL3M, VIGA, SceneWeaver, etc.) come from WebSearch result summaries and are marked unverified; GitHub README and docs facts were read directly. Verify the numbers against the papers before quoting them.
- BlenderAlchemy defaults are unconfirmed: the numeric depth and breadth used in the paper, and whether the incumbent program is included in the tournament bracket. SceneCraft's number of feedback rounds and library-learning ablation, LL3M's user-study and cost figures, and Make-it-Real's GPT-4V evaluation numbers were also not found.
- No surveyed system critiques realism WITHOUT a goal image or reference at 40 m aerial viewpoints. BlenderGym, BlenderAlchemy, VIGA and SEIG all have a target image or text. What reference imagery (real low-altitude oblique photos of west Ames, public drone footage) can serve, and how accurate is a 2026 VLM critic on this view? Needs the degraded-frame calibration test in rule 7.
- The owner's GPU model and VRAM are unknown. This decides whether Cosmos Transfer 2.5 (65.4 GB for the 2B model) or Cosmos 3 Nano (listed for RTX Pro 6000, H100, B200) is feasible at all, and how many Isaac Sim probe renders per hour are realistic. Nothing in this survey gives timings for consumer GPUs.
- Can ovrtx render the existing Isaac Sim 5.1 USD scene and expose an HDR colour output equivalent to Replicator HdrColor so the LUCID sensor pipeline works unchanged? Its README does not mention Isaac Sim, Kit, HdrColor or AOVs, and the library is pre-release.
- Does Isaac Sim 5.1 have what the 6.0 agent skills and connectors provide? NVIDIA's agent-toolkit announcement (June 2026) refers to Isaac Sim 6.0; whether upgrading is worth it is unknown.
- Optimal generation vs verification split when a render costs minutes and a critic call costs seconds is unmeasured (BlenderGym treated both as equal cost). How many critic votes per probe pair are needed before decisions are stable? Needs an empirical test on our own frames.
- Are there published VLM-in-the-loop systems that tune renderer settings (tonemapper, exposure, path-tracing/denoise options) or sun/sky calibration specifically? I found none for 2025 to 2026, only Blender-manual tooling; BlenderGym lighting results suggest numeric anchoring is needed.
- The Anthropic multi-agent figures (90.2%, about 15x tokens) and parts of the Google/MIT scaling numbers came through secondary summaries, and the model pairs are 2025-era. The generalisation to our sequential render loop is my inference.

### Fact-checker summary

Tooling limits first. WebSearch refused (its per-turn budget was already used up). arxiv.org, openaccess.thecvf.com, proceedings.mlr.press, openreview.net, huggingface.co, api.semanticscholar.org, export.arxiv.org, ar5iv, research.google and blendergym.github.io all fail with DNS ENOTFOUND. github.com and www.anthropic.com work. GitHub search began returning 429 after about 12 queries. So I could not open any arXiv or conference page, and every claim that exists only in a paper is 'unverifiable'. source_exists=false means 'could not confirm', not 'fabricated'. I ran no code, installed nothing, and changed and pushed nothing. This step was fact-checking only.

Verified from primary sources. These repos and pages exist and match the report on the points listed in each correction:
- BlenderGym: project page source and README
- BlenderAlchemy: README
- Make-it-Real: README
- VLMaterial: README
- VIGA: README
- usd-content-agents: README, the material refinement doc, and the benchmarks doc
- ovrtx: README and the org page
- kit-usd-agents: README
- Isaac Sim MCP: README
- BlenderMCP: README
- Holodeck: README
- 3D-GPT: README
- UrbanWorld2.0: README
- Cosmos Transfer 1/2.5: READMEs plus the inference and setup docs
- Cosmos 3: README
- Anthropic's multi-agent research post

Errors and mismatches found:
