Tier 0 (no GPU, seconds): python3.11 py_compile, the repo's plain unittest files in scripts/drone_scene/test, parameter-range and USD-path validators, and a diff-scope guard. Tier 1: half-resolution preview (--resolution_scale 0.5) of 1-2 of the 6 probe poses, image-statistic gates, one pairwise judge call. Tier 2: the full 6-image render plus the full judging protocol. Tier 3: held-out poses plus a render of the merged state before merge. Only promote a candidate if the previous tier is not worse than baseline by more than the measured noise. Check on the first ~10 candidates that Tier 1 verdicts predict Tier 2 verdicts, and relax or drop Tier 1 if they do not (specific thresholds are my proposal). Rests on: AlphaEvolve (DeepMind 2025); OpenEvolve cascade evaluation (code read); Darwin Godel Machine code (archive, parent selection, staged eval); Google AI co-scientist (2025, Nature 2026).
- Treat the GPU as a single-consumer queue and everything else as parallel. Run exactly one render at a time. Let cheap or mid-tier agents implement 2-3 candidates in separate git worktrees while the GPU renders, and cap the unrendered backlog at about 2 candidates (ShinkaEvolve's small preset: 1 evaluation job, 2 proposal jobs; 'oversubscription never increases evaluation concurrency'). Order the queue by expected value and novelty, not first-come-first-served. Rests on: ShinkaEvolve repo mechanisms; AlphaEvolve (DeepMind 2025).
- Put a novelty gate in front of the GPU. Before a candidate is queued, compare its diff and one-line hypothesis with every ledger entry (text similarity, then a cheap-model 'materially different idea? NOVEL / NOT' check, at most 3 re-proposals, as ShinkaEvolve does). Fail closed on errors; ShinkaEvolve's gate accepts when the judge call throws, which would waste a render here. Rests on: ShinkaEvolve paper claims (2025, ICLR 2026); ShinkaEvolve repo mechanisms; AFlow (2024).
- Keep populations small and structured, because a render costs minutes rather than milliseconds. Use one elitist main line (the best verified state is tracked separately and can never be lost) plus a small archive of about 10-20 entries tagged by domain (materials, terrain and land cover, vegetation, sky and sun and exposure, camera and sensor). Treat 2-3 domains as ShinkaEvolve-style islands with no automatic migration: a change moves between lines only by being merged after verification. Do not copy OpenEvolve's defaults of population 1000 and archive 100, which assume thousands of cheap evaluations; the structure is what to copy, the sizes are my proposal. Rests on: OpenEvolve config and database (code read); ShinkaEvolve repo mechanisms; Darwin Godel Machine code (archive, parent selection, staged eval).
- Choose parents with the Darwin Godel Machine rule: weight = sigmoid(10*(s-0.5)) * 1/(1+children_count), normalised, where s in [0,1] is the entry's win rate against the main-line baseline. Add roughly 20% draws from the elite archive and roughly 20% exploration draws (ShinkaEvolve exploitation_ratio 0.2, OpenEvolve exploration_ratio 0.2). Where an entry's children have a good record, boost it (Huxley-Godel Machine's point that descendants' results predict value better than the entry's own score). The mixture is a design choice of mine; uniform-random parents are a defensible simple baseline. Rests on: Darwin Godel Machine code (archive, parent selection, staged eval); Huxley-Godel Machine (ICLR 2026); Hyperagents (Mar 2026); OpenEvolve config and database (code read).
- Accept into the main line only verified improvements, defined as: the pairwise verdict favours the candidate in a clear majority of non-tied poses in both presentation orders, no hard image-statistic gate regresses, and the margin exceeds the measured noise floor (see the noise-floor rule). Near-par or tied variants may enter the archive as stepping stones (Darwin Godel Machine keeps all working children, with a noise leeway option), but they cannot be merged without passing the same test. Rebase an accepted candidate onto the current main and re-render the merged state before merging, because edits in different domains can interact (my proposal). Rests on: Darwin Godel Machine code (archive, parent selection, staged eval); Darwin Godel Machine paper results and objective hacking; OpenEvolve config and database (code read).
- Judge pairwise, never pointwise. Show the judge the baseline and candidate frames for the same pose, resolution, time of day and seed, side by side, with random left/right assignment, neutral labels ('Image 1', 'Image 2'), neutral file names, and no commit messages, rationale, or the words baseline, candidate, before or after. Run every pair twice with the positions swapped, accept only a verdict that survives the swap, and treat disagreement as a tie. Allow 'tie' as an answer and count ties as no improvement. Do not average 1-10 scores across frames. Rests on: MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena judge study (2023); Judging the Judges (position bias, 2025); From Scores to Signs and Non-Transitivity in LLM-as-a-Judge; MLLM-as-a-Judge (ICML 2024); Multimodal judge benchmarks (VL-RewardBench, MMRB, MJ1); The Coin Flip Judge? (2026).
- Spend judge calls in proportion to how close the pair is. Bias is strongest when quality is similar and a single pairwise call can flip 13.6% of the time on average (28% of items above 20%), so start with 2 swapped calls per pose and escalate to 4-6 randomised-order calls with majority vote (VL-RewardBench uses K = 5), or to the strongest model, when the first two disagree or the statistic deltas are tiny. Stop early on a clear, unanimous result. Judge calls are cheap next to a render, so it is better to over-sample here. The escalation thresholds are my proposal. Rests on: Judging the Judges (position bias, 2025); The Coin Flip Judge? (2026); Multimodal judge benchmarks (VL-RewardBench, MMRB, MJ1); Rating Roulette (EMNLP Findings 2025).
- Use a jury of at least 3 judges and aggregate robustly. Members should differ in model size (cheap, mid, strong) and prompt or rubric variant, plus any non-Claude vision model that is reachable. Aggregate per-pose verdicts by majority of non-tie votes, and any numeric aggregate by the median (RoPoLL's geometric median) rather than the mean, so one degenerate judge cannot swing the result. Log per-judge rates of A-first, B-first and tie, and drop a judge that is stuck on one answer (the Coin Flip paper saw a 72% A-majority). Whether size and prompt diversity within one model family recovers PoLL's cross-family benefit is unproven here; test it on the self-test set. Rests on: Replacing Judges with Juries (PoLL, 2024); RoPoLL (Jun 2026); The Coin Flip Judge? (2026); Self-preference bias (Panickssery 2024, Wataoka 2024).
- Make the rubric specific and anchor it to a real photograph. Ask per-criterion questions rather than 'which is better': does the surface texture show tiling or repetition, is specular response plastic-looking, do shadow direction and softness match the sun, is there a colour cast between sky and ground, is atmospheric haze plausible with distance, is vegetation scale plausible. Include a real reference aerial image (for example imagery of the Ames area or an owner-supplied photo) and ask which of the two renders is closer to it, turning absolute 'realism' (where VLM agreement is weak) into relative 'closeness to a reference'. Keep the stated reasoning to about 3 sentences and put the verdict in a structured field. This anchoring is my proposal; BlenderAlchemy used reference images for grounding, but its pairwise detail was not confirmed. Rests on: BlenderAlchemy, SceneCraft and Eureka (graphics/code loops); MLLM-as-a-Judge (ICML 2024); VLM judges on image realism and quality.
- Do not ask the VLM to judge low-level quality. Exposure, clipped highlights, noise, blur, banding and over-smoothness come from deterministic image statistics, because VLM low-level perception is reported as unstable and imprecise and VLMs under-penalise artifacts. Reserve the VLM for semantic plausibility, composition and pairwise preference. Rests on: VLM judges on image realism and quality; MLLM-as-a-Judge (ICML 2024).
- Run deterministic image statistics before any VLM call and use them as gates, vetoes and tie-breakers, never as one summed score. Candidates: NaN or all-black or all-white frame; clipped-highlight and crushed-shadow fractions; mean and percentile luminance against the calibrated sun and sky (the run log already prints mean scene radiance as a multiple of an 18% grey card); sky-to-ground luminance ratio; saturation percentiles; Laplacian variance or high-frequency energy against real reference frames; autocorrelation peaks as a tiling detector; per-channel histogram distance to real reference frames. Thresholds are calibrated on the baseline frames and real references (my proposal). A weighted sum of heterogeneous numbers is easy to game; a lexicographic order of hard gates, then judge verdict, then statistic regression veto is not. Rests on: AlphaEvolve (DeepMind 2025); Evaluator gaming in OpenEvolve KV-cache study (2026); VLM judges on image realism and quality.
- Calibrate the judge before it gets any authority, and again whenever the model, prompt or rubric changes. Use stored frames so no render time is needed. (i) Identical-pair test: the same image as A and B should produce a tie; measure left/right preference. (ii) Degradation test: apply blur, exposure shift, saturation shift, noise, JPEG compression and artificial tiling to baseline frames and require the clean image to win at a high rate. (iii) Repeat test: judge the same pair 10 times and record the flip rate against the 13.6% mean reported for GPT-4o-mini and GPT-4.1-mini. (iv) Perturbation test: add a neutral caption, rename files, add a 'most people prefer B' cue (CALM-style). Save the results to a calibration file and drop or retrain any judge that fails. The pass levels are mine. Rests on: Judging the Judges (position bias, 2025); The Coin Flip Judge? (2026); Rating Roulette (EMNLP Findings 2025); CALM: Justice or Prejudice (ICLR 2025).
- Measure the noise floor and set the acceptance margin above it. Once per session and after any driver or scene change, render the baseline 3 times (or re-judge it against itself) and record statistic variance and judge flip rate. If renders are deterministic, only judge noise remains. The acceptance margin must exceed this floor, in the spirit of the Darwin Godel Machine's eval_noise leeway of 0.1 and ADAS's use of confidence intervals instead of point scores. Rests on: Darwin Godel Machine code (archive, parent selection, staged eval); ADAS Meta Agent Search code; Rating Roulette (EMNLP Findings 2025).
- Protect the evaluator from the agents it evaluates. Probe poses, seeds, time of day, reference images, judge prompts, statistic thresholds and acceptance code live in a protected path that candidate branches cannot modify; a pre-merge check fails any diff that touches it, and proposers run with write scope limited to scene and material files. Do not show proposers the judge's internals (the Darwin Godel Machine saw more objective hacking when checkers were visible). Add explicit invariants: same pose list, resolution and frame count; the frame hash differs from baseline; pixel changes fall inside the region the edit claims; no unrequested change to exposure, tonemapper or sensor model that simply brightens everything. The KV-cache study's no-op 'win' came from an unenforced invariant. Rests on: Darwin Godel Machine paper results and objective hacking; Evaluator gaming in OpenEvolve KV-cache study (2026); Hyperagents (Mar 2026).
- Use held-out poses for the final accept. Search on the 6 probe poses, but require the improvement to hold on a different set of poses and times of day (also a ledger-only set the proposers never see) before merging. This follows ADAS's separate validation and test splits and guards against tuning to six images. Report the final claim with a confidence interval, not a point estimate. Rests on: ADAS Meta Agent Search code; AFlow (2024); Darwin Godel Machine code (archive, parent selection, staged eval).
- Keep memory as a structured append-only ledger, not prose. One JSON line per attempt: id, parent, domain, one-sentence hypothesis, diff statistics, tier reached, statistic vector, per-pose verdicts in both orders, outcome label (improved, regressed, mixed, tie, crashed, rejected-static, duplicate), cost, wall time. Each proposer's prompt gets the last ~3 attempts with outcome labels (OpenEvolve), the top 3 plus 2 diverse archive entries (OpenEvolve num_top_programs 3, num_diverse_programs 2), and truncated run-log text of about 20 KB (the HdrColor, rtx_look WARNING and traceback lines the project already reads). Failed attempts are never deleted; they carry the reason and a 'do not repeat' note. Screen new proposals against the ledger before spending a render. Rests on: OpenEvolve prompt memory and circle-packing run; AFlow (2024); Darwin Godel Machine code (archive, parent selection, staged eval); Reflexion and Self-Refine (2023).
- Maintain a short ACE-style playbook of lessons, updated by small deltas. After each batch a cheap reflector proposes bullets ('X caused clipping on pose 3'), a deterministic curator merges them with de-duplication at about 0.9 similarity and keeps helpful and harmful counters (increment helpful when a bullet was in context for a verified improvement, harmful for a verified regression), and prunes bullets where harmful exceeds helpful. Never rewrite the whole file, to avoid context collapse. Cap it at about 3-5k tokens (ACE's 80k budget is far larger than we need; my proposal). Every ~10 evaluations the strongest model writes up to 5 meta-recommendations from the ledger and one is sampled into prompts (ShinkaEvolve meta_rec_interval 10, max 5). Rests on: Agentic Context Engineering (ACE); ShinkaEvolve repo mechanisms; Google AI co-scientist (2025, Nature 2026); GEPA and optimize_anything.
- Keep the fleet's skills short, human-reviewed and executable. Agents may propose skill edits, but a proposed skill or prompt change is promoted only after an A/B re-test that shows a higher verified-win rate over several attempts, scored on several tasks and requiring every task to be scored (no missing-metric loopholes). Prefer 2-3 focused modules over a comprehensive manual, and prefer scripts with tests (probe render, pairwise compare, ledger update) over prose procedures. Rests on: SkillsBench (Feb 2026); SkillWeaver and Agent Workflow Memory; Voyager skill library (2023); Hyperagents (Mar 2026).
- Tier the models by role and let evidence adjust the mix. Cheap models: static review, statistics reports, first-pass judge calls, the reflector, log summarising, the novelty check. Mid-tier: implementing edits, drafting proposals, one jury seat. Strongest model only for: the periodic meta-review and plateau analysis, adjudicating disputed comparisons (swap disagreement or a conflict between statistics and the judge), the evaluator-integrity audit of each accepted diff, and final merge approval. Add a UCB1-style bandit over (model, task type) that tracks verified improvements per dollar, using ShinkaEvolve's form reward + c*sqrt(2 ln t / n), epsilon 0.2 and cost_aware_coef 0.5, with a hard cost cap. AlphaEvolve's fast-breadth and strong-depth split (one survey infers ~85/15) is a reasonable starting prior, not a published setting. PoLL and RoPoLL support small cheap judges over one big one. Rests on: AlphaEvolve (DeepMind 2025); ShinkaEvolve repo mechanisms; Replacing Judges with Juries (PoLL, 2024); RoPoLL (Jun 2026); SkillsBench (Feb 2026).
- Handle plateaus deliberately. If one domain has no verified improvement after about 8 consecutive evaluations (my proposal; ShinkaEvolve's 100-generation stagnation default assumes thousands of samples), switch domain, start a fresh line from main or the initial state (ShinkaEvolve dynamic islands, CodeEvolve's plateau-based exploration schedule), and ask the strongest model for a non-incremental idea with a fresh prompt. OpenEvolve's circle-packing plateau at 2.377 was broken by a new system prompt and a change of method, not by tuning. Reserve about 20% of attempts for such non-incremental proposals. Stop globally on the render budget, the wall-clock budget, or 3 consecutive domain resets with no gain. Rests on: OpenEvolve prompt memory and circle-packing run; CodeEvolve (Oct 2025); ShinkaEvolve repo mechanisms; AlphaEvolve at scale on 67 math problems (Nov 2025).
- Use tournament ranking only for the archive, not for accept/reject. Accept/reject stays a fixed-baseline pairwise test. After each accepted or near-par entry, run round-robin pairwise judgements among the top ~5 archive entries using stored frames (no new render), fit Bradley-Terry scores with ties as half-wins (start at 1200 Elo as the co-scientist does), and give close pairs the deeper judging (more swapped calls, jury, strong model) while distant pairs get a single swapped call. Fixed-baseline pairwise ranking is unstable because LLM preferences are not transitive. Rests on: Google AI co-scientist (2025, Nature 2026); From Scores to Signs and Non-Transitivity in LLM-as-a-Judge; Rating Roulette (EMNLP Findings 2025).
- Keep the owner in the loop where the judge is weakest. Seed proposers with the owner's realism criteria and reference photos (expert guidance changed AlphaEvolve's results strongly), and have the owner spot-check a contact sheet after every ~5 accepted changes; record judge-owner agreement and treat a drop as a reason to recalibrate. The fleet renders only on the owner's PC and asks before any push to seyedalicheraghi/IsaacSim (CLAUDE.md rules). Judge verdicts are relative rankings, not proof of realism, because the 2026 evidence on VLM judging of synthetic images is weak. Rests on: AlphaEvolve at scale on 67 math problems (Nov 2025); VLM judges on image realism and quality; Google AI co-scientist (2025, Nature 2026).

### Rules the fact-checker says to drop or fix

- Rule 1 (cascade): keep, but cite only OpenEvolve's evaluator.py and the DGM code (both verified). Drop AlphaEvolve and co-scientist as support (cascade and Reflection-agent claims unverifiable). Do not repeat the 'uncaught IndexError' claim (it is caught).
- Rule 2 (single GPU queue): keep, cite only ShinkaEvolve (small_budget = 1 evaluation / 2 proposal jobs, and the 'oversubscription never increases evaluation concurrency' quote are verified). Drop AlphaEvolve's async pipeline as support (unverified).
- Rule 3 (novelty gate): mechanism verified in novelty_judge.py (fails open on judge exception, rejects with no client, 3 attempts). The evidence that it improves sample efficiency is paper-level and unverified. Also require an exact 'NOVEL' token: the repo's startswith('NOVEL') would accept 'NOVELTY...'. Drop AFlow as support (check_modification body was not seen).
- Rule 5 (parent choice): the DGM formula is verified. The Huxley-Godel 'descendants predict value better than the entry's own score' premise (metaproductivity-performance mismatch, CMP) is unverified: only 'estimates of the promise of subtrees' is in its README. Soften it to a hypothesis. Note DGM's 1/(1+children) factor penalises heavily expanded parents, so it needs reconciling with a children-outcome boost.
- Rule 6 (accept only verified improvements): keep, but drop the DGM objective-hacking paper result as evidence (unverified). If citing DGM's keep_better, note the leeway is measured against the original/initial agent's score, not the parent's.
- Rule 7 (pairwise, swapped, ties): the practice is sound design but the numeric evidence is unverified: MT-Bench swap-consistency (65.0 / 23.8%), Judging the Judges, From Scores to Signs (r = 0.390, 73.6%, 17-44%, non-arXiv PDF with unknown authors), VL-RewardBench and Coin Flip. Only the 'over 80% agreement' MT-Bench figure and MLLM-as-a-Judge's existence and task structure are verified. Remove or label the numbers as unverified.
- Rule 8 (spend more judge calls on close pairs): remove the '13.6% mean flip rate / 28% above 20%' (Coin Flip, unverifiable and with an inconsistent date) and 'VL-RewardBench uses K = 5' (unverified; README example uses --k 10). Replace with 'measure the flip rate on our own frames'.
- Rule 9 (jury, median aggregation): rests entirely on PoLL, RoPoLL, Coin Flip (72% A-majority) and self-preference papers, none of which could be opened. Present as an untested hypothesis, drop the quoted numbers, and keep the proposal to test it on the self-test set.
- Rule 10 (rubric plus real reference photo): keep as the report's own proposal; drop the BlenderAlchemy attribution (not verified, my repo URL guess returned 404) and the VLM-realism kappa figures.
- Rule 11 (do not ask the VLM for low-level quality): the supporting evidence is Q-Bench (2024, GPT-4V; its README actually praises GPT-4V as near a junior human) and unopened 2026 snippets. Reframe as an engineering preference (deterministic statistics are exact and cheap), not as an evidence-backed limit of VLMs.
- Rule 12 (image-statistic gates): keep as own design. Drop the KV-cache reward-hacking study (unverifiable) and the AlphaEvolve multi-metric claim as citations.
- Rule 13 (calibrate the judge): keep the procedure, but remove 'against the 13.6% mean' (unverifiable) and the Rating Roulette, CALM and Judging the Judges citations until they can be opened.
- Rule 14 (noise floor): keep. DGM eval_noise 0.1 and ADAS bootstrap CIs are verified. Drop Rating Roulette as support.
- Rule 15 (protect the evaluator): keep. Hyperagents' all-domains-must-be-scored behaviour is verified. 'DGM saw more hacking when checkers were visible' and the KV-cache no-op 'win' are unverified, so do not cite them as findings.
- Rule 16 (held-out poses): ADAS valid_size 128 / test_size 800 is verified. AFlow's validation_rounds = 5 is repeated evaluation of the same candidate, not a held-out split, so do not cite AFlow for the split.
- Rule 17 (ledger): keep. OpenEvolve's last-3 / newest-first / improved-regressed-mixed memory and DGM's per-generation metadata are verified. Correct the details: OpenEvolve attaches artifacts only to the current program (not past attempts), uses just three outcome labels, and the security filter is optional. The extra labels (tie, crashed, duplicate) are the fleet's own.
- Rule 18 (ACE playbook): keep. ACE numbers and defaults are verified from the README and are self-reported. Drop the co-scientist meta-review as support (unverifiable).
- Rule 19 (skills short, human-reviewed, executable): the key evidence (SkillsBench: curated +16.2 pp, self-generated about 0) is unverified and the SkillWeaver / AWM gains are not in their READMEs. Keep the rule as a design preference, remove all SkillsBench numbers, and drop the SkillWeaver/AWM percentages.
- Rule 20 (model tiering): remove 'PoLL and RoPoLL support small cheap judges' and the SkillsBench 'smaller models with skills match larger' support (unverified). ShinkaEvolve's bandit formula is verified, but epsilon 0.2 and exploration_coef 1.0 are Quick Start/example values, not library defaults; only cost_aware_coef 0.5 is a config default. The ~85/15 AlphaEvolve split is already flagged as one survey's inference.
- Rule 21 (plateaus): keep with a fix. OpenEvolve's phase 2 also changed population (60 to 70), islands (4 to 5) and exploitation (0.7 to 0.6), alongside the new prompt and longer code, and the README credits SLSQP, so say 'method change plus reconfiguration', not 'not by tuning'. CodeEvolve's plateau scheduler and ShinkaEvolve's stagnation_threshold 100 are verified. Drop the 'AlphaEvolve at scale' citation (unverified).
- Rule 22 (tournament and Bradley-Terry for the archive): rests on the co-scientist Elo (1200 start), From Scores to Signs and Rating Roulette, all unverifiable. Keep Bradley-Terry as ordinary statistics but do not cite these papers or the 1200 figure as evidence.
- Rule 23 (keep the owner in the loop): the 'expert guidance changed AlphaEvolve's results strongly' evidence and the VLM-realism evidence are unverified. The rule stands on the owner's own rules in CLAUDE.md (render only on this PC, ask before pushing), not on these papers.

### Open questions

- Source access: WebFetch resolved only github.com, raw.githubusercontent.com and storage.googleapis.com in this run (arxiv.org, openreview, huggingface, venue sites, nature.com, sakana.ai all returned ENOTFOUND). The AlphaEvolve white paper PDF fetched from storage.googleapis.com came back as unreadable binary, and the PDF reader needs pdftoppm, which is not installed and which I was not allowed to install. The shared 200-call web search budget ran out before I could verify the remaining items. So 'confirmed_from_primary_source = true' means I read the authors' own repo, README or source code; it does not mean I read the paper. Every paper-level number comes from search-result summaries and is marked false.
- Unconfirmed specifics: AlphaEvolve ablation numbers, the ~85/15 Flash/Pro split (one survey's inference), the 50 vs 67 problem count, the FlashAttention 32.5% figure; DGM compute cost and runtime, and the exact no-archive ablation score (39.7% appears only in a third-party paper); which models formed the PoLL panel; ShinkaEvolve's novelty threshold (0.95 in a paper summary, 0.99 in the docs, 1.0 default in novelty_judge.py); ADAS and AFlow headline tables, and AFlow's 4.55% cost figure; Eureka's ablation outcome; whether Reflexion's buffer holds 1-3 reflections; Self-Refine per-task numbers.
- No study found that measures VLM judges on rendered-versus-real photorealism, which is our core need. The 2026 evidence (kappa 0.37-0.69, under-penalised artifacts, first-position picks by a 4B judge) is indirect and comes only from search snippets I did not open. The fleet needs its own calibration set: owner ratings of renders on this scene, plus synthetic-degradation tests.
- No study found that compares pointwise and pairwise judging while allowing ties, fitting Bradley-Terry and swapping positions in one design. The 6-pose pairwise protocol, the 2-then-escalate call budget and the accept margin are my proposals and should be tuned by measuring the flip rate and judge-owner agreement here.
- PoLL's benefit was attributed to disjoint model families. An all-Claude fleet can vary size, prompt and rubric but not family. Whether that recovers the benefit is unknown. A non-Claude VLM reachable from the owner's PC would be a useful extra jury seat; this was not investigated.
- Does a cheap preview (half resolution, 1-2 poses) predict the full 6-frame verdict well enough to save GPU time? This is empirical for this scene. If correlation is low, the cascade only saves time through the static checks and statistic gates.
- No independent benchmark compares OpenEvolve, ShinkaEvolve, CodeEvolve and AlphaResearch under equal budgets. Sample-efficiency claims (for example ShinkaEvolve's ~150 samples for circle packing) are self-reported on problems with cheap, exact evaluators, unlike a noisy, minutes-long, VLM-judged render. Expect lower sample efficiency here.
- 2026 coverage is thin and snippet-level: Hyperagents (Mar 2026, repo read but results not), Huxley-Godel Machine (ICLR 2026), SkillsBench (Feb 2026), From Scores to Signs (Mar 2026), RoPoLL (Jun 2026), The Coin Flip Judge? (2026), the KV-cache reward-hacking study (Aug 2026) and the VLM realism papers (2603.04325, 2603.27862, 2610.01243). I did not open any of them and did not verify author lists for several. Treat their numbers as leads to check before the owner relies on them.
- Not investigated: concrete numeric thresholds for the image-statistic gates (they need the baseline frames and real reference imagery for Ames, Iowa), render determinism under Isaac Sim 5.1 RTX (which sets the render-noise floor), and whether wider LLM-as-judge mitigations such as multi-agent debate judges or rubric-trained judges beat the simple swap-and-jury design. Also not checked: the hardware limits of running judge calls while the single GPU renders.

### Fact-checker summary

I checked what I could without running code or installing anything, and most of the report's paper-level evidence is still unverified. Only github.com, raw.githubusercontent.com and storage.googleapis.com resolved. arxiv.org, export.arxiv.org, ar5iv, alphaxiv, huggingface, openreview, Semantic Scholar, ACL Anthology, nature.com, deepmind.google, research.google, sakana.ai and several project pages all failed DNS (ENOTFOUND). The WebSearch budget (200 per turn, shared) was already used up, so I could not search by exact title. I did not try to get around either limit. 'source_exists' = false means I could not confirm the source, not that it is fake. Where a source was only partly confirmed, I note what was confirmed in 'correction'.

Verified from authors' repos or READMEs: 5 findings fully supported (OpenEvolve config, ShinkaEvolve repo, ADAS, ACE, GEPA). 15 are partly supported: the repo-level parts match, but paper-level numbers could not be checked. 16 are unverifiable (no usable source): co-scientist, DGM paper numbers, SkillsBench, KV-cache study, Judging the Judges, self-preference, CALM, PoLL, RoPoLL, Rating Roulette, Coin Flip, From Scores to Signs, VL-RewardBench/MMRB/MJ1.

Concrete errors found:
(1) OpenEvolve cascade: the empty-threshold IndexError is caught (outer try in _cascade_evaluate), not uncaught.
(2) DGM keep_better compares with the original/initial agent's score minus leeway, not the parent's score.
(3) DGM's '90% submitted' condition applies to which archive entries set the full-eval threshold (second-highest, floor 0.4), not to the child.
(4) CodeEvolve's README does not compare against OpenEvolve or ShinkaEvolve (ShinkaEvolve is not mentioned).
(5) The '67 problems' is the separate Nov 2025 paper 'Mathematical exploration and discovery at scale', not a second count for the AlphaEvolve white paper.
(6) OpenEvolve prompt memory: previous attempts carry no artifacts, and the security filter is optional.
(7) OpenEvolve circle packing: phase 2 also changed population, islands and exploitation, so 'not by tuning' is overstated.
(8) The Coin Flip entry is internally inconsistent: arXiv ID 2606 vs 'April 2026'.
(9) The Voyager venue 'NeurIPS 2023' is unconfirmed.
(10) Q-Bench's README is more positive about GPT-4V than the report's gloss.
(11) ThetaEvolve's README headline is circle packing, not the 1.5068 vs 1.5032 autocorrelation numbers.
(12) ShinkaEvolve's bandit epsilon 0.2 and exploration_coef 1.0 are Quick Start/example values, not library defaults.

Extra findings from the code read: DGM's CLI choices list has a missing comma, and its 'best' parent option sorts ascending (picks the lowest). ShinkaEvolve's novelty gate accepts when the judge call throws and parses 'NOVEL' by prefix. Hyperagents is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0.

The design rules whose numbers rest on judge-reliability papers (rules 7, 8, 9, 13, 22) and on SkillsBench (rule 19) are the ones to fix first (see 'rules_to_drop_or_fix'). Rules built on verified code (cascade, parent weights, ledger, noise leeway, held-out split, single evaluation queue) are fine once the citations are corrected.

No files were written or pushed in this step. The relayed user request about uploading the agents to the IsaacSim repo is not part of this fact-check.

## realism-metrics

### Rethinking FID: Towards a Better Evaluation Metric for Image Generation (CMMD paper)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.09603 (Jayasumana et al., Google Research, arXiv Jan 2024; CVPR Jun 2024, CVPR 2024)
- **Claim:** Argues FID is unreliable for modern generators: Inception features represent varied content poorly, the Gaussian/normality assumption is wrong, and sample complexity is poor; FID contradicts human raters, misses distortion levels and shifts with sample size. Proposes CMMD = maximum mean discrepancy with a Gaussian RBF kernel on CLIP embeddings; the paper describes it as unbiased, distribution-free and sample-efficient, with a sample-size experiment (Stable Diffusion on COCO 30K, Fig. 8). Read via search-result abstract/HTML summaries only; paper body was not fetched (arxiv.org and cvf hosts did not resolve).
- **Numbers:** none read from the paper body; no minimum sample size is stated in the README
- **For the fleet:** CMMD-style MMD on frozen-encoder embeddings is the right family for a loop with few images, because it needs no covariance estimate (FID needs n well above 2048 for a 2048-D covariance). But the paper's 'unbiased' claim does not match the released code (see CMMD reference implementations), so re-implement it as a true unbiased U-statistic.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** arxiv.org, openaccess.thecvf.com, openreview and Semantic Scholar did not resolve, so the abstract and body were not opened. Existence, title, arXiv ID 2401.09603 and authors are confirmed second-hand by the Google CMMD README: 'Rethinking FID: Towards a Better Evaluation Metric for Image Generation', Jayasumana, Ramalingam, Veit, Glasner, Chakrabarti, Kumar. That README gives no venue and makes no 'unbiased' or 'sample efficient' statement. Unverified: CVPR 2024 venue, 'unbiased/distribution-free/sample-efficient' wording, Fig. 8 sample-size experiment.

### CMMD reference implementations (google-research JAX and cmmd-pytorch)

- **Source:** https://github.com/google-research/google-research/tree/master/cmmd (Google Research; sayakpaul/cmmd-pytorch port, files carry 2024 and 2026 copyright headers; port repo date unconfirmed, GitHub (Apache-2.0 both))
- **Claim:** JAX reference: CLIP ViT-L/14@336px via scenic, bicubic resize, RBF kernel sigma=10, output scaled x1000, batch_size 32, max_count -1 (all images). The distance.py docstring says it implements the minimum-variance/BIASED MMD estimator (Gretton 2012 Eq. 5): it averages kernel values over all n^2 pairs including the diagonal, even though the paper calls CMMD unbiased. The PyTorch port uses transformers CLIP (openai/clip-vit-large-patch14-336), L2-normalises embeddings, and reproduces the JAX value 7.696 on its bundled example. OUR DERIVATION (not from the literature): with unit-norm embeddings, gamma*d^2 <= 0.02, so to first order the statistic equals 10*||mean_x - mean_y||^2 (empirical means); finite-sample noise therefore adds a floor of about 10*(tr(Sigma_x)/n + tr(Sigma_y)/m). With an assumed tr(Sigma) of about 0.3 (to be measured), n=8 probe vs m=2000 reference gives a floor near 0.37, comparable to or larger than published model-to-model CMMD gaps; n=256, m=2000 gives about 0.013.
- **Numbers:** sigma=10; gamma=1/200; scale=1000; reference model CLIP ViT-L/14@336; example output 7.696; COCO-30k CMMD in cmmd-pytorch README: SDXL base (30 steps, 1024px) 0.696, SSD-1B 0.669, SDXL Turbo (512px) 0.548, SD v1.5 (50 steps, 512px) 0.582, PixArt-XL-2-1024-MS 1.140, SPRIGHT-T2I SD2 0.512
- **For the fleet:** Do not use the released biased code for a handful of images. Re-implement as the unbiased estimator (drop the diagonal terms of k_xx and k_yy), keep sigma=10 and x1000 scale so numbers stay comparable with the literature, and measure the real-vs-real noise floor on your own reference bank. Note the near-linear kernel makes the score mostly a mean-embedding shift; it is weak at seeing diversity differences, which is acceptable for a single-scene loop.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Confirmed in distance.py: _SIGMA=10, _SCALE=1000, gamma=1/(2*sigma^2)=1/200, mean over full n x n kernel matrices (diagonal included), docstring says 'minimum-variance/biased version ... Eq.(5)' of Gretton 2012, 2026 copyright header. Docstring also says 'the unbiased estimate and the minimum-variance estimate for MMD are almost identical' (Lemma 6), which the report omits. main.py: batch_size default 32, max_count default -1. README: ViT-L/14@336px via scenic. Port (sayakpaul/cmmd-pytorch): openai/clip-vit-large-patch14-336, bicubic interpolate, L2 normalisation, Apache-2.0, 7.696 example, COCO-30k table (0.696, 0.669, 0.548, 0.582, 1.140, 0.512) all match. Not opened: JAX embedding.py (bicubic resize for the JAX version is inferred from the port). Own derivation re-checked and correct to first order: with unit-norm embeddings MMD^2 ~ 0.01*||mean_x-mean_y||^2, so x1000 gives 10*||dmu||^2; floors 10*(0.3/8+0.3/2000)=0.376 and 10*(0.3/256+0.3/2000)=0.013. These depend on the unmeasured tr(Sigma)=0.3 and are hypotheses. The diagonal term is a positive offset (bias) that is constant for fixed n and m; the variance remains.

### Exposing flaws of generative model evaluation metrics (FD-DINOv2 paper)

- **Source:** https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2023/hash/0bc795afae289ed465a65a3b4b1f4eb7-Abstract-Conference.html (Stein et al., Layer 6 AI, arXiv Jun 2023; NeurIPS Dec 2023, NeurIPS 2023 (main track))
- **Claim:** Large human realism study: no existing metric strongly tracks perceived realism; diffusion models are penalised by Inception-V3-based metrics; DINOv2-ViT-L/14 as the feature extractor (FD-DINOv2) supports a much richer evaluation; generative models memorise on CIFAR10 but not necessarily on ImageNet. Released generated datasets, human-evaluation data and the dgm-eval library. Read via search-result summaries only.
- **Numbers:** none read from the paper body (see dgm-eval library for README counts)
- **For the fleet:** Never use Inception-feature FID. If a Frechet-type distance is wanted, use DINOv2 features, but it still needs n well above the feature dimension (1024 for ViT-L/14, 768 for ViT-B/14), so with a handful of renders use it only on pooled patch embeddings with the same n every round, as a relative trend, with bootstrap CIs.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** Full title per the dgm-eval README citation is 'Exposing flaws of generative model evaluation metrics and their unfair treatment of diffusion models' (Stein, Cresswell, Hosseinzadeh, Sui, Ross, Villecroze, Liu, Caterini, Taylor, Loaiza-Ganem), NeurIPS 2023 vol. 36. The report title is truncated. proceedings.neurips.cc did not resolve, so the human-study findings, 'memorise on CIFAR10 but not ImageNet' and the arXiv Jun 2023 date are unverified here. The README does support the DINOv2 recommendation and the 41-model study.

### dgm-eval library (FD-DINOv2, KD, Realism, Density/Coverage)

- **Source:** https://github.com/layer6ai-labs/dgm-eval (Layer 6 AI, unconfirmed, GitHub, MIT License)
- **Claim:** README: studies 41 generative models and states 'Researchers should replace Inception-V3 in all evaluation metrics', recommending DINOv2. Metrics listed include FD, FD-infinity, sFID, kernel distance, Inception Score, FLS, precision/recall, density/coverage, Vendi, AuthPct, CT score, Realism, approximate sliced Wasserstein. Eight encoders (Inception, ConvNeXt, SimCLRv2, SwAV, CLIP, DINOv2, MAE, data2vec). Default DINOv2-ViT-L/14 for final values; ViT-B/14 (--arch vitb14) suggested for progress tracking, about 4x more efficient. Examples use --nsample 512 (quick) and --nsample 50000 (heatmaps); no minimum n is stated. Install by git clone + pip install -e . (Python 3.10 suggested).
- **Numbers:** 41 models; 14 metrics listed (README text says 15); 8 encoders; 512 and 50000 as example nsample values; ViT-B/14 about 4x faster than ViT-L/14
- **For the fleet:** Good source of tested per-sample fidelity metrics (Realism score, Density, Coverage) that score generated samples against a large real bank, which is the only realistic way to score few renders. Use DINOv2-B/14 inside the loop and L/14 only for end-of-session reports. Not on PyPI as far as checked, so vendor by git commit in a separate venv.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** Confirmed on the README: 41 models; quote 'Researchers should replace Inception-V3 in all evaluation metrics'; says 15 metrics but lists 14 (FD, FD-infinity, sFID, KD, IS, FLS, P&R, D&C, Vendi, AuthPct, CT score, FLS-POG, Realism, ASW); 8 encoders; DINOv2-ViT-L/14 default; --arch vitb14 'a factor of 4 more efficient' for tracking progress; nsample 512 and 50000 examples; no minimum n; python 3.10; MIT. The 'not on PyPI' remark was not checked.

### Effectively Unbiased FID and Inception Score (FID bias)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/1911.07023 (Chong and Forsyth, Nov 2019 (arXiv); CVPR Jun 2020, CVPR 2020)
- **Claim:** FID and IS computed on finite samples are biased; the bias depends on the model being evaluated, so model A can beat model B only because its bias term is smaller, and fixing N does not remove this. They extrapolate to N to infinity (FID-infinity, IS-infinity) and use quasi-Monte Carlo integration for better finite-sample estimates. A secondary source in my searches says at least 10k samples are usually recommended and 5k is still biased (origin of that statement not verified).
- **Numbers:** none
- **For the fleet:** FID/FD values from different n, or from a render whose features have different spread, are not comparable. In a loop with 4-16 frames per round use a fixed n and a fixed reference bank every round, and prefer an unbiased statistic (MMD U-statistic, KID) whose bias does not depend on the model.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** arxiv.org did not resolve; existence is unconfirmed here (not evidence it does not exist). The title as I recall it is 'Effectively Unbiased FID and Inception Score and where to find them', so the report title is shortened. The CVPR 2020 venue and the FID-infinity extrapolation could not be checked. The '10k+ samples recommended, 5k still biased' statement is, per the report, of unverified origin and is also unverified here.

### FID/KID tooling: clean-fid, torch-fidelity, piq

- **Source:** https://pypi.org/project/torch-fidelity/ (Parmar (clean-fid); Obukhov (torch-fidelity); photosynthesis-team (piq), torch-fidelity 0.4.0 released 2026-02-17; piq 0.8.0 released 2023-07-04; clean-fid version unconfirmed, PyPI / GitHub)
- **Claim:** torch-fidelity 0.4.0 (Apache-2.0) offers ISC, FID, KID, precision/recall with feature extractors InceptionV3, CLIP, DINOv2 and VGG16 and custom extractors; its README says values match reference implementations to machine precision. clean-fid (MIT) offers FID, KID and CLIP ViT-B/32 FID, with precomputed KID stats only for small datasets (AFHQ, BreCaHAD, MetFaces) and custom stats via make_custom_stats; its leaderboards average 10 evaluation runs. piq 0.8.0 (Apache-2.0) implements FR (PSNR, SSIM, MS-SSIM, FSIM, VSI, HaarPSI, LPIPS, DISTS...), NR (TV, BRISQUE, CLIP-IQA) and distribution metrics (IS, FID, GS, KID, MSID, PR). None of the pages states KID kernel or subset size or a minimum n; from memory (unconfirmed this session) KID uses a cubic polynomial kernel, an unbiased estimator and subsets of 1000.
- **Numbers:** none beyond versions and dates
- **For the fleet:** KID with DINOv2 or CLIP features from torch-fidelity is a ready-made unbiased small-n metric; set the KID subset size no larger than the smaller sample and report the spread over subsets. piq is a permissive-licence source of BRISQUE and CLIP-IQA without the pyiqa non-commercial licence.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** torch-fidelity PyPI: 0.4.0, 17 Feb 2026, Apache-2.0, InceptionV3/CLIP/DINOv2/VGG16, ISC/FID/KID/PRC; GitHub README: values 'match reference implementations up to floating point's machine precision', no KID kernel or subset size, no minimum n. piq PyPI: 0.8.0, 4 Jul 2023, Apache, FR/NR (TV, BRISQUE, CLIP-IQA)/DB (IS, FID, GS, KID, MSID, PR) as stated. clean-fid GitHub: MIT, KID, clip_vit_b_32 FID, precomputed KID stats only for AFHQ, BreCaHAD, MetFaces, make_custom_stats, leaderboards from 10 runs. clean-fid's PyPI page failed to load, so its release date stays unconfirmed. Licence caveat missing from the report: the Inception torchscript model bundled with clean-fid is under NVIDIA's Source Code License. The KID defaults (cubic polynomial kernel, subsets of 1000) are recalled from memory and unconfirmed.

### Feature Likelihood Divergence: KID stays consistent at small test sizes

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/pdf/2302.04440 (Jiralerspong et al., Feb 2023, NeurIPS 2023 (venue not verified in this session))
- **Claim:** Per a search-result summary, FLD and KID appear relatively consistent even for smaller test-set sizes while FID is clearly biased there. Secondary evidence only; paper body not read.
- **Numbers:** none
- **For the fleet:** Supports choosing KID/unbiased MMD over FID for small-n loops, but verify empirically with a real-vs-real subsampling curve on your own embeddings.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** arxiv.org did not resolve. The heading is not a paper title. The paper at arXiv 2302.04440, as I recall it, is 'Feature Likelihood Divergence: Evaluating the Generalization of Generative Models Using Samples' (Jiralerspong et al., NeurIPS 2023). The claim that KID is consistent at small test sizes rests only on a search summary and was not checked.

### LPIPS (Learned Perceptual Image Patch Similarity)

- **Source:** https://github.com/richzhang/PerceptualSimilarity (Zhang, Isola, Efros, Shechtman, Wang, CVPR 2018; PyPI lpips 0.1.4 released 2021-08-25, CVPR 2018; PyPI package lpips)
- **Claim:** pip install lpips (BSD-2-clause per the repo LICENSE; PyPI says BSD). Input RGB tensors Nx3xHxW scaled to [-1,1]; lower = more similar. net='alex' is the default and the README calls it fastest and best as a forward metric; net='vgg' is closer to a traditional perceptual loss; lpips=True adds the learned linear calibration. README quick start uses 64x64 patches; current version 0.1 and users are asked to state the version.
- **Numbers:** none
- **For the fleet:** LPIPS(alex) between the last accepted render and the candidate render of the SAME frame (same pose, time, sensor-noise seed) measures how much an edit changed pixels-level appearance; use it as a change-magnitude and unintended-regression guard, not as a realism score (it needs a paired reference).
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** README: pip install lpips, RGB Nx3xHxW normalised to [-1,1], net=alex default described as fastest and best as a forward metric, vgg closer to a traditional perceptual loss (for backprop), BSD-2-Clause, current version 0.1, quick start uses 64x64 tensors, a third option squeeze exists. PyPI: lpips 0.1.4, 25 Aug 2021, BSD. The CVPR 2018 venue was not on the fetched pages.

### DreamSim

- **Source:** https://pypi.org/project/dreamsim/ (Fu, Tamir, Sundaram, Chai, Zhang, Dekel, Isola (MIT, Weizmann, Adobe), arXiv Jun 2023 (v3 8 Dec 2023); PyPI 0.2.1 released 2024-10-15, NeurIPS 2023)
- **Claim:** Perceptual similarity trained on human judgements of synthetic triplets (NIGHTS); captures mid-level layout/pose/semantics that pixel-patch metrics miss. pip install dreamsim (MIT, Linux, Python 3). Default model is an ensemble of CLIP, DINO and OpenCLIP ViT-B/16; single-branch variants (OpenCLIP-ViTB/32, DINO-ViTB/16, CLIP-ViTB/32) are about 3x faster; DINOv2 B/14 and SynCLR B/16 backbones added 14 Oct 2024. Input RGB in [0,1], 224x224 via the provided preprocess; model(img1,img2) returns a distance; model.embed() gives embeddings. Weights download on first run.
- **Numbers:** NIGHTS 2AFC agreement of the ensemble: 96.9% validation, 96.2% test
- **For the fleet:** Pair with LPIPS as a second change-magnitude guard that is robust to small shifts; run on 224x224 crops or downsized full frames; do not use it as a standalone realism metric (it is full-reference). Pre-download weights on the owner PC because the run environment may be offline.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** PyPI: dreamsim 0.2.1, 15 Oct 2024, MIT, authors Fu, Tamir, Sundaram, Chai, Zhang, Dekel, Isola; default ensemble of CLIP, DINO and OpenCLIP ViT-B/16; single-branch models (OpenCLIP-ViTB/32, DINO-ViTB/16, CLIP-ViTB/32) ~3x faster; 14 Oct 2024 added DINOv2 B/14 and SynCLR B/16; NIGHTS ensemble 96.9/96.2. GitHub README: RGB in [0,1] at 224x224, higher = more different, embed() exists, Linux, NeurIPS 2023 (Spotlight). Not checked: the affiliations and the arXiv Jun 2023 / v3 Dec 2023 dates.

### IQA-PyTorch (pyiqa) toolbox and NR benchmark

- **Source:** https://github.com/chaofengc/IQA-PyTorch (Chaofeng Chen et al. (NTU S-Lab), pyiqa 0.1.16 released 2026-07-08, GitHub/PyPI)
- **Claim:** One interface to FR metrics (lpips, ssim, psnr, dmm...), NR metrics (niqe, brisque, musiq variants, clipiqa/clipiqa+, topiq_nr, arniqa, qalign, qualiclip, qrealign mini/lite/pro [needs transformers>=5.0], fgresq, maclip, afine) and FID/sFID. LICENCE: PolyForm Noncommercial 1.0.0 (any noncommercial purpose permitted, including personal research), plus NTU S-Lab licence for some components; commercial use is not permitted. Benchmark CSV (PLCC/SRCC/KRCC) shows classic NSS metrics are weak on camera-photo MOS datasets while learned/VLM metrics are strong.
- **Numbers:** PLCC/SRCC. NIQE: LIVEC 0.480/0.451, KonIQ 0.316/0.377, SPAQ 0.669/0.693. BRISQUE: LIVEC 0.351/0.313, KonIQ 0.211/0.230, SPAQ 0.533/0.526. MUSIQ-koniq: LIVEC 0.830/0.789, KonIQ 0.896/0.865 (in-domain), SPAQ 0.863/0.868. CLIP-IQA: LIVEC 0.688/0.696, KonIQ 0.721/0.657. CLIP-IQA+: LIVEC 0.831/0.805, KonIQ 0.845/0.803. TOPIQ-NR: LIVEC 0.826/0.811, KonIQ 0.944/0.930 (in-domain), SPAQ 0.874/0.870. Q-Align: LIVEC 0.894/0.881; qalign_8bit SPAQ 0.936/0.932
- **For the fleet:** Useful for a drop-detector bundle (MUSIQ, CLIP-IQA+, TOPIQ-NR, Q-Align 4/8-bit) but keep it in a separate venv and do not vendor it into the repo; reimplement NIQE-style statistics in numpy so the core stays permissively licensed. Remember all of these are trained on camera-photo MOS, not realism.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** PyPI: pyiqa 0.1.16, 8 Jul 2026, PolyForm Noncommercial 1.0.0 plus NTU S-Lab License. README confirms qrealign mini/lite/pro (0.8B/4B/9B, Qwen3.5-VL backbone) and 'Requires transformers>=5.0', and fgresq, maclip, afine, dmm, sfid, qualiclip. The benchmark CSV (tests/NR_benchmark_results.csv) matches every number the report quotes: NIQE 0.480/0.451, 0.316/0.377, 0.669/0.693; BRISQUE 0.351/0.313, 0.211/0.230, 0.533/0.526; MUSIQ-koniq 0.830/0.789, 0.896/0.865, 0.863/0.868; CLIP-IQA 0.688/0.696, 0.721/0.657; CLIP-IQA+ 0.831/0.805, 0.845/0.803; TOPIQ-NR 0.826/0.811, 0.944/0.930, 0.874/0.870; Q-Align 0.894/0.881; qalign_8bit SPAQ 0.936/0.932. Per the README, the NR models are trained on KonIQ-10k and tested on LIVEC, KonIQ and SPAQ, which fits the 'in-domain' notes.

### NIQE and BRISQUE (background knowledge, not verified this session)

- **Source:** https://github.com/chaofengc/IQA-PyTorch (Mittal, Soundararajan, Bovik (NIQE, 2013); Mittal, Moorthy, Bovik (BRISQUE, 2012), 2012-2013, IEEE Signal Processing Letters (NIQE); IEEE TIP (BRISQUE) - from memory, unconfirmed)
- **Claim:** Both are built on mean-subtracted contrast-normalised (MSCN) coefficients and generalised-Gaussian fits. NIQE is opinion-unaware: it fits a multivariate Gaussian to patch features of pristine natural photographs and scores deviation. Web search was exhausted before I could verify the originals; the only verified facts here are the pyiqa benchmark numbers (SRCC on KonIQ 0.377 NIQE / 0.230 BRISQUE) and that pyiqa marks both lower-is-better and 'no backward'.
- **Numbers:** see IQA-PyTorch benchmark numbers
- **For the fleet:** Re-fit the NIQE multivariate Gaussian on a bank of REAL aerial/drone patches (numpy + scipy) to get a domain-matched 'aerial NIQE' of MSCN statistics; the stock NIQE model is fitted on ground-level photographs and is a poor judge of aerial texture.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** The original papers were not opened. The benchmark numbers (KonIQ SRCC 0.377 NIQE, 0.230 BRISQUE) are verified against the pyiqa CSV. The MSCN / generalised-Gaussian / opinion-unaware description is standard but unchecked here, as are the venues and the 'lower is better / no backward' flags. The cited URL (pyiqa) is not a source for the original papers.

### Q-Align and Q-ReAlign

- **Source:** https://github.com/Q-Future/Q-Align (Wu et al. (Q-Future), arXiv Dec 2023; ICML Jul 2024; README mentions Q-ReAlign (date unconfirmed), ICML 2024 (PMLR 235:54015-54029))
- **Claim:** Trains an LMM to output discrete text rating levels and converts level probabilities to a weighted-average score; OneAlign (HF q-future/one-align) scores quality and aesthetics for images/video. Base model mPLUG-Owl2; code pinned to transformers==4.36.1. README says Q-ReAlign is a retrained version on Qwen3.5-VL matching performance with 50% fewer parameters. Code licence: S-Lab License 1.0 (non-commercial; commercial use requires contacting the authors). README gives no inference memory figure (training needs 2x RTX3090 for LoRA).
- **Numbers:** pyiqa table: Q-Align LIVEC PLCC/SRCC 0.894/0.881; qalign_8bit SPAQ 0.936/0.932
- **For the fleet:** Q-Align gives the best correlation with human MOS among the options in the pyiqa table, but it is a 7B VLM that must not share the GPU with Isaac Sim; run it only after the render process exits, with pyiqa's qalign_4bit/8bit if VRAM is tight, and only as a quality drop-detector.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** README: ICML 2024 banner; OneAlign at q-future/one-align (IQA, IAA, VQA); the update targets transformers==4.36.1 (old code 4.31.0, v1.0.x incompatible); Q-ReAlign 'trained on modern VLMs and gains the same performance with 50% fewer parameters', linked retrain on Qwen3.5-VL; LoRA fine-tuning of OneAlign needs 2x RTX 3090; mPLUG-Owl2 code modified. LICENSE file is 'S-Lab License 1.0' (non-commercial redistribution and use, contact authors for commercial). The README itself does not name a licence. The README BibTeX lists arXiv 2312.17090 (2023), not ICML. The PMLR 235:54015-54029 pages are unverified. The 'must not share the GPU with Isaac Sim' point is a design choice, not a sourced fact.

### DeQA-Score

- **Source:** https://github.com/zhiyuanyou/DeQA-Score (You, Cai, Gu, Xue, Dong, arXiv Jan 2025; CVPR Jun 2025, CVPR 2025 (pp. 14483-14494))
- **Claim:** Replaces one-hot score tokens with a Gaussian-derived soft-label score distribution and a Thurstone-style fidelity loss; improves score regression (about +1.3% PLCC on KonIQ, +4.6% on LIVE-WILD per search summary). README: LoRA on mPLUG-Owl2-llama2-7b, trained on KonIQ, SPAQ, KADID; HF zhiyuanyou/DeQA-Score-Mix3; supports transformers 4.36.1 and newer with minor changes; no licence stated; no inference memory figure.
- **Numbers:** +1.3% PLCC KonIQ; +4.6% PLCC LIVE-WILD (secondary summary)
- **For the fleet:** Another optional VLM scorer; adds a score-distribution output, which gives an uncertainty estimate per image. Same limits as other IQA: trained on camera-photo MOS, not realism.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** Confirmed: authors You, Cai, Gu, Xue, Dong; CVPR 2025 pp. 14483-14494; LoRA on mPLUG-Owl2 llama2-7b; trained on KonIQ, SPAQ, KADID; HF zhiyuanyou/DeQA-Score-Mix3; transformers 4.36.1 plus later versions (4.46.3, 4.57.6). WRONG: the report says 'no licence stated'. The repo is MIT. It is described as based on Q-Align and built on mPLUG-Owl2, so downstream terms still need a check. Not on the README: '+1.3% PLCC KonIQ / +4.6% LIVE-WILD' (unverified) and the 'Thurstone-style fidelity loss' wording.

### Q-Insight family and VisualQuality-R1 (reasoning IQA, 2025-2026)

- **Source:** https://github.com/bytedance/Q-Insight (ByteDance/Peking Univ. (Q-Insight, VQ-Insight, RALI); Tianhe Wu, Jian Zou, Jie Liang, Lei Zhang, Kede Ma (VisualQuality-R1), Q-Insight arXiv Mar 2025 (2503.22679); VisualQuality-R1 arXiv May 2025 (2505.14460), Q-Insight NeurIPS 2025 spotlight; VQ-Insight AAAI 2026 oral; RALI ICLR 2026 oral; VisualQuality-R1 NeurIPS 2025)
- **Claim:** RL-trained (GRPO) VLM quality assessors that output a reasoning trace plus a score; Q-Insight supports score regression, degradation perception and pairwise comparison; VisualQuality-R1 trains with reinforcement learning to rank using a Thurstone comparison model and reports beating discriminative NR-IQA models. RALI is a CLIP-based scorer using about 4% of Q-Insight's parameters and inference time at comparable accuracy (README claim). Repo states no licence, base model or GPU requirement.
- **Numbers:** RALI about 4% of Q-Insight's parameters and inference time (README)
- **For the fleet:** Pairwise 'which image has better quality, and why' prompts from these models match the fleet's A/B round structure better than absolute scores, but they still measure photographic quality not realism; treat as optional and benchmark on known degradations before trusting.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** Confirmed on the bytedance/Q-Insight README: arXiv 2503.22679, NeurIPS 2025 spotlight, VQ-Insight arXiv 2506.18564 AAAI 2026 oral, RALI arXiv 2510.11369 ICLR 2026 oral, RALI uses about 4% of Q-Insight's parameters and inference time at comparable accuracy. TianheWu/VisualQuality-R1 README: 'Reasoning-Induced Image Quality Assessment via Reinforcement Learning to Rank', Wu, Zou, Liang, Zhang, Ma, arXiv 2505.14460, NeurIPS 2025 spotlight. WRONG: the report says the repo 'states no licence'. Q-Insight is Apache-2.0 and VisualQuality-R1 is Apache-2.0. The README does not name a base model (correct as reported). Unverified: GRPO, the Thurstone comparison model (not mentioned on the README), and the pairwise-comparison feature list.

### CLIP-IQA

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2207.12396 (Wang, Chan, Loy (NTU S-Lab), arXiv Jul 2022; AAAI 2023, AAAI 2023 (37(2):2555-2563))
- **Claim:** Zero-shot-style assessment of 'look' (quality) and 'feel' (abstract attributes) using CLIP with antonym prompt pairs; code at IceClear/CLIP-IQA; available as clipiqa and clipiqa+ in pyiqa and as CLIP-IQA in piq (Apache-2.0).
- **Numbers:** pyiqa: CLIP-IQA+ LIVEC PLCC/SRCC 0.831/0.805
- **For the fleet:** Antonym prompt pairs can be repurposed cheaply for realism-relevant attributes (e.g. 'a photo' vs 'a rendering', 'hazy' vs 'crisp') on CLIP embeddings already computed for CMMD; calibrate on real-vs-sim before trusting.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** The GitHub repo IceClear/CLIP-IQA confirms the title 'Exploring CLIP for Assessing the Look and Feel of Images', authors Jianyi Wang, Kelvin C.K. Chan, Chen Change Loy (S-Lab, NTU) and AAAI 2023. arXiv 2207.12396 and the AAAI page numbers could not be opened. The antonym-prompt mechanism was not checked. Omitted by the report: the original code is under the NTU S-Lab License 1.0 (non-commercial). piq's CLIP-IQA is Apache-2.0 per the piq PyPI page. Its CLIP-IQA+ numbers are confirmed from the pyiqa CSV.

### Natural-image power-spectrum statistics

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/pdf/2408.10616 (Torralba and Oliva (2003); toolbox paper arXiv 2408.10616; other reviews, 2003 (Torralba and Oliva); 2024 (toolbox), Network: Computation in Neural Systems (2003); arXiv)
- **Claim:** Natural images have amplitude spectra A(f) ~ 1/f^alpha. Sources in my search: an older tech report gives power slope about 2 (amplitude slope near 1); a review gives amplitude slope 0.8-1.5 with peak near 1.2 (power slope about 2.4); Torralba and Oliva find alpha and amplitude vary with orientation and with environment type (man-made vs natural). Matching the 1/f^2 power spectrum is not sufficient: phase-randomised images keep the spectrum but look unnatural. No study directly comparing aerial or rendered imagery spectra was found. All via search-result summaries.
- **Numbers:** amplitude slope about 1 (power about 2) typical; 0.8-1.5 range, peak 1.2 (power about 2.4) in one review
- **For the fleet:** The spectral slope is a cheap necessary-not-sufficient check; compare against slopes measured on your own REAL aerial reference bank per scene type and per orientation, not against a universal constant like 2.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** arXiv 2408.10616 and the other sources were not opened (arxiv.org unreachable, no search budget). The numbers (amplitude slope ~1 / power ~2; 0.8-1.5 with a peak near 1.2; orientation and environment dependence) rest on search summaries and are unconfirmed. 'Torralba and Oliva (2003)' as a Network: Computation in Neural Systems paper is not confirmed here.

### Fourier Spectrum Discrepancies in Deep Network Generated Images

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/1911.06465 (Dzanic, Shah, Witherden, Nov 2019 (arXiv); NeurIPS Dec 2020, NeurIPS 2020)
- **Claim:** Generated images systematically fail to reproduce high-frequency Fourier attributes. Fitting a 2-parameter exponential decay (magnitude, decay rate) to the high-frequency spectrum lets an SVM separate real from generated with up to 99.2% accuracy across GAN/VAE architectures, with as few as 8 training examples on a 5,000-image dataset. Spectral synthesis can fool such classifiers, and 'A Closer Look at Fourier Spectrum Discrepancies' (arXiv 2103.17195) reports the detector can be bypassed by changing only the final scaling step. Via search summaries.
- **Numbers:** 99.2% accuracy; 8 training examples; 5,000-image dataset
- **For the fleet:** A tiny classifier on spectral-tail features is sample-efficient enough for a loop with few images and is a good fast 'is this render's HF statistics real-like' check. Because it can be gamed, never optimise a render directly to minimise it; keep it as one of several independent guards.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** The Dzanic et al. paper was not opened. Indirectly corroborated by the repo of the follow-up (A Closer Look at Fourier Spectrum Discrepancies for CNN-generated Images Detection, Chandrasegaran, Tran, Cheung, CVPR 2021 Oral, pp. 7200-7209), which says earlier work detected CNN-generated images with up to 99% accuracy through high-frequency spectral decay. Correction: that follow-up says the discrepancy 'can be avoided by a minor architecture change in the last upsampling operation', not 'changing only the final scaling step'. Unverified: 99.2%, 8 training examples, 5,000-image dataset, NeurIPS 2020, arXiv IDs.

Only the abstract was seen.

### FADE fog density predictor

- **Source:** https://www.colorado.edu/lab/live/perceptual-fog-density-assessment-and-image-defogging-research-live (Choi, You, Bovik (UT Austin LIVE), Nov 2015, IEEE Trans. Image Processing 24(11):3888-3901)
- **Claim:** Referenceless Fog Aware Density Evaluator from natural-scene-statistics fog-aware features; needs no reference image, depth, transmission map or human-rated training data; gives a whole-image score and a per-patch fog-density index; correlates with human fog-density judgements; code and LIVE Image Defogging Database released for public use. Licence of the code not checked.
- **Numbers:** none
- **For the fleet:** A single-image haze-density proxy usable on real footage that has no depth; compute it on real oblique drone frames and on sim frames to compare the amount of aerial perspective without needing a depth map.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** colorado.edu did not resolve. The Choi, You, Bovik IEEE TIP 24(11):3888-3901 (2015) citation and the code-release claim were not checked. The licence was not checked either, as the report notes.

### Dark channel prior and atmospheric scattering model

- **Source:** https://mmlab.ie.cuhk.edu.hk/archive/2011/Haze.pdf (He, Sun, Tang, CVPR 2009; TPAMI 2011, CVPR 2009 / IEEE TPAMI 2011)
- **Claim:** Haze model I(x)=J(x)t(x)+A(1-t(x)) with transmission t and airlight A; most local patches of haze-free outdoor images contain pixels with very low intensity in some colour channel (dark channel), so haze thickness and a depth map by-product can be estimated from one image. The relation t=exp(-beta d) (Koschmieder) was not stated explicitly in the snippets I saw; the exponential form is standard physics and is used below as our own arithmetic: visibility V=3.912/beta; V=20 km gives beta about 0.196 /km and t(2 km) about 0.68; V=40 km gives beta about 0.098 /km and t(2 km) about 0.82.
- **Numbers:** V=3.912/beta (2% contrast threshold): beta 0.196 /km at V=20 km, t(2 km)=0.68; beta 0.098 /km at V=40 km, t(2 km)=0.82 (our arithmetic)
- **For the fleet:** Per-distance-bin dark-channel mean, RMS contrast and colour shift toward the sky colour, using the Replicator depth AOV in the sim, give a direct measurement of aerial perspective along the 2 km flight; fit beta and compare with a clear-day visibility prior. For real footage without depth, use the flat-ground row-to-distance mapping d = h/tan(angle below horizon) or the dark-channel/FADE proxies.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** The CUHK PDF did not resolve, so the paper text and venues (CVPR 2009, TPAMI 2011) were not checked. The report's own arithmetic is correct: V=3.912/beta (ln 50 for the 2% threshold); V=20 km gives beta=0.196/km and t(2 km)=0.676; V=40 km gives beta=0.098/km and t(2 km)=0.822.

### Camera simulation for robot simulation: which camera-model components matter

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/pdf/2211.08599 (Elmquist, Serban, Negrut (Univ. Wisconsin-Madison), Nov 2022, arXiv preprint (venue unconfirmed))
- **Claim:** Ablates camera-model parts (in-camera: lens distortion, noise, signal processing; scene: lighting, reflection; rendering quality) and measures sim-vs-real image differences through a perception algorithm: large-scale changes to colour, scene and location have far greater impact than local feature-level artefacts; white-balance and auto-exposure modelling are singled out. Per-component numbers were not seen.
- **Numbers:** none seen
- **For the fleet:** Fix global colour, exposure, white balance and tone first (mean scene radiance vs the 18% grey card line in the drone_scene log, sky/ground luminance ratio, saturation), before spending rounds on fine textures, lens distortion or noise.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** arXiv 2211.08599 was not opened. The title looks like a paraphrase; as I recall the paper (Elmquist, Serban, Negrut) is titled 'Camera Simulation for Robot Simulation: How Important Are Various Camera Model Components?'. Treat the title as unverified. The claim that global colour, scene and location matter more than local artefacts was not checked. Even if true, it was measured through one perception algorithm and does not directly transfer to a realism score.

### SEDD style-based sim-to-real gap metric

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10203 (unconfirmed (autonomous-driving sim-to-real profiling authors), arXiv Oct 2025; v3 2026-01-26, arXiv (title changed from 'A Style-Based Metric...' to 'A Style-Based Profiling Framework for Quantifying the Synthetic-to-Real Gap in Autonomous Driving Datasets'))
- **Claim:** No-reference Style Embedding Distribution Discrepancy: Gram-matrix style extraction plus metric learning; ranks Virtual KITTI 2 above Virtual KITTI in fidelity and quantifies gap reduction from sim-to-real translation; argues that traditional NR-IQA can score clear but unrealistic synthetic images too highly and that reference-based metrics need matched real references that simulators rarely have. Abstract-level only.
- **Numbers:** none seen
- **For the fleet:** Directly supports not using IQA scores as the realism objective, and suggests a style-statistics (Gram-matrix) distance on a real aerial bank as a cheap no-reference realism signal; implement it as a 5-line torch addition on frozen VGG/DINO feature maps if a style term is wanted.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.19913 (Alina Marcu, 2024-11-29, arXiv preprint)

Via search summaries.

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.16468 (Safaei, Khastgir, Alirezaei, Ploeg, Tong, Zhao (v3 adds Cheng), 2025-12-18; v3 2026-01-28, IEEE IV 2026 (per v3 listing))

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2105.04619 (Richter, Abu AlHaija, Koltun (Intel ISL), 2021-05-10, arXiv preprint (journal venue not verified))
- **Claim:** Older work (2021): a network enhances rendered frames using G-buffer inputs and a multi-level perceptual adversarial objective with a patch-sampling strategy to reduce scene-layout artefacts; code isl-org/PhotorealismEnhancement. Via search summary.
- **Numbers:** none
- **For the fleet:** The idea reusable here is a realism DISCRIMINATOR on frozen features of real vs rendered patches whose logit map localises the most fake-looking regions to steer edits; do not use generative enhancement for the deliverable, as it changes scene content.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly

The G-buffer, perceptual-discriminator and patch-sampling description is not on the README. The repo has no licence on its page and has been archived and read-only since 2 Sep 2024 (Intel discontinued it).

### LOKI: Synthetic Data Detection Benchmark using Large Multimodal Models

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.09732 (Ye et al. (Sun Yat-sen Univ., Shanghai AI Lab), arXiv Oct 2024, revised Apr 2025, ICLR 2025 (Spotlight))
- **Claim:** 18K questions over 26 subcategories (video, image, 3D, text, audio) with judgement, multiple-choice and fine-grained anomaly selection/explanation tasks; 22 open-source and 6 closed-source LMMs tested; LMMs show promise as synthetic-data detectors but with clear limitations; defines a Normalized Bias Index. Figures I could not reconcile: one search summary says GPT-4o is best closed model at 63.9% overall judgement accuracy (excluding audio) versus humans about 76%, and 73.7% vs 86.4% on multiple choice; another survey cites 80.8% vs 84.5%; an image-column value of 80.1 for humans was cited. Repo README lists no headline numbers or licence.
- **Numbers:** 18K questions; 26 subcategories; 22 open + 6 closed models; GPT-4o 63.9% vs humans about 76% (judgement, secondary and unreconciled)
- **For the fleet:** Even strong multimodal models are clearly below human performance at telling synthetic from real in general content, and show category biases; do not let an LLM be the sole realism arbiter. Use it for localised artefact hypotheses and pairwise ranking only.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** The repo is opendatalab/LOKI, with the title 'LOKI: A Comprehensive Synthetic Data Detection Benchmark using Large Multimodal Models'. It is tagged [ICLR 2025 Spotlight] and lists 26 categories. Not on the README: 18K questions, '22 open + 6 closed' models (the README says 'over 25' and '20+' models), and any GPT-4o or human accuracies. The 63.9% vs 76% and 80.8% vs 84.5% figures stay unreconciled and unconfirmed. The README states no licence.

### VLM Judges Can Rank but Cannot Score

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25235 (Kumar, Tayebati, Naik, Krishnan, Trivedi (UIC, Capital One AI Labs), Apr 2026, arXiv preprint, under review)
- **Claim:** Conformal prediction on score-token log-probabilities over 3 VLM judges and 14 task categories: prediction intervals cover about 40% of the score range for aesthetics and natural images but about 70% for chart and math reasoning; 'ranking-scoring decoupling': judges can have high rank correlation yet wide, uninformative intervals; width tracks task difficulty and annotation quality. Code github.com/divake/VLM-Judge-Uncertainty. Abstract-level only.
- **Numbers:** 3 judges; 14 categories; interval width about 40% of range (aesthetics/natural images) vs about 70% (charts/math)
- **For the fleet:** Use VLM judges for pairwise ranking (and take the log-prob margin as a confidence), never for absolute 1-10 realism scores; require agreement across order swaps and repeated samples.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: partly
- **Correction:** The repo divake/VLM-Judge-Uncertainty (MIT) confirms the title, arXiv 2604.25235, 3 judges (LLaVA-Critic-7B, Phi-4, Gemini 2.5 Flash), 14 task categories, the 'rank but cannot score' decoupling, narrow intervals on aesthetics and wide on charts, math and infographics, and at least 90% coverage. The README says 'interval width varies up to 70% across 14 visual task categories'. It does not contain the report's '~40% of the score range for aesthetics/natural images vs ~70% for charts/math', so that reading of the numbers is unconfirmed. Authors, affiliations (UIC, Capital One) and the 'Apr 2026' month were not opened.

### Fooling the LVLM Judges: Visual Biases in LVLM-Based Evaluation (FRAME)

- **Source:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15249 (unconfirmed, May 2025, arXiv preprint)
- **Claim:** All tested LVLM judges are vulnerable across all domains, consistently inflating scores for visually manipulated images (brightness and gamma changes, beauty filters), and pairwise evaluations are similarly susceptible. Via search summary.
- **Numbers:** none
- **For the fleet:** A judge can reward polish (contrast, saturation, sharpening) rather than realism, which is exactly what an agent tuning post-processing could exploit; keep brightness/contrast/saturation changes out of the judged pairs by histogram-matching the pair before judging, and add a real anchor image to every pool.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** arXiv 2505.15249 was not opened; the title, authors and brightness/gamma/beauty-filter inflation claims are unconfirmed.

### VLM informativeness bias (ACL 2026)

- **Source:** https://aclanthology.org/2026.acl-long.703/ (unconfirmed, 2026, ACL 2026 (long paper 703))
- **Claim:** VLM judges often pay limited attention to the image when deciding and prefer more informative-sounding candidate answers; BIRCH (correct candidate answers against the image before comparing) reduces this bias by up to 17%. Via search summary.
- **Numbers:** up to 17% bias reduction
- **For the fleet:** Ask the judge to state image-grounded, checkable observations (e.g. shadow direction vs sun azimuth, relative sizes) before giving a verdict, and verify them with metrics where possible.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** false
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable
- **Correction:** aclanthology.org did not resolve; the paper, BIRCH and the 17% figure are unconfirmed.

- **Claim:** Realism of fog/rain/snow/night edits on 40 clear-day car-camera images is assessed with a VLM jury (binary semantic-preservation and condition-realism criteria, reasoning + yes/no) and embedding-based distribution analysis; best generative method gets about 3.6x the acceptance rate of the best rule-based method; fog is easiest, night hardest. The jury is validated only against real adverse-condition images (it does not accept all of them) and the authors state human-study validation is future work.

- **For the fleet:** Closest published precedent to the fleet's design: VLM jury plus embedding distribution. Copy its idea of a real-image acceptance ceiling: run the same judge prompt on real reference photos and treat that acceptance rate as the target, not 100%.

- **Fact-checker:** source exists: not checked; claim supported: unverifiable

Other pages: captured at least once every three years per state.

- **For the fleet:** Free, no-account-required read of cloud-optimized NAIP, though a key gives more permissive access (per the example notebook). NAIP gives 0.6 m nadir RGB(N) of the exact area, good for ground colour, field patterns and canopy cover, but not for fine texture: at 40 m with the default 87-degree, 2048-px LUCID camera the nadir ground sample is about 3.7 cm/px (our arithmetic), about 16x finer than 0.6 m.

The notebook says it 'works with or without an API key', with more permissive access with a key, and 'Data are captured at least once every three years for each state'. The arithmetic checks out against this repo's scripts/drone_scene/README.md (default TRI032S-CC, 2048x1536, 87x69 deg): 2*40*tan(43.5 deg)/2048 = 3.7 cm/px, 0.6 m GSD needs ~648 m, ratio ~16x.

- **For the fleet:** For a small area on the owner PC: search with bbox about [-93.72, 42.00, -93.64, 42.06] (west Ames; our choice), sort items by datetime, pick the newest, open item.assets['image'].href with rasterio, transform the bbox into the item's CRS (UTM zone 15N expected, unconfirmed), build a window with rasterio.windows.from_bounds, and read only bands 1-3 (and 4 for NIR); only that window's COG blocks are fetched. Save with manifest (item id, datetime, gsd, licence text) under ~/.cache/drone_scene/reference/. Pip packages: pystac-client, planetary-computer, rasterio (licences not checked this session).

- **Numbers:** 21M / 86M / 300M / 1.1B parameters
- **For the fleet:** A permissively licensed feature backbone for patch-embedding MMD/KID and nearest-neighbour realism scores; ViT-B/14 fits easily beside other jobs once Isaac Sim has exited.
- **Researcher says primary source confirmed:** true
- **Fact-checker:** source exists: true; claim supported: yes
- **Correction:** README: DINOv2 code and weights Apache License 2.0; ViT-S/14 21M, B/14 86M, L/14 300M, g/14 1,100M; hub names dinov2_vits14 / vitb14 / vitl14 / vitg14 plus _reg variants; Cell-DINO and XRay-DINO carry non-commercial licences.

### Design rules proposed by the researcher

- GPU scheduling: run the metric stack as a separate process (own venv or pip --target directory, mirroring how the repo already isolates the map-builder dependencies) only after the Isaac Sim render process has exited; never alongside it. Order per round: render probes -> Tier A CPU statistics -> Tier B torch embeddings -> optional VLM-based IQA models -> Tier C judge calls (API, no local GPU).
